arXiv:1901.02324v2 [stat.ML] 02 Mar 2020
Mathieu Blondel, André F.T. Martins, Vlad Niculae
Learning with Fenchel-Young Losses
Mathieu Blondel mathieu@mblondel.org
Affiliation: NTT Communication Science Laboratories
Affiliation: Kyoto, Japan
André F. T. Martins andre.martins@unbabel.com
Affiliation: Unbabel & Instituto de Telecomunicações
Affiliation: Lisbon, Portugal
Vlad Niculae vlad@vene.ro
Affiliation: Instituto de Telecomunicações
Affiliation: Lisbon, Portugal
Abstract
Over the past decades, numerous loss functions have been been proposed for a
variety of supervised learning tasks, including regression, classification,
ranking, and more generally structured prediction. Understanding the core
principles and theoretical properties underpinning these losses is key to choose the
right loss for the right problem, as well as to create new losses which combine their
strengths. In this paper, we introduce Fenchel-Young losses, a generic way to
construct a convex loss function for a regularized prediction function.
We provide an in-depth study of
their properties in a very broad setting, covering all the aforementioned
supervised learning tasks, and revealing new connections between sparsity,
generalized entropies, and separation margins.
We show that Fenchel-Young losses unify many
well-known loss functions and allow to create useful new ones easily. Finally,
we derive efficient predictive and training algorithms, making Fenchel-Young
losses appealing both in theory and practice.
††shortheadings: Learning with Fenchel-Young Losses / Blondel, Martins, and Niculae††firstpage: 1††heading: 21 2020 1- 1/19; Revised
10/19 1/20 19-021††editor: Sathiya Keerthi
keywords
loss functions, output regularization, convex duality, structured prediction
1 Introduction
Loss functions are a cornerstone of statistics and machine learning: They
measure the difference, or “loss,” between a ground truth and a prediction.
As such, much work has been devoted to designing loss functions for a variety
of supervised learning tasks, including regression (Huber, 1964),
classification (Crammer and Singer, 2001), ranking (Joachims, 2002) and
structured prediction
(Lafferty et al., 2001; Collins, 2002; Tsochantaridis et al., 2005), to name only a few
well-known directions.
For the case of probabilistic classification, proper composite loss functions
(Reid and Williamson, 2010; Williamson et al., 2016) offer a principled framework unifying
various existing loss functions. A proper composite loss is the composition of
a proper loss between two probability distributions, with an invertible mapping
from real vectors to probability distributions. The theoretical properties of
proper loss functions, also known as proper scoring rules
(Grünwald and Dawid (2004); Gneiting and Raftery (2007); and references therein), such as
their Fisher consistency (classification calibration) and correspondence with
Bregman divergences, are now well-understood. However, not all existing losses
are proper composite loss functions; a notable example is the hinge loss used in
support vector machines. In fact, we shall see that any loss function enjoying a
separation margin, a prevalent concept in statistical learning theory which has
been used to prove the famous perceptron mistake bound (Rosenblatt, 1958) and
many other generalization bounds (Vapnik, 1998; Schölkopf and Smola, 2002), cannot be
written in composite proper loss form.
At the same time, loss functions are often intimately related to an underlying
statistical model and prediction function. For instance, the logistic loss
corresponds to the multinomial distribution and the softmax operator, while the
conditional random field (CRF) loss (Lafferty et al., 2001) for structured
prediction is tied with marginal inference (Wainwright and Jordan, 2008).
Both are instances of generalized linear models (Nelder and Baker, 1972; McCullagh and Nelder, 1989),
associated with exponential family distributions. More recently,
Martins and Astudillo (2016) proposed a new classification loss based on the projection
onto the simplex. Unlike the logistic loss, this “sparsemax” loss induces
probability distributions with sparse support, which is desirable in
some applications for interpretability or computational efficiency reasons.
However, the sparsemax loss was derived in a relatively ad-hoc manner and it is
still relatively poorly understood. Is it one of a kind or can we generalize it
in a principled manner? Thorough understanding of the core
principles underpinning existing losses and their associated predictive model,
potentially enabling the creation of useful new losses, is one of the main
quests of this paper.
This paper.
The starting point of this paper are the notions of output
regularization and regularized prediction functions, which we use to
provide a variational perspective on many existing prediction
functions, including the aforementioned softmax, sparsemax
and marginal inference. Based on simple convex duality
arguments, we then introduce Fenchel-Young losses, a new way to automatically
construct a loss function associated with any regularized prediction function.
As we shall see, our proposal recovers many existing loss functions, which is in
a sense surprising since many of these losses were originally proposed by
independent efforts. Our framework goes beyond the simple probabilistic
classification setting: We show how to create loss functions over various
structured domains, including convex polytopes and convex cones. Our framework encourages
the loss designer to think geometrically about the outputs desired for
the task at hand. Once a (regularized) prediction function has been designed,
our framework generates a corresponding loss function automatically.
We will demonstrate the ease of creating loss functions, including useful new
ones, using abundant examples throughout this paper.
Previous papers.
This paper builds upon two previously published shorter conference papers. The
first (Niculae et al., 2018) introduced Fenchel-Young losses in the structured
prediction setting but only provided a limited analysis of their properties.
The second (Blondel et al., 2019) provided a more in-depth analysis but focused on
unstructured probabilistic classification. This paper provides a comprehensive
study of Fenchel-Young losses across various domains. Besides a much more
thorough treatment of previously covered topics, this paper contributes entirely
new sections, including §6 on losses for positive
measures, §8 on primal and dual training
algorithms, and §A.2 on loss
“Fenchel-Youngization”. We provide in §7 a new
unifying view between structured predictions losses,
and discuss at length various convex polytopes, promoting a
geometric approach to structured prediction loss design; we also
provide novel results in this section regarding structured separation margins
(Proposition 13), proving the unit margin of the
SparseMAP loss.
We demonstrate how to use our framework to create useful new losses,
including ranking losses, not covered in the previous two papers.
Notation.
We denote the (d−1)(d-1)-dimensional probability simplex by △d≔{𝒑∈ℝ+d:‖𝒑‖1=1}\triangle^{d}\coloneqq\{{\bm{p}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}\colon\|{\bm{p}}\|_{1}=1\}.
We denote the convex hull of a set 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}} by 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}) and the conic hull by
𝖼𝗈𝗇𝖾(𝒴)\cone({\mathcal{Y}}).
We denote the domain of a function Ω:ℝd→ℝ∪{∞}\Omega\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\} by 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)≔{𝝁∈ℝd:Ω⁡(𝝁)<∞}\dom(\Omega)\coloneqq\{\bm{\mu}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\colon\Omega(\bm{\mu})<\infty\}.
We denote the Fenchel conjugate of Ω\Omega by
Ω∗​(𝜽)≔sup𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩−Ω⁡(𝝁)\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega)}{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}-\Omega(\bm{\mu}).
We denote the indicator function of a set 𝒞{\mathcal{C}} by
I𝒞​(𝝁)≔{0 if ​𝝁∈𝒞∞ otherwise I_{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{\mu})\coloneqq\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }\bm{\mu}\in{\mathcal{C}}\\
\infty&\mbox{ otherwise }\end{cases}
(1)
and its support function by σ𝒞​(𝜽)≔sup𝝁∈𝒞⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in{\mathcal{C}}}{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}.
We define the proximity operator (a.k.a. proximal operator) of Ω\Omega by
𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑Ω​(𝜼)≔𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)12​‖𝝁−𝜼‖2+Ω⁡(𝝁).\mathsf{prox}_{\Omega}(\bm{\eta})\coloneqq\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega)}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\eta}\|^{2}+\Omega(\bm{\mu}).
(2)
We denote the interior and relative interior of 𝒞{\mathcal{C}} by 𝗂𝗇𝗍(𝒞)\interior({\mathcal{C}}) and
𝗋𝖾𝗅𝗂𝗇𝗍(𝒞)\relint({\mathcal{C}}), respectively.
We denote [𝒙]+≔max⁡(𝒙,𝟎)[{\bm{x}}]_{+}\coloneqq\max({\bm{x}},\bm{0}), evaluated element-wise.

2 Regularized prediction functions
In this section, we introduce the concept of regularized prediction function
(§2.1), which is central to this paper. We then give
simple and well-known examples of such functions (§2.2)
and discuss their properties in a general setting (§2.3,§2.4).
2.1 Definition
We consider a general predictive setting with input variables 𝒙∈𝒳{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}, and
a parametrized model 𝒇W:𝒳→ℝd\bm{f}_{W}:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{d} (which could be a linear
model or a neural network), producing a score
vector 𝜽≔𝒇W​(𝒙)∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\coloneqq\bm{f}_{W}({\bm{x}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}. In a simple multi-class
classification setting, the score vector is typically used to pick the
highest-scoring class among dd possible ones
y^​(𝜽)∈𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑j∈[d]θj.\widehat{y}(\bm{\theta})\in\argmax_{j\in[d]}\theta_{j}.
(3)
This can be generalized to an arbitrary output space 𝒴⊆ℝd{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
by using instead
𝒚^​(𝜽)∈𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐲∈𝒴⟨𝜽,𝐲⟩,\widehat{{\bm{y}}}(\bm{\theta})\in\argmax_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}{{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}},
(4)
where intuitively ⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle} captures the affinity between 𝒙{\bm{x}} (since 𝜽\bm{\theta}
is produced by fW​(𝒙)f_{W}({\bm{x}})) and 𝒚{\bm{y}}.
Therefore, (4) seeks the output 𝒚{\bm{y}} with greatest
affinity with 𝒙{\bm{x}}.
The support function σ𝒴​(𝜽)=max𝒚∈𝒴⁡⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩=⟨𝜽,𝒚^​(𝜽)⟩\sigma_{\mathcal{Y}}(\bm{\theta})=\max_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}{{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}}={\langle\bm{\theta},\widehat{{\bm{y}}}(\bm{\theta})\rangle} can be
interpreted as the largest projection of any element of 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}} onto the line
generated by 𝜽\bm{\theta}.
Clearly, (4) recovers (3) with
𝒴={𝒆1,…,𝒆d}{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\bm{e}_{1},\dots,\bm{e}_{d}\}, where 𝒆j\bm{e}_{j} is a standard basis vector,
𝒆j≔[0,…​0,1⏟j,0,…​0]\bm{e}_{j}\coloneqq[0,\dots 0,\underbrace{1}_{j},0,\dots 0].
In this case, the cardinality |𝒴|{|{\mathcal{Y}}|} and the dimensionality
dd coincide, but this need not be the case in general.
Eq. (4) is often called a linear maximization oracle or
maximum a-posteriori (MAP) oracle (Wainwright and Jordan, 2008). The latter name comes
from the fact that (4) coincides with the mode of the Gibbs
distribution defined by
p⁡(𝒚,𝜽)∝exp⁡⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩.p({\bm{y}};\bm{\theta})\propto\exp{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}.
(5)
Prediction over convex hulls.
We now extend the prediction function (4) by replacing 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}}
with its convex hull 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)≔{𝔼𝐩​[Y]:𝐩∈△|𝒴|}\conv({\mathcal{Y}})\coloneqq\{{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]\colon{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}\} and introducing a regularization function Ω\Omega into the
optimization problem:
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)∈𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩−Ω⁡(𝝁).\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\in\argmax_{\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}})}{{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}}-\Omega(\bm{\mu}).
(6)
We emphasize that the regularization is w.r.t. predictions (outputs) and not w.r.t. model parameters (denoted by WW in this paper), as is usually the case in the
literature. We illustrate the regularized prediction function pipeline in Figure
1.
Unsurprisingly, the choice Ω=0\Omega=0 recovers the unregularized prediction
function (4).
This follows from the fundamental
theorem of linear programming
(Dantzig et al., 1955, Theorem 6), which states
that the maximum of a linear form over a
convex polytope is always achieved at one
of its vertices:
𝒚^0​(𝜽)∈𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐲∈𝒴⟨𝜽,𝐲⟩.\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{0}(\bm{\theta})\in\argmax_{\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}})}{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}=\argmax_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}.
(7)
Why regularize outputs?
The regularized prediction function (8) casts computing a
prediction as a
variational problem. It involves an
optimization problem that balances between two terms: an “affinity” term
⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}, and a “confidence” term Ω⁡(𝝁)\Omega(\bm{\mu}) which should be low if
𝝁\bm{\mu} is “uncertain.” Two important classes of convex Ω\Omega are (squared)
norms and, when 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega) is the probability simplex, generalized negative
entropies. However, our framework does not require Ω\Omega to be
convex in general.
Introducing Ω⁡(𝝁)\Omega(\bm{\mu}) in (8) tends to move
the prediction away from the vertices of 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}). Unless the
regularization term Ω⁡(𝝁)\Omega(\bm{\mu}) is negligible compared to the affinity term
⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}, a prediction becomes a convex combination of several vertices. As
we shall see in §7, which is dedicated to
structured prediction over convex hulls, we can interpret
this prediction as the mean under some underlying distribution. This
contrasts with (4), which always outputs the most
likely vertex, i.e., the mode.
Figure 1: Illustration of the proposed regularized prediction framework over
a convex hull 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}). A parametrized model
𝒇W:𝒳→ℝd\bm{f}_{W}\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d} (linear model, neural network, etc.)
produces a score vector 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.
The regularized prediction function 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega} produces a prediction
𝒚^∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}}). Regularized
prediction functions are not limited to convex hulls and can be defined over
arbitrary domains (Def. 1).
Prediction over arbitrary domains.
Regularized prediction functions are in fact not limited to convex hulls. We now
state their precise definition in complete generality.
Definition 1.
Prediction function regularized by Ω\Omega
Let Ω:ℝd→ℝ∪{∞}\Omega\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\} be a regularization function,
with 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)⊆ℝd\dom(\Omega)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}. The prediction function regularized by
Ω\Omega is defined by
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)∈𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩−Ω⁡(𝝁).\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\in\argmax_{\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega)}{{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}}-\Omega(\bm{\mu}).
(8)
Allowing extended-real Ω\Omega permits
general
domain constraints in (8) via indicator functions.
For instance, choosing Ω=I𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\Omega=I_{\conv({\mathcal{Y}})},
where I𝒞I_{\mathcal{C}} is the indicator function defined in
(1),
recovers the MAP oracle (4).
Importantly, the choice of domain 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega) is not limited to convex hulls.
For instance, we will also consider conic hulls, 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝖾(𝒴)\cone({\mathcal{Y}}), later in this
paper.
Choosing 𝛀\mathbf{\Omega}.
Regularized prediction functions 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega} involve two main design choices:
the domain 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega) over which Ω\Omega is defined and Ω\Omega itself.
The choice of 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega) is mainly dictated by the type of output
we want from 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}, such as
𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)=𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\dom(\Omega)=\conv({\mathcal{Y}}) for convex combinations of elements of 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}}, and
𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)=𝖼𝗈𝗇𝖾(𝒴)\dom(\Omega)=\cone({\mathcal{Y}}) for conic combinations. The choice of the
regularization Ω\Omega itself further governs certain properties of
𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}, including, as we shall see in the sequel, its sparsity or its use
of prior knowledge regarding the importance or misclassification cost of certain
outputs.
The choices of 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega) and Ω\Omega may also be constrained by
computational considerations. Indeed, while computing 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) involves
a potentially challenging constrained maximization problem in general, we will
see that certain choices of Ω\Omega lead to closed-form expressions.
The power of our framework is that the user can focus solely on designing and
computing 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}: We will see in §3 how to
automatically construct a loss function associated with 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}.
2.2 Examples
To illustrate regularized prediction functions, we give several concrete
examples enjoying a closed-form expression.
When Ω=I△d\Omega=I_{\triangle^{d}},
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) is a one-hot representation of the argmax
prediction
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)∈𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐩∈△d⟨𝜽,𝐩⟩=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐲∈{𝐞1,…,𝐞d}⟨𝜽,𝐲⟩.\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\in\argmax_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}~\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{p}}\rangle=\argmax_{{\bm{y}}\in\{\bm{e}_{1},\dots,\bm{e}_{d}\}}\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle.
(9)
We can see that output as a probability distribution that assigns all probability
mass on the same class.
When Ω=−𝖧s+I△d\Omega=-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}+I_{\triangle^{d}}, where
𝖧s(𝒑)≔−∑ipilogpi\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}({\bm{p}})\coloneqq-\sum_{i}p_{i}\log p_{i} is Shannon’s entropy,
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) is the well-known softmax
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝗌𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑(𝜽)≔exp⁡(𝜽)∑j=1dexp⁡(θj).\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\softmax(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\frac{\exp(\bm{\theta})}{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\exp(\theta_{j})}.
(10)
See Boyd and Vandenberghe (2004, Ex. 3.25) for a derivation. The resulting distribution
always has dense support.
When Ω=12∥⋅∥2+I△d\Omega=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|^{2}+I_{\triangle^{d}},
𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega} is the
Euclidean projection onto the probability simplex
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝗌𝗉𝖺𝗋𝗌𝖾𝗆𝖺𝗑(𝜽)≔𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝐩∈△d‖𝐩−𝜽‖2,\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\sparsemax(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\argmin_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}\|{\bm{p}}-\bm{\theta}\|^{2},
(11)
a.k.a. the sparsemax transformation (Martins and Astudillo, 2016).
It is well-known that
𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝐩∈△d‖𝐩−𝜽‖2=[𝜽−τ​𝟏]+,\argmin_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}\|{\bm{p}}-\bm{\theta}\|^{2}=[\bm{\theta}-\tau\bm{1}]_{+},
(12)
for some threshold τ∈ℝ\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}.
Hence, the predicted distribution can have sparse support (it may
assign exactly zero probability to low-scoring classes). The threshold τ\tau
can be computed exactly in O⁡(d)O(d) time
(Brucker, 1984; Duchi et al., 2008; Condat, 2016).
The regularized prediction function paradigm is, however, not limited to the
probability simplex: When Ω(𝒑)=−∑i𝖧s([pi,1−pi])+I[0,1]d(𝒑)\Omega({\bm{p}})=-\sum_{i}\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}([p_{i},1-p_{i}])+I_{[0,1]^{d}}({\bm{p}}), we get
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝗌𝗂𝗀𝗆𝗈𝗂𝖽(𝜽)≔𝟏𝟏+exp⁡(−𝜽),\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\sigmoid(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\frac{\bm{1}}{\bm{1}+\exp(-\bm{\theta})},
(13)
i.e., the sigmoid function evaluated coordinate-wise. We can think of its
output as a positive measure (unnormalized probability distribution).
We will see in §4 that the first three examples (argmax,
softmax and sparsemax) are particular instances of a broader family of
prediction functions, using the notion of generalized entropy. The last
example is a special case of regularized prediction function over positive
measures, developed in §6. Regularized prediction
functions also encompass more complex convex polytopes for structured
prediction, as we shall see in §7.
Figure 2: Examples of regularized prediction functions. The unregularized
𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑\argmax prediction always hits a vertex of the probability simplex,
leading to a probability distribution that puts all probability mass on the same
class. Unlike, 𝗌𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑\softmax which
always occurs in the relative interior of the simplex and thus leads to a dense
distribution, 𝗌𝗉𝖺𝗋𝗌𝖾𝗆𝖺𝗑\sparsemax (Euclidean
projection onto the probability simplex) may hit the boundary, leading
to a sparse probability distribution. We also display the 𝗌𝗂𝗀𝗆𝗈𝗂𝖽\sigmoid
operator which lies in the unit cube and is thus
not guaranteed to output a valid probability distribution.
2.3 Gradient mapping and dual objective
From Danskin’s theorem
(Danskin, 1966) (see also Bertsekas (1999, Proposition B.25))
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) is a subgradient of Ω∗\Omega^{*} at 𝜽\bm{\theta}, i.e.,
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)∈∂Ω∗​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\in\partial\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}).
If, furthermore, Ω\Omega is strictly convex,
then 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) is the gradient of Ω∗\Omega^{*} at 𝜽\bm{\theta}, i.e.,
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=∇Ω∗​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}).
This interpretation of 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) as a (sub)gradient mapping
will play a crucial role in the next section for deriving a
loss function associated with 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}).
Viewing 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) as the (sub)gradient of Ω∗​(𝜽)\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}) is also
useful to derive the dual of (8).
Let Ω≔Ψ+Φ\Omega\coloneqq\Psi+\Phi. It is well-known
(Borwein and Lewis, 2010; Beck and Teboulle, 2012) that
Ω∗​(𝜽)=(Ψ+Φ)∗​(𝜽)=inf𝒖∈ℝdΦ∗​(𝒖)+Ψ∗​(𝜽−𝒖)≕(Φ∗​□​Ψ∗)​(𝜽),\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})=(\Psi+\Phi)^{*}(\bm{\theta})=\inf_{\bm{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Phi^{*}(\bm{u})+\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}-\bm{u})\eqqcolon(\Phi^{*}\square\Psi^{*})(\bm{\theta}),
(14)
where f​□​gf\square g denotes the infimal convolution of ff with gg.
Furthermore, from Danskin’s theorem, 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=∇Ψ∗​(𝜽−𝒖⋆)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}-\bm{u}^{\star}), where 𝒖⋆\bm{u}^{\star} denotes an optimal
solution of the infimum in (14).
We can think of that infimum as the dual
of the optimization problem in (8).
When Ψ=Ψ∗=12∥⋅∥2\Psi=\Psi^{*}=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|^{2},
Ω∗​(𝜽)\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}) is known as the Moreau envelope of Φ∗\Phi^{*}
(Moreau, 1965) and
using Moreau’s decomposition, we obtain
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝜽−𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑Φ∗​(𝜽)=𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑Φ​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{\theta}-\mathsf{prox}_{\Phi^{*}}(\bm{\theta})=\mathsf{prox}_{\Phi}(\bm{\theta}).
As another example, when Ω=Ψ+I𝒞\Omega=\Psi+I_{\mathcal{C}}, we obtain
Ω∗​(𝜽)=inf𝒖∈ℝdσ𝒞​(𝒖)+Ψ∗​(𝜽−𝒖),\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})=\inf_{\bm{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{u})+\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}-\bm{u}),
(15)
where we used I𝒞∗=σ𝒞I_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}=\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}, the support function of 𝒞{\mathcal{C}}.
In particular, when 𝒞=𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴){\mathcal{C}}=\conv({\mathcal{Y}}), we have σ𝒞​(𝒖)=max𝒚∈𝒴⁡⟨𝒖,𝒚⟩\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{u})=\max_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}{\langle\bm{u},{\bm{y}}\rangle}. This dual view is informative insofar as it suggests
that regularized prediction functions 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) with Ω=Ψ+I𝒞\Omega=\Psi+I_{\mathcal{C}} minimize a trade-off between maximizing the value achieved
by the unregularized prediction function σ𝒞​(𝒖)\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{u}), and a proximity
term Ψ∗​(𝜽−𝒖)\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}-\bm{u}).
2.4 Properties
We now discuss simple yet useful properties of regularized prediction functions.
The first two assume that Ω\Omega is a symmetric function, i.e.,
that it satisfies
Ω⁡(𝝁)=Ω⁡(𝑷​𝝁)∀𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω),∀𝐏∈𝒫,\Omega(\bm{\mu})=\Omega(\bm{P}\bm{\mu})\quad\forall\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega),\forall\bm{P}\in\mathcal{P},
(16)
where 𝒫\mathcal{P} is the set of d×dd\times d permutation matrices.
Proposition 2.
Properties of regularized prediction functions
𝐲^Ω​(𝛉)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})
1.
Effect of a permutation. If Ω\Omega is symmetric, then
∀𝑷∈𝒫\forall\bm{P}\in\mathcal{P}:
𝒚^Ω​(𝑷​𝜽)=𝑷​𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{P}\bm{\theta})=\bm{P}\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}).
2.
Order preservation.
Let 𝝁=𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\bm{\mu}=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}). If Ω\Omega is symmetric, then the coordinates of
𝝁\bm{\mu} and 𝜽\bm{\theta} are sorted the same way, i.e., θi>θj⇒μi≥μj\theta_{i}>\theta_{j}\Rightarrow\mu_{i}\geq\mu_{j} and μi>μj⇒θi>θj\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}\Rightarrow\theta_{i}>\theta_{j}.
3.
Approximation error.
Assume 𝒴⊆𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\dom(\Omega).
If Ω\Omega is γ\gamma-strongly convex
and bounded with
L≤Ω⁡(𝝁)≤UL\leq\Omega(\bm{\mu})\leq U for all 𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega),
then
12​‖𝒚^​(𝜽)−𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)‖2≤U−Lγ\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}(\bm{\theta})-\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\|^{2}\leq\frac{U-L}{\gamma}.
4.
Temperature scaling. For any constant t>0t>0,
𝒚^t​Ω​(𝜽)∈∂Ω∗​(𝜽/t)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{t\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\in\partial\Omega^{*}(\nicefrac{{\bm{\theta}}}{{t}}).
If Ω\Omega is strictly convex,
𝒚^t​Ω​(𝜽)=𝒚^Ω​(𝜽/t)=∇Ω∗​(𝜽/t)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{t\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\nicefrac{{\bm{\theta}}}{{t}})=\nabla\Omega^{*}(\nicefrac{{\bm{\theta}}}{{t}}).
5.
Constant invariance. For any constant c∈ℝc\in{\mathbb{R}},
𝒚^Ω+c​(𝜽)=𝒚^Ω​(𝜽,𝒚)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega+c}(\bm{\theta})=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}).
The proof is given in Appendix
B.1.
For classification, the order-preservation property ensures that the
highest-scoring class according to 𝜽\bm{\theta} and 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) agree
with each other:
𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑i∈[d]θi=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑i∈[d](𝐲^Ω​(𝜽))i.\argmax_{i\in[d]}\theta_{i}=\argmax_{i\in[d]}\left(\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\right)_{i}.
(17)
Temperature scaling is useful to control how close we are to unregularized
prediction functions. Clearly, 𝒚^t​Ω​(𝜽)→𝒚^​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{t\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\to\widehat{{\bm{y}}}(\bm{\theta})
as t→0t\to 0, where 𝒚^​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}(\bm{\theta}) is defined in (4).
3 Fenchel-Young losses
In the previous section, we introduced regularized prediction functions
over arbitrary domains, as
a generalization of classical (unregularized) decision functions.
In this section, we introduce Fenchel-Young losses for learning
models whose output layer is a regularized prediction function.
We first give their definitions and state their properties
(§3.1). We then discuss
their relationship with Bregman divergences (§3.2) and
their Bayes risk (§3.3). Finally, we
show how to construct a cost-sensitive loss from any Fenchel-Young loss
(§3.4).
3.1 Definition and properties
Given a regularized prediction function 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}, we define its
associated loss as follows.
Definition 3.
Fenchel-Young loss generated by Ω\Omega
Let Ω:ℝd→ℝ∪{∞}\Omega\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\} be a regularization
function such that the maximum in
(8) is achieved for all 𝛉∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.
Let 𝐲∈𝒴⊆𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω){\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq\dom(\Omega) be a ground-truth label and 𝛉∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω∗)=ℝd\bm{\theta}\in\dom(\Omega^{*})={\mathbb{R}}^{d} be a vector of prediction scores.
The Fenchel-Young loss LΩ:𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω∗)×𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)→ℝ+L_{\Omega}\colon\dom(\Omega^{*})\times\dom(\Omega)\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+} generated by Ω\Omega is
LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)≔Ω∗​(𝜽)+Ω⁡(𝒚)−⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩.L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})\coloneqq\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})+\Omega({\bm{y}})-{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}.
(18)
It is easy to see that Fenchel-Young losses can be rewritten as
LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=f𝜽​(𝒚)−f𝜽​(𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)),L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=f_{\bm{\theta}}({\bm{y}})-f_{\bm{\theta}}(\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})),
(19)
where f𝜽​(𝝁)≔Ω⁡(𝝁)−⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩f_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\mu})\coloneqq\Omega(\bm{\mu})-{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle},
highlighting the relation with regularized prediction functions.
Therefore, as long as we can compute a regularized prediction function
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}), we can automatically obtain an associated Fenchel-Young loss
LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}).
Conversely, we also have that 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega} outputs the prediction minimizing the
loss:
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)∈𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)LΩ​(𝜽,𝝁).\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\in\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega)}L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};\bm{\mu}).
(20)
Examples of existing losses that fall into the Fenchel-Young loss family are
given in Table 1. Some of these examples will be
discussed in more details in the sequel of this paper.
Note that we will allow Ω\Omega in some cases to depend on the ground-truth
𝒚{\bm{y}}. Since we do not know 𝒚{\bm{y}} at test time, this requires us to use another
prediction function as a replacement for the regularized prediction function.
As we explain in §3.4, this discrepancy is well motivated
and allows us to express popular cost-sensitive losses, such as the structured
hinge loss.

Fenchel-Young losses. For multi-class classification, we assume
𝒴={𝒆i}i=1d{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d} and the ground-truth is 𝒚=𝒆k{\bm{y}}=\bm{e}_{k}, where 𝒆i\bm{e}_{i}
denotes a standard basis (“one-hot”) vector. For structured
classification, we assume that elements of 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}} are dd-dimensional binary
vectors with d≪|𝒴|d\ll{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}, and we denote by 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)={𝔼𝐩​[Y]:𝐩∈△|𝒴|}\conv({\mathcal{Y}})=\{{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]\colon{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}\} the corresponding marginal polytope
(Wainwright and Jordan, 2008). We denote by 𝖧s(𝒑)≔−∑ipilogpi\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}({\bm{p}})\coloneqq-\sum_{i}p_{i}\log p_{i} the Shannon entropy of a distribution 𝒑∈△|𝒴|{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}.
