log∑𝒚′exp⟨𝜽,𝒚′⟩−⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩\log\sum_{{\bm{y}}^{\prime}}\exp{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}-{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}
SparseMAP
𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}})
12​‖𝝁‖2\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mu}\|^{2}
𝖲𝗉𝖺𝗋𝗌𝖾𝖬𝖠𝖯(𝜽)\sparsemap(\bm{\theta})
12​‖𝒚−𝜽‖2−12​‖𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)−𝜽‖2\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-\bm{\theta}\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{\theta}\|^{2}
Properties.
As the name indicates, this family of loss functions is grounded in the
Fenchel-Young inequality
(Borwein and Lewis, 2010, Proposition 3.3.4)
Ω∗​(𝜽)+Ω⁡(𝝁)≥⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩∀𝜽∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω∗),𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω).\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})+\Omega(\bm{\mu})\geq{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}\quad\forall\bm{\theta}\in\dom(\Omega^{*}),\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega).
(21)
The inequality, together with well-known results regarding convex conjugates,
imply the following properties of Fenchel-Young losses.
Proposition 4.
Properties of Fenchel-Young losses
1.
Non-negativity. LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)≥0L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})\geq 0 for any 𝜽∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω∗)=ℝd\bm{\theta}\in\dom(\Omega^{*})={\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and 𝒚∈𝒴⊆𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω){\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq\dom(\Omega).
2.
Zero loss. If Ω\Omega is a lower semi-continuous proper convex
function, then
min𝜽⁡LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=0\min_{\bm{\theta}}L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=0, and LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=0⇔𝒚∈∂Ω∗​(𝜽)L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=0\Leftrightarrow{\bm{y}}\in\partial\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}). If Ω\Omega is strictly convex,
then LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=0⇔𝒚=𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=∇Ω∗​(𝜽)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝜽∈ℝdLΩ​(𝜽,𝐲)L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=0\Leftrightarrow{\bm{y}}=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})=\argmin_{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}).
3.
Convexity & subgradients. LΩL_{\Omega} is convex in 𝜽\bm{\theta} and the
residual vectors are its subgradients: 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)−𝒚∈∂LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})-{\bm{y}}\in\partial L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}).
4.
Differentiability & smoothness.
If Ω\Omega is strictly convex, then LΩL_{\Omega}
is differentiable and ∇LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)−𝒚\nabla L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})-{\bm{y}}.
If Ω\Omega is strongly convex, then LΩL_{\Omega} is smooth, i.e., ∇LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)\nabla L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}) is Lipschitz continuous.
5.
Temperature scaling. For any constant t>0t>0,
Lt​Ω​(𝜽,𝒚)=t​LΩ​(𝜽/t,𝒚)L_{t\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=tL_{\Omega}(\nicefrac{{\bm{\theta}}}{{t}};{\bm{y}}).
6.
Constant invariance. For any constant c∈ℝc\in{\mathbb{R}},
LΩ+c​(𝜽,𝒚)=LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)L_{\Omega+c}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}).
Remarkably, the non-negativity, convexity and constant invariance properties
hold even if Ω\Omega is not convex. The zero loss property
follows from the fact that, if Ω\Omega is l.s.c. proper convex, then
(21) becomes an equality (i.e., the duality gap
is zero) if and only if 𝜽∈∂Ω⁡(𝝁)\bm{\theta}\in\partial\Omega(\bm{\mu}).
It suggests that
the minimization of Fenchel-Young losses attempts to adjust the model to produce
predictions 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) that are close to the target 𝒚{\bm{y}},
reducing the duality gap. This is illustrated with
Ω=12​‖𝝁‖2\Omega=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mu}\|^{2} and 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)=ℝd\dom(\Omega)={\mathbb{R}}^{d} (leading to the squared
loss) in Figure 3.
Domain of 𝛀∗\mathbf{\Omega^{*}}.
Our assumption that the maximum in the regularized prediction
function (8) is achieved for all 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} implies that
𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω∗)=ℝd\dom(\Omega^{*})={\mathbb{R}}^{d}.
This assumption is quite mild and does not require 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega) to be bounded.
Minimizing LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}) w.r.t. 𝜽\bm{\theta} is therefore an
unconstrained convex optimization problem.
This contrasts with proper loss functions, which are defined over the
probability simplex,
as discussed in §10.
Figure 3: Illustration of the Fenchel-Young loss LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=Ω∗​(𝜽)+Ω⁡(𝒚)−⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})+\Omega({\bm{y}})-{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}, here with
Ω⁡(𝝁)=12​‖𝝁‖2\Omega(\bm{\mu})=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mu}\|^{2} and 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)=ℝd\dom(\Omega)={\mathbb{R}}^{d}.
Minimizing LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}) w.r.t. 𝜽\bm{\theta} can be seen as minimizing the duality gap, the difference between
Ω⁡(𝝁)\Omega(\bm{\mu}) and the tangent 𝝁↦⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩−Ω∗​(𝜽)\bm{\mu}\mapsto{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}-\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}),
at 𝝁=𝒚\bm{\mu}={\bm{y}} (the ground truth). The regularized prediction 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) is
the value of 𝝁\bm{\mu} at which the tangent touches Ω⁡(𝝁)\Omega(\bm{\mu}).
When Ω\Omega is of Legendre type, LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}) is equal to the Bregman
divergence generated by Ω\Omega between 𝒚{\bm{y}} and 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) (cf.
§3.2). However,
we do not require that assumption in this paper.
3.2 Relation with Bregman divergences
Fenchel-Young losses seamlessly work when 𝒴=𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω){\mathcal{Y}}=\dom(\Omega) instead of 𝒴⊂𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω){\mathcal{Y}}\subset\dom(\Omega).
For example, in the case of the logistic loss, where
−Ω-\Omega is the Shannon entropy restricted to △d\triangle^{d}, allowing 𝒚∈△d{\bm{y}}\in\triangle^{d} instead of 𝒚∈{𝒆i}i=1d{\bm{y}}\in\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
yields the cross-entropy loss,
LΩ(𝜽;𝒚)=𝖪𝖫(𝒚∥𝗌𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑(𝜽))L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=\mathsf{KL}({\bm{y}}\|\softmax(\bm{\theta})), where 𝖪𝖫\mathsf{KL} denotes the
(generalized)
Kullback-Leibler divergence
𝖪𝖫(𝒚∥𝝁)≔∑iyilogyiμi−∑iyi+∑iμi.\mathsf{KL}({\bm{y}}\|\bm{\mu})\coloneqq\sum_{i}y_{i}\log\frac{y_{i}}{\mu_{i}}-\sum_{i}y_{i}+\sum_{i}\mu_{i}.
(22)
This can be useful in a multi-label setting with supervision in the form of
label proportions.
From this example, it is tempting to conjecture that a similar result holds for
more general Bregman divergences (Bregman, 1967).
Recall that the Bregman divergence BΩ:𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)×𝗋𝖾𝗅𝗂𝗇𝗍(𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω))→ℝ+B_{\Omega}\colon\dom(\Omega)\times\relint(\dom(\Omega))\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+} generated by a strictly
convex and differentiable Ω\Omega is
BΩ(𝒚∥𝝁)≔Ω(𝒚)−Ω(𝝁)−⟨∇Ω(𝝁),𝒚−𝝁⟩.B_{\Omega}({\bm{y}}\|\bm{\mu})\coloneqq\Omega({\bm{y}})-\Omega(\bm{\mu})-{\langle\nabla\Omega(\bm{\mu}),{\bm{y}}-\bm{\mu}\rangle}.
(23)
In other words, this is the difference at 𝒚{\bm{y}} between Ω\Omega and its
linearization around 𝝁\bm{\mu}. It turns out that LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}) is not in
general equal to BΩ(𝒚∥𝒚^Ω(𝜽))B_{\Omega}({\bm{y}}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})). In fact the latter is
not necessarily convex in 𝜽\bm{\theta} while the former always is.
However, there is a duality relationship
between Fenchel-Young losses and Bregman divergences, as we now discuss.
A “mixed-space” Bregman divergence.
Letting 𝜽=∇Ω​(𝝁)\bm{\theta}=\nabla\Omega(\bm{\mu}) (i.e., (𝜽,𝝁)(\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}) is a dual pair), we have
Ω∗​(𝜽)=⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩−Ω⁡(𝝁)\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})={\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}-\Omega(\bm{\mu}). Substituting in
(23), we get
BΩ(𝒚∥𝝁)=LΩ(𝜽;𝒚).B_{\Omega}({\bm{y}}\|\bm{\mu})=L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}).
In other words,
Fenchel-Young losses can be
viewed as a “mixed-form Bregman divergence”
(Amari, 2016, Theorem 1.1)
where the
argument 𝝁\bm{\mu} in (23) is replaced by its dual point 𝜽\bm{\theta}.
This difference is best seen by comparing
the function signatures, LΩ:𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω∗)×𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)→ℝ+L_{\Omega}\colon\dom(\Omega^{*})\times\dom(\Omega)\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+} vs. BΩ:𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)×𝗋𝖾𝗅𝗂𝗇𝗍(𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω))→ℝ+B_{\Omega}\colon\dom(\Omega)\times\relint(\dom(\Omega))\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}. An important consequence is that
Fenchel-Young losses do not impose any restriction on their left argument 𝜽\bm{\theta}:
Our assumption that the maximum in the prediction
function (8) is achieved for all 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} implies
𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω∗)=ℝd\dom(\Omega^{*})={\mathbb{R}}^{d}.
In contrast, a Bregman divergence would typically need to be composed with a
mapping from ℝd{\mathbb{R}}^{d} to 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega), such as 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}, resulting in a
possibly non-convex function.
Case of Legendre-type functions.
We can make the relationship with Bregman divergences further precise
when Ω=Ψ+I𝒞\Omega=\Psi+I_{\mathcal{C}}, where Ψ\Psi is restricted to the class of
so-called Legendre-type functions (Rockafellar, 1970; Wainwright and Jordan, 2008). We
first recall the definition of this class of functions and then state our results.
Definition 5.
Essentially smooth and Legendre type functions
A function Ψ\Psi is essentially smooth if
•
𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ)\dom(\Psi) is non-empty,
•
Ψ\Psi is differentiable throughout 𝗂𝗇𝗍(𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ))\interior(\dom(\Psi)),
•
and limi→∞∇Ψ​(𝝁i)=+∞\lim_{i\to\infty}\nabla\Psi(\bm{\mu}^{i})=+\infty for any
sequence {𝝁i}\{\bm{\mu}^{i}\} contained in 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ)\dom(\Psi), and converging to a
boundary point of 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ)\dom(\Psi).
A function Ψ\Psi is of Legendre type if
•
it is strictly convex on 𝗂𝗇𝗍(𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ))\interior(\dom(\Psi))
•
and essentially smooth.
For instance, Ψ⁡(𝝁)=12​‖𝝁‖2\Psi(\bm{\mu})=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mu}\|^{2} is Legendre-type with
𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ)=ℝd\dom(\Psi)={\mathbb{R}}^{d}, and Ψ⁡(𝝁)=∑iμi​log⁡μi\Psi(\bm{\mu})=\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\log\mu_{i} is
Legendre-type with 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ)=ℝ+d\dom(\Psi)={\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{+}.
However, Ω⁡(𝝁)=12​‖𝝁‖2+Iℝ+d​(𝝁)\Omega(\bm{\mu})=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mu}\|^{2}+I_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}(\bm{\mu}) is not
Legendre-type, since the gradient of Ω\Omega does not explode everywhere on the
boundary of ℝ+d{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}.
The Legendre-type assumption crucially implies that
∇Ψ​(∇Ψ∗​(𝜽))=𝜽for all𝜽∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ∗).\nabla\Psi(\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}))=\bm{\theta}\quad\text{for all}\quad\bm{\theta}\in\dom(\Psi^{*}).
(24)
We can use this fact to derive the following results, proved in
Appendix B.2.
Proposition 6.
Relation with Bregman divergences
Let Ψ\Psi be of Legendre type with 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ∗)=ℝd\dom(\Psi^{*})={\mathbb{R}}^{d} and
let 𝒞⊆𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ){\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\dom(\Psi) be a convex set.
Let Ω\Omega be the restriction of Ψ\Psi to 𝒞⊆𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ){\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\dom(\Psi), i.e.,
Ω≔Ψ+I𝒞\Omega\coloneqq\Psi+I_{\mathcal{C}}.
1.
Bregman projection. The prediction function regularized by
Ω\Omega, 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}), reduces to the Bregman projection of
𝒚^Ψ​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Psi}(\bm{\theta}) onto 𝒞{\mathcal{C}}:
𝒚^Ω(𝜽)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝝁∈𝒞⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩−Ψ(𝝁)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝝁∈𝒞BΨ(𝝁∥𝐲^Ψ(𝜽)).\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\argmax_{\bm{\mu}\in{\mathcal{C}}}{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}-\Psi(\bm{\mu})=\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in{\mathcal{C}}}B_{\Psi}(\bm{\mu}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Psi}(\bm{\theta})).
(25)
2.
Difference of divergences. For all 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} and 𝒚∈𝒞{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{C}}:
LΩ(𝜽;𝒚)=BΨ(𝒚∥𝒚^Ψ(𝜽))−BΨ(𝒚^Ω(𝜽)∥𝒚^Ψ(𝜽)).L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=B_{\Psi}({\bm{y}}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Psi}(\bm{\theta}))-B_{\Psi}(\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Psi}(\bm{\theta})).
(26)
3.
Bound. For all 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} and 𝒚∈𝒞{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{C}}:
0≤BΨ(𝒚∥𝒚^Ω(𝜽))⏟possibly
non-convex in 𝜽≤LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)⏟convex in ​𝜽0\leq\underbrace{B_{\Psi}({\bm{y}}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}))}_{\text{possibly
non-convex in }\bm{\theta}}\leq\underbrace{L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})}_{\text{convex in }\bm{\theta}}
(27)
with equality when the loss is minimized
𝒚^Ω(𝜽)=𝒚⇔LΩ(𝜽;𝒚)=0⇔BΨ(𝒚∥𝒚^Ω(𝜽))=0.\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})={\bm{y}}\Leftrightarrow L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=0\Leftrightarrow B_{\Psi}({\bm{y}}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}))=0.
(28)
4.
Composite form.
When 𝒞=𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ){\mathcal{C}}=\dom(\Psi), i.e., Ω=Ψ\Omega=\Psi, we have equality for all
𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
LΩ(𝜽;𝒚)=BΩ(𝒚∥𝒚^Ω(𝜽)).L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=B_{\Omega}({\bm{y}}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})).
(29)
We illustrate these properties using Ψ=12∥⋅∥2\Psi=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|^{2} as a
running example. From the first property, since
𝒚^Ψ​(𝜽)=𝜽\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Psi}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{\theta}
and BΨ(𝒚∥𝝁)=12∥𝒚−𝝁∥2B_{\Psi}({\bm{y}}\|\bm{\mu})=\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-\bm{\mu}\|^{2}, we get
𝒚^Ω(𝜽)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝝁∈𝒞BΨ(𝝁∥𝐲^Ψ(𝜽))=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝝁∈𝒞BΨ(𝝁∥𝜽)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝝁∈𝒞∥𝝁−𝜽∥2,\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in{\mathcal{C}}}B_{\Psi}(\bm{\mu}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Psi}(\bm{\theta}))=\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in{\mathcal{C}}}B_{\Psi}(\bm{\mu}\|\bm{\theta})=\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\theta}\|^{2},
(30)
recovering the Euclidean projection onto 𝒞{\mathcal{C}}.
The reduction of regularized prediction functions to Bregman projections
(when Ψ\Psi is of Legendre type) is
useful because there exist efficient algorithms for computing the Bregman
projection onto various convex sets
(Yasutake et al., 2011; Suehiro et al., 2012; Krichene et al., 2015; Lim and Wright, 2016).
Therefore, we can use these algorithms to compute 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) provided
that 𝒚^Ψ​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Psi}(\bm{\theta}) is available.
From the second property, we obtain for all 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} and 𝒚∈𝒞{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=12​‖𝒚−𝜽‖2−12​‖𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)−𝜽‖2.L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-\bm{\theta}\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{\theta}\|^{2}.
(31)
This recovers the expression of the sparsemax loss given in
Table 1 with 𝒞=△d{\mathcal{C}}=\triangle^{d}.
From the third claim, we obtain for all 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} and 𝒚∈𝒞{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
12​‖𝒚−𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)‖2≤LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚).\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\|^{2}\leq L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}).
(32)
This shows that LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}) provides a
convex upper-bound for the possibly non-convex composite function BΨ(𝒚∥𝒚^Ω(𝜽))B_{\Psi}({\bm{y}}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})). In particular, when 𝒞=△d{\mathcal{C}}=\triangle^{d},
we get 12​‖𝒚−𝗌𝗉𝖺𝗋𝗌𝖾𝗆𝖺𝗑(𝜽)‖2≤LΩ​(𝜽,𝐲)\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-\sparsemax(\bm{\theta})\|^{2}\leq L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}). This suggests that the sparsemax loss is useful for sparse label
proportion estimation, as confirmed in our experiments
(§9).
Finally, from the last property,
if Ω=Ψ=12∥⋅∥2\Omega=\Psi=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|^{2},
we obtain LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=12​‖𝒚−𝜽‖2L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-\bm{\theta}\|^{2}, which is indeed the squared loss given in Table
1.
3.3 Expected loss, Bayes risk and Bregman information
In this section, we discuss the relation between the pointwise Bayes risk
(minimal achievable loss) of a Fenchel-Young loss and Bregman information
(Banerjee et al., 2005).
Expected loss.
Let YY be a random variable taking values in 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}} following the distribution
𝒑∈△|𝒴|{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}. The expected loss (a.k.a. expected risk) is then
𝔼𝒑​[LΩ​(𝜽,Y)]\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};Y)]
=∑𝒚∈𝒴p⁡(𝒚)​LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}p({\bm{y}})L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})
(33)
=∑𝒚∈𝒴p⁡(𝒚)​(Ω∗​(𝜽)+Ω⁡(𝒚)−⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩)\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}p({\bm{y}})(\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})+\Omega({\bm{y}})-{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle})
(34)
=𝔼𝒑​[Ω⁡(Y)]+Ω∗​(𝜽)−⟨𝜽,𝔼𝒑​[Y]⟩\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[\Omega(Y)]+\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})-{\langle\bm{\theta},{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]\rangle}
(35)
=LΩ​(𝜽,𝔼𝒑​[Y])+𝕀Ω​(Y,𝒑).\displaystyle=L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y])+{\mathbb{I}}_{\Omega}(Y;{\bm{p}}).
(36)
Here, we defined the Bregman information of YY by
𝕀Ω(Y;𝒑)≔min𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)𝔼𝒑[BΩ(Y∥𝝁)]=𝔼𝒑[BΩ(Y∥𝔼𝒑[Y])]=𝔼𝒑[Ω(Y)]−Ω(𝔼𝒑[Y]).{\mathbb{I}}_{\Omega}(Y;{\bm{p}})\coloneqq\min_{\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega)}{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[B_{\Omega}(Y\|\bm{\mu})]={\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}\left[B_{\Omega}(Y\|{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y])\right]={\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[\Omega(Y)]-\Omega({\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]).
(37)
We refer the reader to Banerjee et al. (2005) for a detailed discussion as
to why the last two equalities hold.
The r.h.s. is exactly equal to the difference between the two sides of
Jensen’s inequality 𝔼⁡[Ω⁡(Y)]≥Ω⁡(𝔼⁡[Y]){\mathbb{E}}[\Omega(Y)]\geq\Omega({\mathbb{E}}[Y]) and is therefore
non-negative. For this reason, it is sometimes also called Jensen gap
(Reid and Williamson, 2011).
Bayes risk.
From Proposition 4, we know that min𝜽⁡LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=0\min_{\bm{\theta}}L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=0 for all 𝒚∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω){\bm{y}}\in\dom(\Omega). Therefore, the pointwise Bayes risk
coincides precisely with the Bregman information of YY,
min𝜽∈ℝd⁡𝔼𝒑​[LΩ​(𝜽,Y)]=min𝜽∈ℝd⁡LΩ​(𝜽,𝔼𝒑​[Y])+𝕀Ω​(Y,𝒑)=𝕀Ω​(Y,𝒑),\min_{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};Y)]=\min_{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y])+{\mathbb{I}}_{\Omega}(Y;{\bm{p}})={\mathbb{I}}_{\Omega}(Y;{\bm{p}}),
(38)
provided that 𝔼𝒑​[Y]∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω){\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]\in\dom(\Omega).
A similar relation between Bayes risk and Bregman
information exists for proper losses (Reid and Williamson, 2011).
We can think of (38) as a measure of the “difficulty” of the
task.
Combining (36) and (38), we obtain
𝔼𝒑​[LΩ​(𝜽,Y)]−min𝜽∈ℝd⁡𝔼𝒑​[LΩ​(𝜽,Y)]=LΩ​(𝜽,𝔼𝒑​[Y]),{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};Y)]-\min_{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};Y)]=L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]),
(39)
the pointwise “regret” of 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} w.r.t. 𝒑∈△|𝒴|{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}.
If 𝒴={𝒆i}i=1d{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d},
LΩ​(𝜽,𝔼𝒑​[Y])=LΩ​(𝜽,𝒑)L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y])=L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{p}}).
3.4 Cost-sensitive losses
Fenchel-Young losses also include the hinge loss of support vector machines.
Indeed, from any classification loss LΩL_{\Omega}, we can construct a
cost-sensitive version of it as follows. Define
Ψ⁡(𝝁,𝒚)≔Ω⁡(𝝁)−⟨𝒄𝒚,𝝁⟩,\Psi(\bm{\mu};{\bm{y}})\coloneqq\Omega(\bm{\mu})-{\langle\bm{c}_{{\bm{y}}},\bm{\mu}\rangle},
(40)
where 𝒄𝒚∈ℝ+|𝒴|\bm{c}_{{\bm{y}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}_{+} is a fixed
cost vector that may depend on the ground truth 𝒚{\bm{y}}. For example, 𝒄𝒚=𝟏−𝒚\bm{c}_{{\bm{y}}}=\mathbf{1}-{\bm{y}} corresponds to the 0/1 cost and can be used to impose a margin.
Then, LΨL_{\Psi} is a cost-sensitive version of LΩL_{\Omega}, which can be written
as
LΨ⁡(⋅,𝒚)​(𝜽,𝒚)=LΩ​(𝜽+𝒄𝒚,𝒚)=Ω∗​(𝜽+𝒄𝒚)+Ω⁡(𝒚)−⟨𝜽+𝒄𝒚,𝒚⟩.L_{\Psi(\cdot;{\bm{y}})}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}+\bm{c}_{\bm{y}};{\bm{y}})=\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}+\bm{c}_{\bm{y}})+\Omega({\bm{y}})-{\langle\bm{\theta}+\bm{c}_{{\bm{y}}},{\bm{y}}\rangle}.
(41)
This construction recovers the multi-class hinge loss (Crammer and Singer (2001);
Ω=0\Omega=0), the softmax-margin loss (Gimpel and Smith (2010); Ω=−𝖧s\Omega=-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}), and
the cost-augmented sparsemax (Shalev-Shwartz and Zhang (2016, Eq. (13)),
Niculae et al. (2018); Ω=12∥⋅∥2\Omega=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|^{2}).
It is easy to see that the associated regularized prediction function is
𝒚^Ψ​(𝜽)=𝒚^Ω​(𝜽+𝒄𝒚).\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Psi}(\bm{\theta})=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}+\bm{c}_{\bm{y}}).
(42)
For the 0/1 cost 𝒄𝒚=𝟏−𝒚\bm{c}_{{\bm{y}}}=\mathbf{1}-{\bm{y}} and 𝒚=𝒆k{\bm{y}}=\bm{e}_{k},
we have
𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑i∈[d](𝐲^Ψ​(𝜽))i=k⟹𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑i∈[d](𝐲^Ω​(𝜽))i=k,\argmax_{i\in[d]}~(\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Psi}(\bm{\theta}))_{i}=k\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\argmax_{i\in[d]}~(\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}))_{i}=k,
(43)
justifying the use of 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega} at prediction time.
4 Probabilistic prediction with Fenchel-Young losses
In the previous section, we presented Fenchel-Young losses in a broad setting.
We now restrict to classification over the probability simplex.
More precisely, we restrict to the case 𝒴={𝒆i}i=1d{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d},
(i.e., unstructured multi-class classification), and assume
that 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)⊆𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)=△d\dom(\Omega)\subseteq\conv({\mathcal{Y}})=\triangle^{d}.
In this case, the regularized prediction function (8) becomes
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)∈𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐩∈△d⟨𝜽,𝐩⟩−Ω⁡(𝐩),\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\in\argmax_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}{{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{p}}\rangle}}-\Omega({\bm{p}}),
(44)
where 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} is a vector of (possibly negative) prediction scores
produced by a model 𝒇W​(𝒙)\bm{f}_{W}({\bm{x}})
and 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d} is a discrete probability distribution.
It is a generalized exponential family distribution
(Grünwald and Dawid, 2004; Frongillo and Reid, 2014) with natural parameter 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} and
regularization Ω\Omega.
Of particular interest is the case where 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) is
sparse, meaning that there are scores 𝜽\bm{\theta} for which the
resulting 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) assigns zero probability to some classes. As seen in
§2.2, this happens for example with the sparsemax
transformation, but not with the softmax.
Later, in §5, we will
establish conditions for the regularized prediction function to be sparse
and will connect it to the notion of separation margin.
We first discuss generalized entropies and the properties of the Fenchel-Young
losses they induce (§4.1).
We then discuss their expected loss, Bayes risk and Fisher consistency
(§4.2).
We then give examples of
generalized entropies and corresponding loss functions, several of them new to
our knowledge (§4.3).
Finally, we discuss the binary classification setting, recovering several
examples of commonly-used loss functions (§4.4).
4.1 Fenchel-Young loss generated by a generalized entropy
Generalized entropies.
A natural choice of regularization function Ω\Omega over the probability
simplex is Ω=−𝖧\Omega=-\mathsf{H}, where 𝖧\mathsf{H} is a generalized entropy
(Grünwald and Dawid, 2004), also called uncertainty function by DeGroot (1962):
a concave function over △d\triangle^{d}, used to measure the “uncertainty” in a
distribution 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}.
Assumptions:
We will make the following assumptions about 𝖧\mathsf{H}.
A.1.
Zero entropy: 𝖧⁡(𝒑)=0\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})=0 if 𝒑{\bm{p}} is a delta distribution,
i.e., 𝒑∈{𝒆i}i=1d{\bm{p}}\in\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}.
A.2.
Strict concavity: 𝖧⁡((1−α)​𝒑+α​𝒑′)>(1−α)​𝖧​(𝒑)+α​𝖧​(𝒑′)\mathsf{H}\big((1-\alpha){\bm{p}}+\alpha{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\big)>{(1-\alpha)}\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})+\alpha\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}}^{\prime}), for 𝒑≠𝒑′{\bm{p}}\neq{\bm{p}}^{\prime}, α∈(0,1)\alpha\in(0,1).
A.3.
Symmetry: 𝖧⁡(𝒑)=𝖧⁡(𝑷​𝒑)\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})=\mathsf{H}(\bm{P}{\bm{p}}) for any 𝑷∈𝒫\bm{P}\in\mathcal{P}.
Assumptions A.2 and A.3 imply that 𝖧\mathsf{H} is Schur-concave (Bauschke and Combettes, 2017),
a common requirement in generalized entropies. This in turn implies assumption
A.1, up to a constant (that constant can easily be subtracted so as to satisfy
assumption A.1).
As suggested by the next result,
proved in §B.3,
together, these assumptions
imply that 𝖧\mathsf{H} can be used as a sensible
uncertainty measure.
Proposition 7.
If 𝖧\mathsf{H} satisfies assumptions A.1–A.3, then it is non-negative and uniquely
maximized by the uniform distribution 𝐩=𝟏/d{\bm{p}}=\mathbf{1}/d.
That is, assumptions A.1-A.3 ensure that the uniform distribution is the maximum
entropy distribution.
A particular case of generalized entropies satisfying assumptions A.1–A.3 are
uniformly separable functions of the form 𝖧⁡(𝒑)=∑j=1dh⁡(pj)\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}h(p_{j}),
where h:[0,1]→ℝ+h:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+} is a non-negative strictly concave function such
that h⁡(0)=h⁡(1)=0h(0)=h(1)=0. However, our framework is not restricted to this form.
Induced Fenchel-Young loss.
If the ground truth is 𝒚=𝒆k{\bm{y}}=\bm{e}_{k} and assumption
A.1 holds, the Fenchel-Young loss definition (18) becomes
L−𝖧​(𝜽,𝒆k)=(−𝖧)∗​(𝜽)−θk.L_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})=(-\mathsf{H})^{*}(\bm{\theta})-\theta_{k}.
(45)
This form was also recently proposed by Duchi et al. (2018, Proposition 3).
By using the fact that Ω∗​(𝜽+c​𝟏)=Ω∗​(𝜽)+c\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}+c\bm{1})=\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})+c for all c∈ℝc\in{\mathbb{R}} if 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)⊆△d\dom(\Omega)\subseteq\triangle^{d},
we can further rewrite it as
L−𝖧​(𝜽,𝒆k)=(−𝖧)∗​(𝜽−θk​𝟏).L_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})=(-\mathsf{H})^{*}(\bm{\theta}-\theta_{k}\bm{1}).
(46)
This expression shows that Fenchel-Young losses over △d\triangle^{d} can be
written solely in terms of the generalized “cumulant function” (−𝖧)∗(-\mathsf{H})^{*}.
Indeed, when 𝖧\mathsf{H} is Shannon’s entropy, we recover the cumulant (a.k.a.
log-partition) function
(−𝖧s)∗(𝜽)=log∑i=1dexp(θi)(-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}})^{*}(\bm{\theta})=\log\sum_{i=1}^{d}\exp(\theta_{i}).
When 𝖧\mathsf{H} is strongly concave over △d\triangle^{d},
we can also see (−𝖧)∗(-\mathsf{H})^{*} as a smoothed max operator
(Nesterov, 2005; Niculae and Blondel, 2017; Mensch and Blondel, 2018) and hence L−𝖧​(𝜽,𝒆k)L_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k}) can be seen as a smoothed upper-bound of the perceptron loss
(𝜽;𝒆k)↦maxi∈[d]⁡θi−θk(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})\mapsto\max_{i\in[d]}\theta_{i}-\theta_{k}.
It is well-known that minimizing the logistic loss (𝜽;𝒆k)↦log∑iexpθi−θk(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})\mapsto\log\sum_{i}\exp\theta_{i}-\theta_{k} is equivalent to minimizing the 𝖪𝖫\mathsf{KL} divergence
between 𝒆k\bm{e}_{k} and 𝗌𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑(𝜽)\softmax(\bm{\theta}), which in turn is equivalent to maximizing the
likelihood of the ground-truth label, 𝗌𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑(𝜽)k\softmax(\bm{\theta})_{k}. Importantly, this
equivalence does not carry over for generalized entropies 𝖧\mathsf{H}: minimizing
L−𝖧​(𝜽,𝒆k)L_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k}) is not in general equivalent to minimizing B−𝖧​(𝒆k,𝒚^−𝖧​(𝜽))B_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{e}_{k},\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta})) or maximizing 𝒚^−𝖧​(𝜽)k\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta})_{k}. In fact,
maximizing the likelihood is generally a non-concave problem. Fenchel-Young
losses can be seen as a principled way to construct a convex loss regardless of
𝖧\mathsf{H}.
4.2 Expected loss, Bayes risk and Fisher consistency
Let YY be a random variable taking values in 𝒴={𝒆i}i=1d{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}.
If 𝖧\mathsf{H} satisfies assumption A.1,
we can use (45) to obtain simpler expressions of
the expected loss and pointwise Bayes risk
than the ones derived in §3.3.
Expected loss.
Indeed, the expected loss (risk) for all 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d} simplifies to:
𝔼𝒑​[L−𝖧​(𝜽,Y)]=∑i=1dpi​L−𝖧​(𝜽,𝒆i)=∑i=1dpi​((−𝖧)∗​(𝜽)−θj)=(−𝖧)∗​(𝜽)−⟨𝒑,𝜽⟩.{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[L_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta};Y)]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}L_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}((-\mathsf{H})^{*}(\bm{\theta})-\theta_{j})=(-\mathsf{H})^{*}(\bm{\theta})-{\langle{\bm{p}},\bm{\theta}\rangle}.
(47)
Bayes risk and entropy.
The pointwise (conditional) Bayes risk thus becomes
min𝜽∈ℝd⁡𝔼𝒑​[L−𝖧​(𝜽,Y)]=min𝜽∈ℝd⁡(−𝖧)∗​(𝜽)−⟨𝒑,𝜽⟩=𝖧⁡(𝒑).\min_{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[L_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta};Y)]=\min_{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(-\mathsf{H})^{*}(\bm{\theta})-{\langle{\bm{p}},\bm{\theta}\rangle}=\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}}).
(48)
Therefore, the pointwise Bayes risk is equal to the generalized entropy 𝖧\mathsf{H}
generating L−𝖧L_{-\mathsf{H}}, evaluated at 𝒑{\bm{p}}. This is consistent with
(38), which states that the pointwise Bayes risk is equal to
the Bregman information of YY under 𝒑{\bm{p}}, because
𝕀−𝖧​(Y,𝒑)=−𝕀𝖧​(Y,𝒑)=𝖧⁡(𝔼𝒑​[Y])−𝔼𝒑​[𝖧⁡(Y)]=𝖧⁡(𝒑),{\mathbb{I}}_{-\mathsf{H}}(Y;{\bm{p}})=-{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathsf{H}}(Y;{\bm{p}})=\mathsf{H}({\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y])-{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[\mathsf{H}(Y)]=\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}}),
(49)
where we used 𝔼𝒑​[Y]=𝒑{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]={\bm{p}} and assumption A.1.
A similar relation
between generalized entropies and pointwise (conditional) Bayes risk
is well-known in the proper loss (scoring rule) literature
(Grünwald and Dawid, 2004; Gneiting and Raftery, 2007; Reid and Williamson, 2010; Williamson et al., 2016).
The main difference is that the minimization above is over ℝd{\mathbb{R}}^{d}, while it is
over △d\triangle^{d} in that literature (§10.1).
As noted by Reid and Williamson (2011, §4.6), Bregman information can also be connected
the notion of statistical information developed by DeGroot (1962), the
reduction between prior and posterior uncertainty 𝖧\mathsf{H}, of which mutual
information is a special case.
Fisher consistency.
From (47), 𝔼𝒑​[L−𝖧​(𝜽,Y)]=L−𝖧​(𝜽,𝒑)+Ω⁡(𝒑){\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[L_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta};Y)]=L_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{p}})+\Omega({\bm{p}}). Combined with Proposition 4, we have that
the pointwise Bayes risk (48) is achieved if and only
if 𝒚^−𝖧​(𝜽)=𝒑\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta})={\bm{p}}. Such losses are Fisher consistent estimators
of probabilities (Williamson et al., 2016).
4.3 Examples
We now give examples of generalized entropies over the simplex △d\triangle^{d} (we
omit the indicator function I△dI_{\triangle^{d}} from the definitions since there
is no ambiguity). We illustrate them together with the regularized prediction
function and loss they produce in Figure 4. Several of the
resulting loss functions are new to our knowledge.
Shannon entropy (Shannon and Weaver, 1949).
This is the foundation of information theory, defined as
𝖧s(𝒑)≔−∑j=1dpjlogpj.\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}({\bm{p}})\coloneqq-\sum_{j=1}^{d}p_{j}\log p_{j}.
(50)
As seen in
Table 1, the resulting Fenchel-Young loss L−𝖧sL_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}}
corresponds to the logistic loss. The associated distribution is the classical
softmax, Eq. (10).
Tsallis α\alpha-entropies (Tsallis, 1988).
These entropies are defined as
𝖧αt​(𝒑)≔k​(α−1)−1​(1−∑j=1dpjα),\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{\alpha}({\bm{p}})\coloneqq k(\alpha-1)^{-1}\left(1-\sum_{j=1}^{d}p_{j}^{\alpha}\right),
(51)
where α≥0\alpha\geq 0 and kk is an arbitrary positive constant.
They arise as a generalization of the Shannon-Khinchin axioms to
non-extensive systems (Suyari, 2004) and have numerous scientific
applications (Gell-Mann and Tsallis, 2004; Martins et al., 2009a).
For convenience, we set k=α−1k=\alpha^{-1} for the rest of this paper.
Tsallis entropies satisfy assumptions A.1–A.3 and can also be written in separable form:
𝖧αt​(𝒑)≔∑j=1dhα​(pj)withhα​(t)≔t−tαα⁡(α−1).\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{\alpha}({\bm{p}})\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{d}h_{\alpha}(p_{j})\quad\text{with}\quad h_{\alpha}(t)\coloneqq\frac{t-t^{\alpha}}{\alpha(\alpha-1)}.
(52)
The limit case α→1\alpha\rightarrow 1 corresponds to the Shannon entropy.
When α=2\alpha=2, we recover the Gini index (Gini, 1912), a
popular “impurity measure” for decision trees:
𝖧2t​(𝒑)=12​∑j=1dpj​(1−pj)=12​(1−‖𝒑‖22)∀𝒑∈△d.\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{2}({\bm{p}})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}p_{j}(1-p_{j})=\frac{1}{2}(1-\|{\bm{p}}\|_{2}^{2})\quad\forall{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}.
(53)
Using the constant invariance property in Proposition 4,
it can be checked that L−𝖧2tL_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{2}} recovers
the sparsemax loss (Martins and Astudillo, 2016) (cf. Table 1).
Another interesting case is α→+∞\alpha\rightarrow+\infty, which gives
𝖧∞t​(𝒑)=0\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{\infty}({\bm{p}})=0, hence L−𝖧∞tL_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{\infty}} is the perceptron loss in
Table 1. The resulting “𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑\argmax” distribution puts
all probability mass on the top-scoring classes.
In summary, the prediction functions for
α=1,2,∞\alpha=1,2,\infty are respectively 𝗌𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑\softmax, 𝗌𝗉𝖺𝗋𝗌𝖾𝗆𝖺𝗑\sparsemax, and
𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑\argmax.
Tsallis entropies can therefore be seen as a continuous
parametric family subsuming these important cases.
Norm entropies.
An interesting class of non-separable entropies are entropies generated by a
qq-norm, defined as
𝖧qn​(𝒑)≔1−‖𝒑‖q.\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{n}}_{q}({\bm{p}})\coloneqq 1-\|{\bm{p}}\|_{q}.
(54)
We call them norm entropies.
By the Minkowski inequality, qq-norms with q>1q>1 are strictly convex on the
simplex, so 𝖧qn\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{n}}_{q} satisfies assumptions A.1–A.3 for q>1q>1. The resulting
norm entropies differ from Tsallis entropies in that the norm is not raised to
the power of qq: a subtle but important difference. The limit case q→∞q\rightarrow\infty is particularly interesting: in this case, we obtain
𝖧n∞=1−∥⋅∥∞\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{n}}_{\infty}=1-\|\cdot\|_{\infty}, recovering the Berger-Parker dominance
index (Berger and Parker, 1970), widely used in ecology to measure species diversity.
We surprisingly encounter 𝖧∞n\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{n}}_{\infty} again in Section 5,
as a limit case for the existence of separation margins.
Squared norm entropies.
Inspired by Niculae and Blondel (2017), as a simple extension of the Gini index
(53), we consider the generalized entropy based on
squared qq-norms:
𝖧qsq​(𝒑)≔12​(1−‖𝒑‖q2)=12−12​(∑j=1dpjq)2q.\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{sq}}_{q}({\bm{p}})\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}(1-\|{\bm{p}}\|^{2}_{q})=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}p_{j}^{q}\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}.
(55)
The constant term 12\frac{1}{2}, omitted by Niculae and Blondel (2017),
ensures satisfaction of A.1.
For q∈(1,2]q\in(1,2], it is known that the squared qq-norm is strongly convex w.r.t. ∥⋅∥q\|\cdot\|_{q} (Ball et al., 1994), implying that (−𝖧qsq)∗(-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{sq}}_{q})^{*}, and therefore
L−𝖧qsqL_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{sq}}_{q}}, is smooth.
Although 𝒚^−𝖧qsq​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{sq}}_{q}}(\bm{\theta}) cannot to our knowledge be solved in
closed form for q∈(1,2)q\in(1,2), efficient iterative algorithms such as projected
gradient are available.
Rényi 𝜷\beta-entropies.
Rényi entropies (Rényi, 1961) are defined for any β≥0\beta\geq 0 as:
𝖧βr​(𝒑)≔11−β​log​∑j=1dpjβ.\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{r}}_{\beta}({\bm{p}})\coloneqq\frac{1}{1-\beta}\log\sum_{j=1}^{d}p_{j}^{\beta}.
(56)
Unlike Shannon and Tsallis entropies, Rényi entropies are not separable, with
the exception of β→1\beta\rightarrow 1, which also recovers Shannon entropy as a
limit case. The case β→+∞\beta\rightarrow+\infty gives 𝖧βr​(𝒑)=−log⁡‖𝒑‖∞\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{r}}_{\beta}({\bm{p}})=-\log\|{\bm{p}}\|_{\infty}.
For β∈[0,1]\beta\in[0,1], Rényi entropies satisfy assumptions
A.1–A.3; for β>1\beta>1, Rényi entropies fail to be concave. They are
however pseudo-concave (Mangasarian, 1965), meaning that, for all 𝒑,𝒒∈△d{\bm{p}},\bm{q}\in\triangle^{d}, ⟨∇𝖧βr​(𝒑),𝒒−𝒑⟩≤0{\langle\nabla\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{r}}_{\beta}({\bm{p}}),\bm{q}-{\bm{p}}\rangle}\leq 0 implies
𝖧βr​(𝒒)≤𝖧βr​(𝒑)\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{r}}_{\beta}(\bm{q})\leq\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{r}}_{\beta}({\bm{p}}). This implies, among other things, that
points 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d} with zero gradient are maximizers of ⟨𝒑,𝜽⟩+𝖧βr​(𝒑){\langle{\bm{p}},\bm{\theta}\rangle}+\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{r}}_{\beta}({\bm{p}}), which allows us to compute the predictive distribution
𝒚^−𝖧βr\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{r}}_{\beta}} with gradient-based methods.
Figure 4: Examples of generalized entropies (left) along with their
prediction distribution (middle) and Fenchel-Young losses (right) for the binary case,
where 𝒑=[t,1−t]∈△2{\bm{p}}=[t,1-t]\in\triangle^{2} and 𝜽=[s,0]∈ℝ2\bm{\theta}=[s,0]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}. Except
for softmax, which never exactly reaches 0, all distributions shown on the center
can have sparse support.
4.4 Binary classification case
When d=2d=2,
the convex conjugate expression simplifies and reduces to a
univariate maximization problem over [0,1][0,1]:
Ω∗​(𝜽)=max𝒑∈△2⁡⟨𝜽,𝒑⟩−Ω⁡(𝒑)=maxp∈[0,1]⁡θ1​p+θ2​(1−p)−Ω⁡([p,1−p])=ϕ∗​(θ1−θ2)+θ2,\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})=\max_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{2}}{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{p}}\rangle}-\Omega({\bm{p}})=\max_{p\in[0,1]}\theta_{1}p+\theta_{2}(1-p)-\Omega([p,1-p])=\phi^{*}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})+\theta_{2},
(57)
where we defined ϕ⁡(p)≔Ω⁡([p,1−p])\phi(p)\coloneqq\Omega([p,1-p]).
Likewise, it is easy to verify that we also have Ω∗​(𝜽)=ϕ∗​(θ2−θ1)+θ1\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})=\phi^{*}(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})+\theta_{1}.
Let us choose 𝜽=[s,−s]∈ℝ2\bm{\theta}=[s,-s]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2} for some s∈ℝs\in{\mathbb{R}}.
Since the ground truth is 𝒚∈{𝒆1,𝒆2}{\bm{y}}\in\{\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2}\},
where 𝒆1\bm{e}_{1} and 𝒆2\bm{e}_{2} represent positive and
negative classes,
from (46), we can write
LΩ​(𝜽,𝒆j)=Ω∗​(𝜽−θj​𝟏)={Ω∗​([0,−s])=ϕ∗​(−s) if ​j=1Ω∗​([s,0])=ϕ∗​(s) if ​j=2.L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{j})=\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}-\theta_{j}\bm{1})=\begin{cases}\Omega^{*}([0,-s])=\phi^{*}(-s)&\mbox{ if }j=1\\
\Omega^{*}([s,0])=\phi^{*}(s)&\mbox{ if }j=2\end{cases}.
(58)
This can be written concisely as ϕ∗​(−y​s)\phi^{*}(-ys) where y∈{+1,−1}y\in\{+1,-1\}.
Losses that can be written in this form are sometimes called
margin losses (Reid and Williamson, 2010).
These are losses that treat the
positive and negative classes symmetrically. We have thus made a connection between margin losses and
the regularization function ϕ⁡(p)≔Ω⁡([p,1−p])\phi(p)\coloneqq\Omega([p,1-p]).
Note, however, that this is different from the notion of margin
we develop in §5.
Examples.
Choosing ϕ⁡(p)=−𝖧s​([p,1−p])\phi(p)=-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}([p,1-p]) leads to
the binary logistic loss
ϕ∗​(−y​s)=log⁡(1+exp⁡(−y​s)).\phi^{*}(-ys)=\log(1+\exp(-ys)).
(59)
Choosing ϕ⁡(p)=−𝖧2t​([p,1−p])=p2−p\phi(p)=-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{2}([p,1-p])=p^{2}-p
leads to
ϕ∗​(u)={0 if ​u≤−1u if ​u≥114​(u+1)2 o.w. .\phi^{*}(u)=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }u\leq-1\\
u&\mbox{ if }u\geq 1\\
\frac{1}{4}(u+1)^{2}&\mbox{ o.w. }\end{cases}.
(60)
The resulting loss, ϕ⁡(−y​s)\phi(-ys), is known as the modified Huber loss in the
literature (Zhang, 2004). Hence, the sparsemax loss is the multiclass
extension of the modified Huber loss, as as already noted in (Martins and Astudillo, 2016).
Finally, we note that the modified Huber loss is closely related to the smoothed
hinge loss (Shalev-Shwartz and Zhang, 2016).
Indeed, choosing ϕ⁡(p)=12​p2−p\phi(p)=\frac{1}{2}p^{2}-p (notice the 12\frac{1}{2}
factor), we obtain
ϕ∗​(u)={0 if ​u≤−1u+12 if ​u≥012​(1+u)2 o.w. .\phi^{*}(u)=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }u\leq-1\\
u+\frac{1}{2}&\mbox{ if }u\geq 0\\
\frac{1}{2}(1+u)^{2}&\mbox{ o.w. }\end{cases}.
(61)
It can be verified that ϕ∗​(−y​s)\phi^{*}(-ys) is indeed the smoothed hinge loss.
5 Separation margin of Fenchel-Young losses
In this section, we are going to see that the simple assumptions A.1–A.3 about
a generalized entropy 𝖧\mathsf{H} are enough to obtain results about the separation margin associated with L−𝖧L_{-\mathsf{H}}.
The notion of margin is well-known in machine learning, lying at the heart of support vector machines and leading to generalization error bounds (Vapnik, 1998; Schölkopf and Smola, 2002; Guermeur, 2007).
We provide a definition and will see that many other Fenchel-Young losses also have a “margin,” for suitable conditions on 𝖧\mathsf{H}.
Then, we take a step further, and connect the existence of a margin with the sparsity of the regularized prediction function,
providing necessary and sufficient conditions for Fenchel-Young losses to have a margin. Finally, we show how this margin can be computed analytically.
Definition 8.
Separation margin
Let L⁡(𝛉,𝐞k)L(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k}) be a loss function over ℝd×{𝐞i}i=1d{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}.
We say that LL has
the separation margin property if there exists m>0m>0 such that:
θk≥m+maxj≠k⁡θj⇒L⁡(𝜽,𝒆k)=0.\theta_{k}\geq m+\max_{j\neq k}\theta_{j}\quad\Rightarrow\quad L(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})=0.
(62)
The smallest possible mm that satisfies (62) is called
the margin of LL, denoted margin⁡(L)\mathrm{margin}(L).
Examples.
The most famous example of a loss with a separation margin is the
multi-class hinge loss,
L⁡(𝜽,𝒆k)=max⁡{0,maxj≠k⁡1+θj−θk}L(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})=\max\{0,\max_{j\neq k}1+\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}\},
which we saw in Table 1 to be
a Fenchel-Young loss: it is immediate from the definition that its margin is 11.
Less trivially, Martins and Astudillo (2016, Prop. 3.5) showed that the sparsemax loss also
has the separation margin property.
On the negative side, the logistic loss does not have a margin, as it is
strictly positive.
Characterizing which Fenchel-Young losses have a margin is an important question
which we address next.
Conditions for existence of margin.
To accomplish our goal, we need to characterize the gradient mappings ∂(−𝖧)\partial(-\mathsf{H}) and ∇(−𝖧)∗\nabla(-\mathsf{H})^{*} associated with generalized entropies
(note that ∂(−𝖧)\partial(-\mathsf{H})
is never single-valued:
if 𝜽\bm{\theta} is in ∂(−𝖧)​(𝒑)\partial(-\mathsf{H})(\bm{p}), then so is 𝜽+c​𝟏\bm{\theta}+c\mathbf{1}, for any
constant c∈ℝc\in\mathbb{R}).
Of particular importance is the subdifferential set
∂(−𝖧)​(𝒆k)\partial(-\mathsf{H})(\bm{e}_{k}).
The next proposition, whose proof we defer to
§B.4, uses this set to provide a necessary and sufficient condition for the existence of a separation margin, along with a formula for computing it.
Proposition 9.
Let 𝖧\mathsf{H} satisfy A.1–A.3. Then:
1.
The loss L−𝖧L_{-\mathsf{H}} has a separation margin iff there is a m>0m>0 such that m​𝒆k∈∂(−𝖧)​(𝒆k)m\bm{e}_{k}\in\partial(-\mathsf{H})(\bm{e}_{k}).
2.
If the above holds, then the margin of L−𝖧L_{-\mathsf{H}} is given by the smallest
such mm or, equivalently,
margin⁡(L−𝖧)=sup𝒑∈△d𝖧⁡(𝒑)1−‖𝒑‖∞.\mathrm{margin}(L_{-\mathsf{H}})=\sup_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}\frac{\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})}{1-\|{\bm{p}}\|_{\infty}}.
(63)
Reassuringly, the first part confirms that
the logistic loss does not have a margin,
since ∂(−𝖧s)​(𝒆k)=∅\partial(-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}})(\bm{e}_{k})=\varnothing.
A second interesting fact is that the denominator of (63) is the generalized
entropy 𝖧∞n​(𝒑)\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{n}}_{\infty}({\bm{p}}) introduced in §4: the ∞\infty-norm entropy.
As Figure 4 suggests, this entropy provides an upper bound for convex losses with unit margin.
This provides some intuition to the formula (63), which seeks a
distribution 𝒑{\bm{p}} maximizing the entropy ratio between 𝖧⁡(𝒑)\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}}) and
𝖧∞n​(𝒑)\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{n}}_{\infty}({\bm{p}}).
Relationship between sparsity and margins.
The next result, proved in §B.5,
characterizes more precisely the image of ∇(−𝖧)∗\nabla(-\mathsf{H})^{*}.
In doing so, it establishes a key result in this paper:
a sufficient condition for the existence of a separation margin in L−𝖧L_{-\mathsf{H}} is the sparsity of the regularized prediction function y^−𝖧≡∇(−𝖧)∗\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}}\equiv\nabla(-\mathsf{H})^{*},
i.e., its ability to reach the entire simplex, including the boundary points. If 𝖧\mathsf{H} is uniformly separable, this is also a necessary condition.
Proposition 10.
Relationship between
margin losses and
sparse predictive probabilities
Let 𝖧\mathsf{H} satisfy A.1–A.3 and be
uniformly separable, i.e., 𝖧⁡(𝐩)=∑i=1dh⁡(pi)\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}h(p_{i}). Then the following statements are all equivalent:
1.
∂(−𝖧)​(𝒑)≠∅\partial(-\mathsf{H})({\bm{p}})\neq\varnothing for any 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d};
2.
The mapping ∇(−𝖧)∗\nabla(-\mathsf{H})^{*} covers the full simplex, i.e., ∇(−𝖧)∗​(ℝd)=△d\nabla(-\mathsf{H})^{*}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})=\triangle^{d};
3.
L−𝖧L_{-\mathsf{H}} has the separation margin property.
For a general 𝖧\mathsf{H} (not necessarily separable) satisfying A.1–A.3,
we have (1) ⇔\Leftrightarrow (2) ⇒\Rightarrow (3).
Let us reflect for a moment on the three conditions stated in Proposition 10.
The first two conditions involve the subdifferential and gradient of −𝖧-\mathsf{H} and its conjugate; the third condition is the margin property of L−𝖧L_{-\mathsf{H}}. To provide some intuition, consider the case where 𝖧\mathsf{H} is separable with 𝖧⁡(𝒑)=∑ih⁡(pi)\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})=\sum_{i}h(p_{i}) and hh is differentiable in (0,1)(0,1). Then, from the concavity of hh, its derivative h′h^{\prime} is decreasing, hence the first condition is met if limt=0+h′​(t)<∞\lim_{t=0^{+}}h^{\prime}(t)<\infty and limt=1−h′​(t)>−∞\lim_{t=1^{-}}h^{\prime}(t)>-\infty.
This is the case with Tsallis entropies for α>1\alpha>1, but not Shannon
entropy, since
h′​(t)=−1−log⁡th^{\prime}(t)=-1-\log t explodes at 00.
As stated in Definition 5,
functions whose gradient “explodes” in the boundary of their domain (hence
failing to meet the first condition in Proposition 10) are
called “essentially smooth” (Rockafellar, 1970).
For those functions, ∇(−𝖧)∗\nabla(-\mathsf{H})^{*} maps only to the relative interior of
△d\triangle^{d}, never attaining boundary points (Wainwright and Jordan, 2008); this is expressed in the second condition.
This prevents essentially smooth functions from generating a sparse 𝒚−𝖧≡∇(−𝖧)∗{\bm{y}}_{-\mathsf{H}}\equiv\nabla(-\mathsf{H})^{*} or (if they are separable) a
loss L−𝖧L_{-\mathsf{H}} with a margin, as asserted by the third condition.
Since Legendre-type functions (Definition
5)
are strictly convex and essentially smooth,
by Proposition 6, loss functions for which
the composite form L−𝖧(𝜽;𝒚)=B−𝖧(𝒚∥𝒚^−𝖧(𝜽))L_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=B_{-\mathsf{H}}({\bm{y}}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{\theta})) holds, which is the
case of the logistic loss but not of the sparsemax loss, do not enjoy a margin
and cannot induce a sparse probability distribution.
This is geometrically visible in Figure 4.
Margin computation.
For Fenchel-Young losses that have the separation margin property, Proposition 9 provided a formula for determining the margin.
While informative, formula (63) is not very practical,
as it involves a generally non-convex optimization problem.
The next proposition, proved in
§B.6, takes a step further and
provides a remarkably simple closed-form expression for generalized
entropies that are twice-differentiable.
To simplify notation, we denote by ∇j𝖧​(𝒑)≡(∇𝖧​(𝒑))j\nabla_{j}\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})\equiv(\nabla\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}}))_{j}
the jthj^{\text{th}} component of ∇𝖧​(𝒑)\nabla\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}}).
Proposition 11.
Assume 𝖧\mathsf{H} satisfies the conditions in Proposition 10
and is twice-differentiable on the simplex. Then, for arbitrary j≠kj\neq k:
margin⁡(L−𝖧)=∇j𝖧​(𝒆k)−∇k𝖧​(𝒆k).\mathrm{margin}(L_{-\mathsf{H}})={\nabla_{j}{\mathsf{H}}(\bm{e}_{k})}-{\nabla_{k}{\mathsf{H}}(\bm{e}_{k})}.
(64)
In particular, if 𝖧\mathsf{H} is separable, i.e., 𝖧⁡(𝐩)=∑i=1|𝒴|h⁡(pi)\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})=\sum_{i=1}^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}h(p_{i}), where h:[0,1]→ℝ+h:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+} is concave, twice differentiable,
with h⁡(0)=h⁡(1)=0h(0)=h(1)=0, then
margin(L−𝖧)=h′(0)−h′(1)=−∫01h′′(t)dt.\mathrm{margin}(L_{-\mathsf{H}})=h^{\prime}(0)-h^{\prime}(1)=-\hskip-0.86108pt\int_{0}^{1}\hskip-1.72218pth^{\prime\prime}(t)dt.
(65)
The compact formula (64) provides a geometric characterization of separable entropies and their margins:
(65) tells us that only the slopes of hh at the two extremities of [0,1][0,1] are relevant in determining the margin.
Example: case of Tsallis and norm entropies.
As seen in §4, Tsallis entropies are separable with h⁡(t)=(t−tα)/(α⁡(α−1))h(t)=(t-t^{\alpha})/(\alpha(\alpha-1)).
For α>1\alpha>1, h′​(t)=(1−α​tα−1)/(α⁡(α−1))h^{\prime}(t)=(1-\alpha t^{\alpha-1})/(\alpha(\alpha-1)), hence h′​(0)=1/(α⁡(α−1))h^{\prime}(0)=1/(\alpha(\alpha-1)) and
h′(1)=−1/αh^{\prime}(1)=-1/\alpha. Proposition 11 then yields
margin⁡(L−𝖧αt)=h′​(0)−h′​(1)=(α−1)−1.\mathrm{margin}(L_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{\alpha}})=h^{\prime}(0)-h^{\prime}(1)=(\alpha-1)^{-1}.
(66)
Norm entropies, while not separable, have gradient
∇𝖧qn​(𝒑)=−(𝒑/‖𝒑‖q)q−1\nabla\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{n}}_{q}({\bm{p}})=-(\nicefrac{{{\bm{p}}}}{{\|{\bm{p}}\|_{q}}})^{q-1},
giving ∇𝖧qn​(𝒆k)=−𝒆k\nabla\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{n}}_{q}(\bm{e}_{k})=-\bm{e}_{k}, so
margin⁡(𝖧qn)=∇j𝖧qn​(𝒆k)−∇k𝖧qn​(𝒆k)=1,\mathrm{margin}(\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{n}}_{q})=\nabla_{j}\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{n}}_{q}(\bm{e}_{k})-\nabla_{k}\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{n}}_{q}(\bm{e}_{k})=1,
(67)
as confirmed visually in Figure 4,
in the binary case.
6 Positive measure prediction with Fenchel-Young losses
In this section, we again restrict to classification and 𝒴={𝒆i}i=1d{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d} but now assume that 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)⊆𝖼𝗈𝗇𝖾(𝒴)=ℝ+d\dom(\Omega)\subseteq\cone({\mathcal{Y}})={\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{+}, where 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝖾(𝒴)\cone({\mathcal{Y}}) is the conic hull of 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}}.
In this case, the regularized prediction function (8) becomes
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)∈𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐦∈ℝ+d⟨𝜽,𝐦⟩−Ω⁡(𝐦),\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\in\argmax_{{\bm{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{+}}{{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{m}}\rangle}}-\Omega({\bm{m}}),
(68)
where 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} is again a vector of prediction scores
and 𝒎∈ℝ+d{\bm{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{+} can be interpreted as a discrete positive measure
(unnormalized probability distribution).
We first demonstrate how Fenchel-Young losses over positive measures allow to
recover one-vs-all reductions (§6.1), theoretically
justifying this popular scheme. We then give examples of loss
instantiations (§6.2).
6.1 Uniformly separable regularizers and one-vs-all loss functions
A particularly simple case is that of uniformly separable Ω\Omega, i.e.,
Ω⁡(𝒎)=∑j=1dϕ⁡(mj)+Iℝ+d​(𝒎)\Omega({\bm{m}})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\phi(m_{j})+I_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}({\bm{m}}), for some ϕ:ℝ+→ℝ\phi\colon{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}. In that case the regularized prediction
function can be computed in a coordinate-wise fashion:
(𝒚^Ω​(𝜽))j=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑m∈ℝ+m​θj−ϕ⁡(m).(\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}))_{j}=\argmax_{m\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}~m\theta_{j}-\phi(m).
(69)
As we shall later see, this simplified optimization problem often enjoys a
closed-form solution.
Intuitively, (𝒚^Ω​(𝜽))j(\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}))_{j}
can be interpreted as the “unnormalized probability” of class jj.
Likewise, the corresponding Fenchel-Young loss is separable over classes:
LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=Ω∗​(𝜽)+Ω⁡(𝒚)−⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩=∑j=1dϕ∗​(θj)+ϕ⁡(yj)−θj​yj=∑j=1dLϕ​(θj,yj),L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})+\Omega({\bm{y}})-{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\phi^{*}(\theta_{j})+\phi(y_{j})-\theta_{j}y_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}L_{\phi}(\theta_{j};y_{j}),
(70)
where yj∈{1,0}y_{j}\in\{1,0\} indicates membership to class jj.
The separability allows to train the model producing each θj\theta_{j} in an
embarrassingly parallel fashion.
Let us now consider the case ϕ⁡(p)=−𝖧⁡([p,1−p])\phi(p)=-\mathsf{H}([p,1-p]), where 𝖧\mathsf{H} is a
generalized entropy and ϕ\phi is restricted to [0,1][0,1]. From
§4.4, we know that ϕ∗​(s)=ϕ∗​(−s)+s\phi^{*}(s)=\phi^{*}(-s)+s for
all s∈ℝs\in{\mathbb{R}}. If
𝖧\mathsf{H} further satisfies assumption A.1, meaning that ϕ⁡(1)=ϕ⁡(0)=0\phi(1)=\phi(0)=0, then
we obtain
Lϕ​(s,1)=ϕ∗​(s)+ϕ⁡(1)−s=ϕ∗​(−s)andLϕ​(s,0)=ϕ∗​(s)+ϕ⁡(0)=ϕ∗​(s).L_{\phi}(s;1)=\phi^{*}(s)+\phi(1)-s=\phi^{*}(-s)\quad\text{and}\quad L_{\phi}(s;0)=\phi^{*}(s)+\phi(0)=\phi^{*}(s).
(71)
Combining the two, we obtain
LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=∑j=1dϕ∗​(−(2​yi−1)​θi),L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\phi^{*}(-(2y_{i}-1)\theta_{i}),
(72)
where we used that (2​yi−1)∈{+1,−1}(2y_{i}-1)\in\{+1,-1\}.
Fenchel-Young losses thus
recover classical one-vs-all loss functions.
Since Fenchel-Young losses satisfy LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=0⇔𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝒚L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=0\Leftrightarrow\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})={\bm{y}}, our framework provides a theoretical justification for
one-vs-all, a scheme that works well in practice (Rifkin and Klautau, 2004).
Further, our framework justifies using 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) as a measure of class
membership, as commonly implemented (with post-normalization) in software
packages (Pedregosa et al., 2011; Buitinck et al., 2013).
Finally, we point out that in the binary case, choosing 𝜽=[s,−s]\bm{\theta}=[s,-s], we
have the relationship
LΩ​(𝜽,𝒆1)=2​ϕ∗​(−s)andLΩ​(𝜽,𝒆2)=2​ϕ∗​(s).L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{1})=2\phi^{*}(-s)\quad\text{and}\quad L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{2})=2\phi^{*}(s).
(73)
Thus, we recover the same loss as in §4.4, up to a
constant 2 factor (i.e., learning over the simplex △d\triangle^{d} or over the
unit cube [0,1]d[0,1]^{d} is equivalent for binary classification).
6.2 Examples
Recall that Ω⁡(𝒎)=∑j=1dϕ⁡(mj)+Iℝ+d​(𝒎)\Omega({\bm{m}})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\phi(m_{j})+I_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}({\bm{m}}).
Choosing ϕ⁡(p)=12​p2\phi(p)=\frac{1}{2}p^{2} gives
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=[𝜽]+andLΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=∑i=1d12​[θi]+2+12​yi2−θi​yi.\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=[\bm{\theta}]_{+}\quad\text{and}\quad L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{2}[\theta_{i}]_{+}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}y_{i}^{2}-\theta_{i}y_{i}.
(74)
Choosing ϕ⁡(p)=−𝖧s​([p,1−p])+I[0,1]​(p)\phi(p)=-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}([p,1-p])+I_{[0,1]}(p) leads to
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝗌𝗂𝗀𝗆𝗈𝗂𝖽(𝜽)≔𝟏𝟏+exp⁡(−𝜽),andLΩ​(𝜽,𝐲)=∑i=1dlog⁡(1+exp⁡(−(2​yi−1)​θi))\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\sigmoid(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\frac{\bm{1}}{\bm{1}+\exp(-\bm{\theta})},\quad\text{and}\quad L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\log(1+\exp(-(2y_{i}-1)\theta_{i}))
(75)
the classical sigmoid function and the one-vs-all logistic function.
Note that −Ω(𝒑)=∑j𝖧s([pj,1−pj])=−∑jpjlogpj+(1−pj)log(1−pj)-\Omega({\bm{p}})=\sum_{j}\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}([p_{j},1-p_{j}])=-\sum_{j}p_{j}\log p_{j}+(1-p_{j})\log(1-p_{j}) is sometimes known as the Fermi-Dirac entropy
(Borwein and Lewis, 2010, Commentary of Section 3.3).
Choosing ϕ⁡(p)=−𝖧2t​([p,1−p])+I[0,1]​(p)=p2−p+I[0,1]​(p)\phi(p)=-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{2}([p,1-p])+I_{[0,1]}(p)=p^{2}-p+I_{[0,1]}(p) leads to
(𝒚^Ω​(𝜽))j=(ϕ∗)′​(sj)={0 if ​u≤−11 if ​u≥112​(u+1) o.w. ∀j∈[d].(\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}))_{j}=(\phi^{*})^{\prime}(s_{j})=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }u\leq-1\\
1&\mbox{ if }u\geq 1\\
\frac{1}{2}(u+1)&\mbox{ o.w. }\end{cases}\quad\forall j\in[d].
(76)
We can think of this function as a “sparse sigmoid”.
7 Structured prediction with Fenchel-Young losses
In this section, we now turn to prediction over the convex hull of a set 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}}
of structured objects (sequences, trees, assignments, etc.), represented as
dd-dimensional vectors, i.e. 𝒴⊆ℝd{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.
In this case, the regularized prediction function (8) becomes
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)⟨𝝁,𝜽⟩−Ω⁡(𝝁).\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\argmax_{\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}})}{\langle\bm{\mu},\bm{\theta}\rangle}-\Omega(\bm{\mu}).
(77)
Typically, |𝒴|{|{\mathcal{Y}}|} will be exponential in dd, i.e., d≪|𝒴|d\ll{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}.
Because 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=∑𝒚∈𝒴p⁡(𝒚)​𝒚=𝔼𝒑​[Y]\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}p({\bm{y}}){\bm{y}}={\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y] for some 𝒑∈△|𝒴|{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}, 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) can be interpreted as the expectation under
some (not necessarily unique) underlying distribution.
We first discuss the concepts of probability distribution regularization
(§7.1) and mean regularization (§7.2), and
the implications in terms of computational tractability and identifiability
(note that in the unstructured setting, probability distribution
and mean regularizations coincide).
We then give several examples of polytopes and show how a Fenchel-Young loss
can seamlessly be constructed over them given access to regularized prediction
functions (§7.3).
Finally, we extend the notion of separation margin to the structured setting
(§7.4).
7.1 Distribution regularization, marginal inference and structured sparsemax
In this section, we discuss the concept of probability distribution
regularization. Our treatment follows closely the variational formulations
of exponential families (Barndorff-Nielsen, 1978; Wainwright and Jordan, 2008) and
generalized exponential families (Grünwald and Dawid, 2004; Frongillo and Reid, 2014) but
adopts the novel viewpoint of regularized prediction functions.
We discuss two instances of that framework: the
structured counterpart of the softmax, marginal inference,
and a new structured counterpart of the sparsemax, structured sparsemax.
Let us start with a probability-space regularized prediction function
𝒚^−𝖧​(𝒔𝜽)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐩∈△|𝒴|⟨𝐩,𝐬𝜽⟩+𝖧⁡(𝐩),\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}})=\argmax_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}}{\langle{\bm{p}},\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}}\rangle}+\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}}),
(78)
where 𝖧\mathsf{H} is a generalized entropy and 𝒔𝜽≔(⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩)𝒚∈𝒴∈ℝ|𝒴|\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}}\coloneqq({\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle})_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}
is a vector that contains the scores of all possible structures.
Note that in the unstructured setting, where 𝒴={𝒆1,…,𝒆d}{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\bm{e}_{1},\dots,\bm{e}_{d}\},
we have 𝒔𝜽=𝜽\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}}=\bm{\theta} and hence the distinction between 𝜽\bm{\theta} and 𝒔𝜽\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}}
is not necessary.
In the structured prediction setting, however,
the above optimization problem is defined over a space of size |𝒴|≫d{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}\gg d.
The regularized prediction function outputs a probability distribution over
structures and from Danskin’s theorem, we have
𝒚^−𝖧​(𝒔𝜽)=∇𝒔𝜽(−𝖧)∗​(𝒔𝜽)=𝒑⋆∈△|𝒴|.\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}}(\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}})=\nabla_{\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}}}(-\mathsf{H})^{*}(\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}})={\bm{p}}^{\star}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}.
(79)
Using the chain rule, differentiating w.r.t. 𝜽\bm{\theta} instead of 𝒔𝜽\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}} gives
∇𝜽(−𝖧)∗​(𝒔𝜽)=𝔼𝒑⋆​[Y]∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴).\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}(-\mathsf{H})^{*}(\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}})={\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{p}}^{\star}}[Y]\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}}).
(80)
Therefore, the gradient w.r.t. 𝜽\bm{\theta} corresponds to
the mean under 𝒑⋆{\bm{p}}^{\star}. This can be equivalently expressed under our
framework by defining a
regularization function directly in mean space. Let us define the regularization
function Ω\Omega over 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}) as
−Ω⁡(𝝁)≔max𝒑∈△|𝒴|⁡𝖧⁡(𝒑)s.t.𝔼𝒑​[Y]=𝝁.-\Omega(\bm{\mu})\coloneqq\max_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}}\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})\quad\text{s.t.}\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]=\bm{\mu}.
(81)
That is, among all distributions
satisfying the first-moment matching
condition 𝔼𝒑​[Y]=𝝁{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]=\bm{\mu},
we seek the distribution 𝒑⋆{\bm{p}}^{\star} with maximum (generalized) entropy. This
allows to make the underlying distribution unique and identifiable.
With this choice of Ω\Omega, simple calculations show that the corresponding
regularized prediction function is precisely the mean under that distribution:
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=∇Ω∗​(𝜽)=𝔼𝒑⋆​[Y]∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴).\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})={\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{p}}^{\star}}[Y]\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}}).
(82)
Similar results hold for higher order moments: If Ω∗\Omega^{*} is
twice-differentiable, the Hessian corresponds to the second moment under
𝒑⋆{\bm{p}}^{\star}:
cov𝒑⋆​[Y]=∇2Ω∗​(𝜽).\textnormal{cov}_{{\bm{p}}^{\star}}[Y]=\nabla^{2}\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}).
(83)
The relation between these various maps
is summarized in Figure 5.
Figure 5: Summary of maps between spaces.
We define Ω\Omega over 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}) using a generalized maximum entropy
principle:
−Ω⁡(𝝁)≔max𝒑∈△|𝒴|⁡𝖧⁡(𝒑)​ s.t. ​𝔼𝒑​[Y]=𝝁-\Omega(\bm{\mu})\coloneqq\max_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}}\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})\text{ s.t. }{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]=\bm{\mu}
(cf. §7.1).
We also define 𝒔𝜽≔(⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩)𝒚∈𝒴∈ℝ|𝒴|\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}}\coloneqq({\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle})_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|},
the vector that contains the scores of all possible structures, a linear map
from ℝd{\mathbb{R}}^{d} to ℝ|𝒴|{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}.
Note that the diagram is not necessarily commutative.
Marginal inference.
A particular case of (81) is
−Ω⁡(𝝁)=max𝒑∈△|𝒴|⁡𝖧s​(𝒑)s.t.𝔼𝒑​[Y]=𝝁,-\Omega(\bm{\mu})=\max_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}}\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}({\bm{p}})\quad\text{s.t.}\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]=\bm{\mu},
(84)
where 𝖧s\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}} is the Shannon entropy.
The distribution achieving
maximum Shannon entropy, which is unique, is the Gibbs distribution
p⁡(𝒚,𝜽)∝exp⁡(⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩)p({\bm{y}};\bm{\theta})\propto\exp({\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}).
As shown in Table 1,
the resulting loss LΩL_{\Omega} is
the CRF loss (Lafferty et al., 2001)
and the resulting regularized prediction function 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega} is known as
marginal inference in the literature (Wainwright and Jordan, 2008):
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝗆𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗂𝗇𝖺𝗅𝗌(𝜽)≔∑𝐲∈𝒴exp⁡(⟨𝜽,𝐲⟩)​𝐲/Z⁡(𝜽),\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\marginals(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\exp({\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}){\bm{y}}\big/Z(\bm{\theta}),
(85)
where Z⁡(𝜽)≔∑𝒚∈𝒴exp⁡(⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩)Z(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\exp({\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}) is the partition
function (normalization constant) of the Gibbs distribution.
Although marginal inference is intractable in general, we will see in
§7.3 that it can
be computed exactly and efficiently for specific polytopes.
The conjugate of Ω⁡(𝝁)\Omega(\bm{\mu}), Ω∗​(𝜽)\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}), corresponds to the log
partition function: Ω∗​(𝜽)=log⁡Z⁡(𝜽)\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})=\log Z(\bm{\theta}).
Structured sparsemax.
From the above perspective, it is tempting to define a sparsemax counterpart of
the CRF loss and of marginal inference by replacing Shannon’s entropy 𝖧s\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}
with the Gini index 𝖧2t\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{2} (see (53) for a definition) in
(84):
−Ω⁡(𝝁)=max𝒑∈△|𝒴|⁡𝖧2t​(𝒑)s.t.𝔼𝒑​[Y]=𝝁.-\Omega(\bm{\mu})=\max_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}}\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{2}({\bm{p}})\quad\text{s.t.}\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]=\bm{\mu}.
(86)
This is equivalent to seeking a distribution 𝒑⋆{\bm{p}}^{\star} with minimum squared norm
Ω⁡(𝝁)+12=min𝒑∈△|𝒴|⁡12​‖𝒑‖2s.t.𝔼𝒑​[Y]=𝝁.\Omega(\bm{\mu})+\frac{1}{2}=\min_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}}\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{p}}\|^{2}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y]=\bm{\mu}.
(87)
The corresponding 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) is then 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝔼𝒑⋆​[Y]\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})={\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{p}}^{\star}}[Y].
Recalling that 𝒑⋆=𝒚^−𝖧2t​(𝒔𝜽){\bm{p}}^{\star}=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{2}}(\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}}),
a naive approach would be to first compute the score vector 𝒔𝜽=(⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩)𝒚∈𝒴∈ℝ|𝒴|\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}}=({\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle})_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|} and then to
project that vector on the simplex to obtain 𝒑⋆{\bm{p}}^{\star}.
Unfortunately, 𝒔𝜽\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}} could be exponentially large and 𝒑⋆{\bm{p}}^{\star} could be
arbitrarily dense in the worst case, making that approach intractable in
general.
As also noted by Pillutla et al. (2018), we can approximate the problem by further
imposing an upper-bound on the sparsity of the distribution, ‖𝒑‖0≤k\|{\bm{p}}\|_{0}\leq k.
Since the optimal kk-sparse distribution solely depends on the top-kk elements
of 𝒔𝜽\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}} (Kyrillidis et al., 2013), we can first use a kk-best oracle to
retrieve the top-kk elements of 𝒔𝜽\bm{s}_{\bm{\theta}} and project them onto the simplex. A
disadvantage of that approach is that kk-best oracles are usually more complex
than MAP oracles and kk could be arbitrarily large in order to guarantee an
exact solution to the original problem. For shortest path problems over a
directed acyclic graph, an alternative approximation is to smooth the maximum
operator directly in the dynamic programming recursion
(Mensch and Blondel, 2018).
7.2 Mean regularization and SparseMAP
Marginal inference is computationally tractable only for certain
polytopes and structured sparsemax requires approximations, even for
polytopes for which exact marginal inference is available. In this section,
we discuss an alternative approach which only requires access to a MAP oracle,
broadening the set of applicable polytopes.
The key idea is mean regularization: We directly regularize the mean
𝝁\bm{\mu} rather than the distribution 𝒑{\bm{p}}.
The mean regularization counterpart of marginal inference is
𝒚^−𝖧s(𝜽)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩+𝖧s(𝝁)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)𝖪𝖫(𝝁∥e𝜽−𝟏).\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}}(\bm{\theta})=\argmax_{\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}})}{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}+\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}(\bm{\mu})=\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}})}\mathsf{KL}(\bm{\mu}\|e^{\bm{\theta}-\bm{1}}).
(88)
It has been used for specific convex polytopes, most importantly in the optimal
transport literature (Cuturi, 2013; Peyré and Cuturi, 2017) but also for learning to
predict permutation matrices (Helmbold and Warmuth, 2009) or permutations
(Yasutake et al., 2011; Ailon et al., 2016).
The mean regularization counterpart of sparsemax is known as SparseMAP
(Niculae et al., 2018):
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝖲𝗉𝖺𝗋𝗌𝖾𝖬𝖠𝖯(𝜽)≔𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩−12​‖𝝁‖2=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)‖𝝁−𝜽‖2.\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\sparsemap(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\argmax_{\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}})}{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}-\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mu}\|^{2}=\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}})}\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\theta}\|^{2}.
(89)
The main advantage of mean regularization is computational.
Indeed, computing 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) now simply involves a dd-dimensional
optimization problem instead of a |𝒴||{\mathcal{Y}}|-dimensional one and
can be cast as a Bregman projection onto 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}})(§3.2). For specific polytopes, that projection
can often be computed
directly (§7.3). More generally, it can always be
computed to arbitrary precision given access to a MAP oracle
𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐲∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)⟨𝜽,𝐲⟩=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐲∈𝒴⟨𝜽,𝐲⟩≕𝖬𝖠𝖯(𝜽),\argmax_{{\bm{y}}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}})}{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}=\argmax_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}\eqqcolon\map(\bm{\theta}),
(90)
thanks to conditional gradient algorithms (§8.3).
Since MAP inference is a cornerstone of structured prediction,
efficient algorithms have been developed for many kinds of structures
(§7.3).
In addition, as conditional gradient algorithms maintain a convex combination of
vertices, they can also return a (not necessarily unique) distribution 𝒑∈△|𝒴|{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|} such that 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝔼𝒑​[Y]\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})={\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{p}}[Y].
From Carathéodory’s theorem, the support of 𝒑{\bm{p}}
contains at most d≪|𝒴|d\ll|{\mathcal{Y}}| elements. This ensures that 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) can
be written as a “small” number of elementary structures.
The price to pay for this computational tractability is that the underlying
distribution 𝒑{\bm{p}} is not necessarily unique.
7.3 Examples
Deriving loss functions for structured outputs can be challenging. In this
section, we give several examples of polytopes 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}) for which
efficient computational oracles (MAP, marginal inference, projection) are
available, thus allowing to obtain a Fenchel-Young loss for learning over these
polytopes. A summary is given in Table 2.
Table 2: Examples of convex polytopes and computational cost of the three main
computational oracles: MAP, marginal inference and projection (in the Euclidean
distance sense or more generally in the Bregman divergence sense).
For polytopes for which
direct marginal inference or projection algorithms are not available, we can
always use conditional gradient algorithms to compute an optimal solution to
arbitrary precision — see §8.3.
