Sequences.
We wish to tag a sequence (𝒙1,…,𝒙n)({\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{n}) of vectors in ℝp{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
(e.g., word representations) with the most likely output sequence
(e.g., entity tags) 𝒔=(s1,…,sn)∈[m]n\bm{s}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\in[m]^{n}. It is
convenient to represent each sequence 𝒔\bm{s} as a n×m×mn\times m\times m binary
tensor 𝒚∈𝒴\bm{y}\in{\mathcal{Y}}, such that yt,i,j=1y_{t,i,j}=1 if 𝒚{\bm{y}} transitions from node
jj to node ii on time tt, and 00 otherwise. The potentials 𝜽\bm{\theta} can
similarly be organized as a n×m×mn\times m\times m real tensor, such that
θt,i,j=ϕt​(𝒙t,i,j)\theta_{t,i,j}=\phi_{t}({\bm{x}}_{t},i,j), where ϕt\phi_{t} is a potential function.
Using the above binary tensor representation, the Frobenius inner product
⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle is equal to ∑t=1nϕt​(𝒙t,st,st−1)\sum_{t=1}^{n}\phi_{t}({\bm{x}}_{t},s_{t},s_{t-1}), the cumulated score of 𝒔\bm{s}.
MAP inference, 𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐲∈𝒴⟨𝐲,𝜽⟩\argmax_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}{\langle{\bm{y}},\bm{\theta}\rangle}, seeks the
highest-scoring sequence and can be computed using
Viterbi’s algorithm (Viterbi, 1967) in O⁡(n​m2)O(nm^{2}) time. Marginal
inference can be computed in the same cost using the forward-backward algorithm
(Baum and Petrie, 1966) or equivalently using backpropagation
(Eisner, 2016; Mensch and Blondel, 2018).
When Ω\Omega is defined over 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}) as in (84),
the Fenchel-Young loss corresponds to a
linear-chain conditional random field loss (Lafferty et al., 2001). In recent
years, this loss has been used to train various end-to-end natural language
pipelines based on neural networks (Collobert et al., 2011; Lample et al., 2016).
Alignments.
Let 𝑨∈ℝm×p\bm{A}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times p} and 𝑩∈ℝn×p\bm{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p} be two
time-series of lengths mm and nn, respectively. We denote by 𝒂i∈ℝp\bm{a}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p} and 𝒃j∈ℝp\bm{b}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p} their ithi^{\text{th}} and jthj^{\text{th}}
observations. Our goal is to find an alignment between 𝑨\bm{A} and 𝑩\bm{B},
matching their observations.
We define 𝜽\bm{\theta} as a m×nm\times n matrix, such that
θi,j\theta_{i,j} is the similarity between observations 𝒂i\bm{a}_{i} and
𝒃j\bm{b}_{j}.
Likewise, we represent an alignment 𝒚{\bm{y}} as a m×nm\times n binary matrix, such
that yi,j=1y_{i,j}=1 if 𝒂i\bm{a}_{i} is aligned with 𝒃j\bm{b}_{j}, and 00 otherwise.
We write 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}} the set of all monotonic alignment matrices, such that the path
that connects the upper-left (1,1)(1,1) matrix entry to the lower-right (m,n)(m,n)
one uses only ↓,→,↘\downarrow,\rightarrow,\searrow moves.
In this setting, MAP inference, 𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐲∈𝒴⟨𝐲,𝜽⟩\argmax_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}{\langle{\bm{y}},\bm{\theta}\rangle}, corresponds
to seeking the maximal similarity (or equivalently, minimal cost) alignment
between the two time-series. It can be computed in O⁡(m​n)O(mn) time using dynamic
time warping, DTW (Sakoe and Chiba, 1978). Marginal inference can be computed
in the same cost by backpropagation, if we replace
the hard minimum with a soft one in the DTW recursion. This algorithm is known
as soft-DTW (Cuturi and Blondel, 2017; Mensch and Blondel, 2018).
Structured SVMs were combined with DTW to learn to predict music-to-score
alignments (Garreau et al., 2014). Our framework easily enables extensions
of this work, such as replacing DTW with soft-DTW, which
amounts to introducing entropic regularization w.r.t. the probability
distribution over alignments.
Spanning trees.
When 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}} is the set of possible directed spanning trees (arborescences) of a
complete graph 𝒢\mathcal{G} with nn vertices, the convex hull 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)⊂ℝn⁡(n−1)\conv({\mathcal{Y}})\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n(n-1)} is known as the
arborescence polytope (Martins et al., 2009b) (each 𝒚∈𝒴{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}} is a binary vector which indicates which arcs belong to the arborescence). MAP inference may be performed by
maximal arborescence algorithms (Chu and Liu, 1965; Edmonds, 1967) in O⁡(n2)O(n^{2}) time
(Tarjan, 1977), and the Matrix-Tree theorem (Kirchhoff, 1847) provides an
O⁡(n3)O(n^{3}) marginal inference algorithm
(Koo et al., 2007; Smith and Smith, 2007).
Spanning tree structures are often used in natural language processing for
(non-projective) dependency parsing, with graph edges corresponding to dependencies
between the words in a sentence.
(McDonald et al., 2005; Kiperwasser and Goldberg, 2016).
Permutations.
We view ranking as a structured prediction problem.
Let 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}} be the set of dd-permutations of a prescribed vector 𝒘∈ℝd{\bm{w}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},
i.e., 𝒴={[wπ1,…,wπd]∈ℝd:𝝅∈Π}{\mathcal{Y}}=\{[w_{\pi_{1}},\dots,w_{\pi_{d}}]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\colon\bm{\pi}\in\Pi\},
where Π\Pi denotes the permutations of (1,…,d)(1,\dots,d).
We assume without loss of generality that 𝒘{\bm{w}} is sorted in descending order,
i.e., w1≥⋯≥wdw_{1}\geq\dots\geq w_{d}.
MAP inference seeks the permutation of 𝒘{\bm{w}} whose inner product
with 𝜽\bm{\theta} is maximized:
max𝒚∈𝒴⁡⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩=max⁡∑i=1d𝒚∈𝒴⁡θi​yi=max⁡∑i=1d𝝅∈Π⁡θi​wπi=max⁡∑i=1d𝝅∈Π⁡θπi​wi.\max_{\bm{y}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}=\max_{\bm{y}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\theta_{i}y_{i}=\max_{\bm{\pi}\in\Pi}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\theta_{i}w_{\pi_{i}}=\max_{\bm{\pi}\in\Pi}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\theta_{\pi_{i}}w_{i}.
(91)
Since 𝒘{\bm{w}} is assumed sorted, an optimal solution 𝝅⋆\bm{\pi}^{\star} of the last
optimization problem is a permutation sorting 𝜽\bm{\theta} in descending order.
The function 𝜽↦∑i=1dθπi⋆​wi\bm{\theta}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{d}\theta_{\pi^{\star}_{i}}w_{i} is called
ordered weighted averaging (OWA) operator (Yager, 1988) and includes the
mean and max operator as special cases. MAP
inference over 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}} can be seen as the variational formulation of the OWA
operator. An optimal solution
𝒚⋆{\bm{y}}^{\star} is simply 𝒘{\bm{w}} sorted using the inverse permutation of 𝝅⋆\bm{\pi}^{\star}.
The overall computational cost is therefore O⁡(d​log⁡d)O(d\log d), for sorting 𝜽\bm{\theta}.
The convex hull of 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}}, 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}), is known as the permutahedron
when 𝒘=[d,…,1]{\bm{w}}=[d,\dots,1]. For arbitrary 𝒘{\bm{w}}, we follow
Lim and Wright (2016) and call 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}) the permutahedron induced
by 𝒘{\bm{w}}.
Its vertices correspond to permutations of 𝒘{\bm{w}}.
We can define mean-regularized ranking prediction functions 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) if we
choose 𝒘{\bm{w}} such that each wiw_{i} represents the “preference” of being in
position ii.
Intuitively, the score vector 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} should be such that each θi\theta_{i}
is the score of instance ii (e.g., a document or a label).
We give several examples below.
•
Choosing 𝒘=[1,0,…,0]{\bm{w}}=[1,0,\dots,0], 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}) is equal to
△d\triangle^{d}, the probability simplex. We thus recover probabilistic
classification as a natural special case.
•
Choosing 𝒘=1k[1,…,1⏟k times,0,…,0]{\bm{w}}=\frac{1}{k}[\underbrace{1,\dots,1}_{k\text{
times}},0,\dots,0], 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}) is equal to {𝒑∈△d:‖𝒑‖∞≤1k}\{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}\colon\|{\bm{p}}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{k}\}, sometimes referred to as the
capped probability simplex
(Warmuth and Kuzmin, 2008; Lim and Wright, 2016). This setting corresponds
to predicting kk-subsets.
•
Choosing 𝒘=2d⁡(d+1)​[d,d−1,…,1]{\bm{w}}=\frac{2}{d(d+1)}[d,d-1,\dots,1] corresponds to
predicting full rankings.
•
Choosing 𝒘=2k⁡(k+1)​[k,k−1,…,1,0,…,0⏟d−k−1​ times]{\bm{w}}=\frac{2}{k(k+1)}[k,k-1,\dots,1,\underbrace{0,\dots,0}_{d-k-1\text{ times}}]
corresponds to predicting partial rankings.
The corresponding polytopes are illustrated in Figure 6.
In all the examples above, 𝒘∈△d{\bm{w}}\in\triangle^{d}, implying 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)⊆△d\conv({\mathcal{Y}})\subseteq\triangle^{d}. Therefore, 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) outputs a probability
distribution.
Figure 6: Examples of instances of permutahedron induced by w{\bm{w}}.
Round circles indicate vertices of the permutahedron, permutations of 𝒘{\bm{w}}.
Choosing 𝒘=[1,0,0]{\bm{w}}=[1,0,0] recovers the probability simplex (red) while choosing
𝒘=[12,12,0]{\bm{w}}=[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},0] recovers the capped probability simplex
(blue). The other two instances are obtained by 𝒘=[23,13,0]{\bm{w}}=[\frac{2}{3},\frac{1}{3},0] (gray) and
𝒘=[12,13,16]{\bm{w}}=[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{6}] (green). Euclidean projection onto
these polytopes can be cast as isotonic regression. More generally, Bregman
projection reduces to isotonic optimization.
As discussed in §3.2, computing the regularized
prediction function 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) is equivalent
to a Bregman projection when Ω=Ψ+I𝒞\Omega=\Psi+I_{\mathcal{C}}, where Ψ\Psi is Legendre type.
The Euclidean projection onto 𝒞=𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴){\mathcal{C}}=\conv({\mathcal{Y}}) reduces to isotonic regression
(Zeng and Figueiredo, 2015; Negrinho and Martins, 2014). The computational cost is O⁡(d​log⁡d)O(d\log d). More
generally, Bregman projections reduce to isotonic optimization
(Lim and Wright, 2016). This provides a unified way to compute
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) efficiently, regardless of 𝒘{\bm{w}}.
The generated Fenchel-Young loss LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}), is illustrated in Figure
7 for various choices of Ω\Omega. When Ω=0\Omega=0, as
expected, the loss is zero as long as the
predicted ranking is correct. Note that in order to define a meaningful loss,
it is necessary that 𝒚∈𝒴{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}} or more generally 𝒚∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴){\bm{y}}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}}). That
is, 𝒚{\bm{y}} should belong to the convex hull of the permutations of 𝒘{\bm{w}}.
Permutahedra have been used to derive online learning to rank algorithms
(Yasutake et al., 2011; Ailon et al., 2016) but it is not obvious how to extract a loss
from these works.
Ordered weighted averaging (OWA) operators have been used to define related
top-kk multiclass losses (Usunier et al., 2009; Lapin et al., 2015) but without
identifying the connection with permutahedra.
Our construction follows directly from the general Fenchel-Young loss framework
and provides a novel geometric perspective.
Figure 7: Ranking losses generated by restricting 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega) to the
permutahedron induced by 𝒘=[3,2,1]{\bm{w}}=[3,2,1]. We set the ground-truth 𝒚=[2,1,3]{\bm{y}}=[2,1,3], i.e., a permutation of 𝒘{\bm{w}}. We set 𝜽=𝒚\bm{\theta}={\bm{y}} and inspect how the loss
changes when varying each θi\theta_{i}.
When Ω=0\Omega=0, the loss is zero as expected when θ1∈[1,3]\theta_{1}\in[1,3],
θ2∈(−∞,2]\theta_{2}\in(-\infty,2] and θ3∈[2,∞)\theta_{3}\in[2,\infty), since the ground-truth
ranking is still correctly predicted in these intervals.
When Ω=12∥⋅∥2\Omega=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|^{2} and Ω=−𝖧s\Omega=-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{s}}, we obtain a smooth
approximation. Although not done here for clarity, if 𝒘{\bm{w}} is normalized
such that 𝒘∈△d{\bm{w}}\in\triangle^{d}, then 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)∈△d\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\in\triangle^{d} as well.
Permutation matrices.
Let 𝒴⊂{0,1}n×n{\mathcal{Y}}\subset\{0,1\}^{n\times n} be the set of n×nn\times n permutation
matrices.
MAP inference is the solution of the linear assignment problem
max𝒚∈𝒴⁡⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩=max⁡∑i=1n𝝅∈Π⁡θi,πi,\max_{\bm{y}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}=\max_{\bm{\pi}\in\Pi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta_{i,\pi_{i}},
(92)
where Π\Pi denotes the permutations of (1,…,n)(1,\dots,n).
The problem can be solved exactly in O⁡(n3)O(n^{3}) time using the Hungarian
algorithm (Kuhn, 1955) or the Jonker-Volgenant algorithm (Jonker and Volgenant, 1987).
Noticeably, marginal inference is known to be #P-complete
(Valiant, 1979; Taskar, 2004, Section 3.5). This makes it an open
problem how to solve marginal inference for this polytope.
In contrast, projections on
the convex hull of 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}},
the set of doubly stochastic matrices known as Birkhoff polytope
(Birkhoff, 1946), can be computed efficiently. Since the
Birkhoff polytope is a special case of transportation polytope, we can leverage
algorithms from the optimal transport
literature to compute the mean-regularized prediction function.
When Ω⁡(𝝁)=⟨𝝁,log⁡𝝁⟩\Omega(\bm{\mu})={\langle\bm{\mu},\log\bm{\mu}\rangle}, 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) can be computed
using the Sinkhorn algorithm (Sinkhorn and Knopp, 1967; Cuturi, 2013). For other
regularizers, we can use Dykstra’s algorithm (Dessein et al., 2016) or dual
approaches (Blondel et al., 2018). The cost of obtaining an
ϵ\epsilon-approximate solution is typically O⁡(n2/ϵ)O(n^{2}/\epsilon).
The Birkhoff polytope has been used to define continuous relaxations of
non-convex ranking losses (Adams and Zemel, 2011). In contrast, the Fenchel-Young loss
over the Birkhoff polytope (new to our knowledge) is convex
by construction.
Although working with permutation matrices is more computationally expensive
than working with permutations, it brings different modeling power, since it
allows to take into account similarity between instances (e.g., text documents)
through the similarity matrix 𝜽\bm{\theta}. It also enables other applications, such as
learning to match.
7.4 Structured separation margins
We end this section by extending some of the results in §5 for the structured prediction case, showing that there is also a relation between structured entropies and margins.
In the sequel, we assume that structured outputs are contained in a sphere of radius rr, i.e., ‖𝒚‖=r\|{\bm{y}}\|=r for any 𝒚∈𝒴{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}.
This holds for all the examples above (sequences, alignments, spanning trees, permutations of a given vector, and permutation matrices). In particular, it holds whenever outputs are represented as binary vectors with a constant number of entries set to 1; this includes overcomplete parametrizations of discrete graphical models
(Wainwright and Jordan, 2008).
Definition 12.
Structured separation margin
Let L⁡(𝛉,𝐲)L(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}) be a loss function over ℝd×𝒴{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{Y}}.
We say that LL has
the structured separation margin property if there exists m>0m>0 such that, for any 𝐲∈𝒴{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}:
⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩≥max𝒚′∈𝒴⁡(⟨𝜽,𝒚′⟩+m2​‖𝒚−𝒚′‖2)⇒L⁡(𝜽,𝒚)=0.{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}\geq\max_{{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\left({\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}+\frac{m}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\|^{2}\right)\quad\Rightarrow\quad L(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=0.
(93)
The smallest possible mm that satisfies (93) is called
the margin of LL, denoted margin⁡(L)\mathrm{margin}(L).
Note that this definition generalizes the unstructured case (Definition 8), which is recovered when 𝒴={𝒆i}i=1d{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}.
Note also that, when outputs are represented as binary vectors,
the term ‖𝒚−𝒚′‖2\|{\bm{y}}-{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\|^{2} is a Hamming distance, which counts how many bits need to be flipped to transform 𝒚′{\bm{y}}^{\prime} into 𝒚{\bm{y}}.
The most famous example of a loss with a structured separation margin in the structured hinge loss used in structured support vector machines (Taskar, 2004; Tsochantaridis et al., 2005).
The next proposition extends Proposition 9. We defer its proof to
§B.7.
Proposition 13.
Assume Ω\Omega is convex and 𝒴{\mathcal{Y}} is contained in a sphere of radius rr. Then:
1.
The loss LΩL_{\Omega} has a structured separation margin iff there is a m>0m>0 such that, for any 𝒚∈𝒴{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}, m​𝒚∈∂Ω⁡(𝒚)m{\bm{y}}\in\partial\Omega({\bm{y}}).
2.
If the above holds, then the margin of LΩL_{\Omega} is given by the smallest
such mm or, equivalently,
margin⁡(LΩ)=sup𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴),𝐲∈𝒴Ω⁡(𝒚)−Ω⁡(𝝁)r2−⟨𝒚,𝝁⟩.\mathrm{margin}(L_{\Omega})=\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}}),\,{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\frac{\Omega({\bm{y}})-\Omega(\bm{\mu})}{r^{2}-{\langle{\bm{y}},\bm{\mu}\rangle}}.
(94)
Unit margin of the SparseMAP loss.
We can invoke Proposition 13 to show that the SparseMAP loss of Niculae et al. (2018) has a structured margin of 11, a novel result that follows directly from our construction.
Indeed, from (94), we have that, for Ω⁡(𝝁)=12​‖𝝁‖2\Omega(\bm{\mu})=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mu}\|^{2}:
margin⁡(LΩ)=sup𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴),𝐲∈𝒴12​r2−12​‖𝝁‖2r2−⟨𝒚,𝝁⟩=1−inf𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴),𝐲∈𝒴12​‖𝒚−𝝁‖2⟨𝒚,𝒚−𝝁⟩≤1,\mathrm{margin}(L_{\Omega})=\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}}),\,{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\frac{\frac{1}{2}r^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mu}\|^{2}}{r^{2}-{\langle{\bm{y}},\bm{\mu}\rangle}}=1-\inf_{\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}}),\,{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\frac{\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-\bm{\mu}\|^{2}}{{\langle{\bm{y}},{\bm{y}}-\bm{\mu}\rangle}}\leq 1,
(95)
where the last inequality follows from the fact that both the numerator and denominator in the second term are non-negative, the latter due to the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality.
We now show that, for any 𝒚∈𝒴{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}, we have inf𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)12​‖𝒚−𝝁‖2⟨𝒚,𝒚−𝝁⟩=0\inf_{\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}})}\frac{\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-\bm{\mu}\|^{2}}{{\langle{\bm{y}},{\bm{y}}-\bm{\mu}\rangle}}=0. Choosing 𝝁=t​𝒚′+(1−t)​𝒚\bm{\mu}=t{\bm{y}}^{\prime}+(1-t){\bm{y}}, for an arbitrary 𝒚′∈𝒴∖{𝒚}{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}\setminus\{{\bm{y}}\}, and letting t→0+t\rightarrow 0^{+}, we obtain 12​‖𝒚−𝝁‖2⟨𝒚,𝒚−𝝁⟩=12​t​‖𝒚−𝒚′‖2⟨𝒚,𝒚−𝒚′⟩→0\frac{\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-\bm{\mu}\|^{2}}{{\langle{\bm{y}},{\bm{y}}-\bm{\mu}\rangle}}=\frac{\frac{1}{2}t\|{\bm{y}}-{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\|^{2}}{{\langle{\bm{y}},{\bm{y}}-{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}}\rightarrow 0.
8 Algorithms for learning with Fenchel-Young losses
In this section, we present generic primal (§8.1) and
dual (§8.2) algorithms for training predictive models with
a Fenchel-Young loss LΩL_{\Omega} for arbitrary Ω\Omega. In doing so, we
obtain unified algorithms for training models with a wealth of existing and new
loss functions.
We then discuss algorithms for computing regularized prediction functions
(§8.3) and proximity operators
(§8.4).
We summarize these “computational oracles” in Figure 8.
Figure 8: Summary of computational oracles:
𝒚^​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}(\bm{\theta}) is used to compute unregularized predictions or as a linear
maximization oracle in conditional gradient algorithms;
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) is used to compute regularized predictions,
loss values LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=f𝜽​(𝒚)−f𝜽​(𝒚^Ω​(𝜽))L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=f_{\bm{\theta}}({\bm{y}})-f_{\bm{\theta}}(\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})),
where f𝜽​(𝝁)≔Ω⁡(𝝁)−⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩f_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\mu})\coloneqq\Omega(\bm{\mu})-{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle},
and loss gradients ∇LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)−𝒚\nabla L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})-{\bm{y}};
finally, 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑Ω​(𝜽)\mathsf{prox}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) is used by training algorithms, in particular
block coordinate ascent algorithms. In the above, we assume Ψ\Psi is
Legendre-type and 𝒞⊆𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ){\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\dom(\Psi).
8.1 Primal training
Let 𝒇W:𝒳→ℝd\bm{f}_{W}:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{d} be a model parametrized by WW. To learn
WW from training data {(𝒙i,𝒚i)}i=1n\{({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{y}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}, (𝒙i,𝒚i)∈𝒳×𝒴({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{y}}_{i})\in{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}, we minimize the regularized empirical risk
minW⁡F⁡(W)+G⁡(W)≔∑i=1nLΩ​(𝒇W​(𝒙i),𝒚i)+G⁡(W),\min_{W}F(W)+G(W)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{\Omega}(\bm{f}_{W}({\bm{x}}_{i});{\bm{y}}_{i})+G(W),
(96)
where GG is a regularization function w.r.t. parameters WW. Typical examples are
Tikhonov regularization, G⁡(W)=λ2​‖W‖F2G(W)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|W\|_{F}^{2}, and elastic net
regularization,
G⁡(W)=λ2​‖W‖F2+λ​ρ​‖W‖2G(W)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|W\|^{2}_{F}+\lambda\rho\|W\|^{2}, for some
λ>0\lambda>0 and ρ≥0\rho\geq 0.
The objective in (96) is very broad and allows
to learn, e.g., linear models or neural networks using Fenchel-Young losses. Since LΩL_{\Omega} is
convex, if 𝒇W\bm{f}_{W} is a linear model and GG is convex, then
(96) is convex as well. Assuming further that Ω\Omega is strongly
convex, then LΩL_{\Omega} is smooth and (96) can be solved globally
using proximal gradient algorithms (Wright et al., 2009; Beck and Teboulle, 2009; Defazio et al., 2014). From
Proposition 4, the gradient of LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}) w.r.t. 𝜽\bm{\theta}
is the residual vector
∇LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)−𝒚∈ℝd.\nabla L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})-{\bm{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.
(97)
Using the chain rule, the gradient of FF w.r.t. WW is
∇F(W)=∑i=1nD𝒇W​(𝒙i)⊤∇LΩ(𝒇W(𝒙i);𝒚i),\nabla F(W)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}D^{\top}_{\bm{f}_{W}({\bm{x}}_{i})}\nabla L_{\Omega}(\bm{f}_{W}({\bm{x}}_{i});{\bm{y}}_{i}),
(98)
where D𝒇W​(𝒙i)D_{\bm{f}_{W}({\bm{x}}_{i})} is the Jacobian of 𝒇W​(𝒙i)\bm{f}_{W}({\bm{x}}_{i}) w.r.t. WW, a
linear map from the space of WW to ℝd{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.
For linear models, we set 𝜽i=𝒇W​(𝒙i)=W​𝒙i\bm{\theta}_{i}=\bm{f}_{W}({\bm{x}}_{i})=W{\bm{x}}_{i}, where W∈ℝd×pW\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times p} and 𝒙i∈ℝp{\bm{x}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}, and thus get
∇F​(W)=(Y^Ω−Y)⊤​X,\nabla F(W)=(\widehat{Y}_{\Omega}-Y)^{\top}X,
(99)
where Y^Ω\widehat{Y}_{\Omega}, YY and XX are matrices whose rows
gather 𝒚^Ω​(W​𝒙i)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(W{\bm{x}}_{i}), 𝒚i{\bm{y}}_{i} and 𝒙i{\bm{x}}_{i}, for i=1,…,ni=1,\dots,n.
In summary, the two main computational
ingredients to solve (96) are 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega} and the proximity
operator of GG (when GG is non-differentiable).
This separation of concerns results in very modular implementations.
Remark on temperature scaling.
When 𝒇W\bm{f}_{W} is a linear model and GG is a homogeneous function, it is
easy to check that the temperature scaling parameter t>0t>0 from Proposition
4 and the regularization strength λ>0\lambda>0 above are
redundant. Hence, we can set t=1t=1 at training time, without loss of generality.
However, at test time, using 𝒚^t​Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{t\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) and tuning tt can
potentially improve accuracy. For sparse prediction functions, tt also gives
control over sparsity (the smaller, the sparser).
8.2 Dual training
We now derive dual training of Fenchel-Young losses.
Although our treatment follows closely Shalev-Shwartz and Zhang (2016),
it is different in that we put the output regularization Ω\Omega at the center
of all computations, leading to a new perspective.
Dual objective.
For linear models 𝒇W​(𝒙)=W​𝒙\bm{f}_{W}({\bm{x}})=W{\bm{x}}, where W∈ℝd×pW\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times p},
it can sometimes be more computationally efficient to solve the corresponding
dual problem, especially in the n≪pn\ll p setting.
From Fenchel’s duality theorem (see for instance Borwein and Lewis (2010, Theorem
3.3.5)),
we find that the dual problem of (96) is
maxα∈ℝn×d−∑i=1nLΩ∗​(−𝜶i,𝒚i)−G∗​(α⊤​X),\max_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{\Omega}^{*}(-\bm{\alpha}_{i};{\bm{y}}_{i})-G^{*}\left(\alpha^{\top}X\right),
(100)
and where LΩ∗L_{\Omega}^{*} is the convex conjugate of LΩL_{\Omega} in its first
argument.
We now rewrite the dual problem using the specific form of LΩL_{\Omega}.
Using LΩ∗​(−𝜶,𝒚)=Ω⁡(𝒚−𝜶)−Ω⁡(𝒚)L_{\Omega}^{*}(-\bm{\alpha};{\bm{y}})=\Omega({\bm{y}}-\bm{\alpha})-\Omega({\bm{y}}) and using
the change of variable 𝝁i≔𝒚i−𝜶i\bm{\mu}_{i}\coloneqq{\bm{y}}_{i}-\bm{\alpha}_{i}, we get
maxμ∈ℝn×d−D⁡(μ)​ s.t. ​𝝁i∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)​∀i∈[n],\max_{\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}}-D(\mu)\text{ s.t. }\bm{\mu}_{i}\in\dom(\Omega)~\forall i\in[n],
(101)
where we defined
D⁡(μ)≔∑i=1nΩ⁡(𝝁i)−Ω⁡(𝒚i)+G∗​(V⁡(μ))andV⁡(μ)≔(Y−μ)⊤​X.D(\mu)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Omega(\bm{\mu}_{i})-\Omega({\bm{y}}_{i})+G^{*}(V(\mu))\quad\text{and}\quad V(\mu)\coloneqq(Y-\mu)^{\top}X.
(102)
This expression is informative as we can interpret each 𝝁i\bm{\mu}_{i} as regularized
predictions, i.e., 𝝁i\bm{\mu}_{i} should belong to 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega),
the same domain as 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}.
The fact that G∗​(V​(μ))G^{*}(V(\mu)) is a function of the predictions μ\mu is similar
to the value regularization framework of Rifkin and Lippert (2007). A key difference
with the regularization Ω\Omega, however, is that G∗​(V​(μ))G^{*}(V(\mu))
depends on the training data XX through V⁡(μ)V(\mu).
When G⁡(W)=λ2​‖W‖F2⇔G∗​(V)=12​λ​‖V‖F2G(W)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|W\|^{2}_{F}\Leftrightarrow G^{*}(V)=\frac{1}{2\lambda}\|V\|^{2}_{F}, we obtain G∗​(V⁡(μ))=12​λ​Trace​((Y−μ)⊤​K​(Y−μ))G^{*}(V(\mu))=\frac{1}{2\lambda}\text{Trace}((Y-\mu)^{\top}K(Y-\mu)), where K≔X​X⊤K\coloneqq XX^{\top} is the
Gram matrix.
Primal-dual relationship.
Assuming GG is a λ\lambda-strongly convex regularizer,
given an optimal dual solution μ⋆\mu^{\star} to (101), we may retrieve
the optimal primal solution W⋆W^{\star} by
W⋆=∇G∗​(V⁡(μ⋆)).W^{\star}=\nabla G^{*}(V(\mu^{\star})).
(103)
Coordinate ascent.
We can solve (101)
using block
coordinate ascent algorithms. At every iteration, we pick i∈[n]i\in[n] and
consider the following sub-problem associated with ii:
𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝝁i∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)Ω​(𝝁i)+G∗​(V⁡(μ)).\argmin_{\bm{\mu}_{i}\in\dom(\Omega)}\Omega(\bm{\mu}_{i})+G^{*}(V(\mu)).
(104)
Since this problem could be hard to solve, we follow Shalev-Shwartz and Zhang (2016, Option
I) and consider instead a quadratic upper-bound.
If GG is λ\lambda-strongly convex, G∗G^{*} is 1λ\frac{1}{\lambda}-smooth w.r.t. the dual norm ∥⋅∥\|\cdot\| and it holds that
G∗​(V⁡(μ))≤G∗​(V⁡(μ¯))+⟨∇G∗​(V⁡(μ¯)),V⁡(μ)−V⁡(μ¯)⟩+12​λ​‖V⁡(μ)−V⁡(μ¯)‖2,G^{*}(V(\mu))\leq G^{*}(V(\bar{\mu}))+\langle\nabla G^{*}(V(\bar{\mu})),V(\mu)-V(\bar{\mu})\rangle+\frac{1}{2\lambda}\|V(\mu)-V(\bar{\mu})\|^{2},
(105)
where μ¯\bar{\mu} denotes the current iterate of μ\mu.
Using V⁡(μ)−V⁡(μ¯)=∑i=1n(𝝁¯i−𝝁i)​𝒙i⊤V(\mu)-V(\bar{\mu})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bar{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{i}){\bm{x}}_{i}^{\top}, we get
G∗​(V⁡(μ))≤G∗​(V⁡(μ¯))+∑i=1n⟨∇G∗​(V⁡(μ¯))​𝒙i,𝝁¯i−𝝁i⟩+σi2​‖𝝁¯i−𝝁i‖2,G^{*}(V(\mu))\leq G^{*}(V(\bar{\mu}))+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\nabla G^{*}(V(\bar{\mu})){\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{i}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{i}}{2}\|\bar{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{i}\|^{2},
(106)
where σi≔‖𝒙i‖2λ\sigma_{i}\coloneqq\frac{\|{\bm{x}}_{i}\|^{2}}{\lambda}.
Substituting G∗​(V​(μ))G^{*}(V(\mu)) by the above upper bound into
(104) and ignoring constant terms, we get
the following approximate sub-problem:
𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝝁i∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)Ω​(𝝁i)−𝝁i⊤​𝐯i+σi2​‖𝝁i‖2=𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑1σi​Ω​(𝐯iσi),\argmin_{\bm{\mu}_{i}\in\dom(\Omega)}\Omega(\bm{\mu}_{i})-\bm{\mu}_{i}^{\top}\bm{v}_{i}+\frac{\sigma_{i}}{2}\|\bm{\mu}_{i}\|^{2}=\mathsf{prox}_{\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}}\Omega}\left(\frac{\bm{v}_{i}}{\sigma_{i}}\right),
(107)
where 𝒗i≔∇G∗​(V⁡(μ¯))​𝒙i+σi​𝝁i¯\bm{v}_{i}\coloneqq\nabla G^{*}(V(\bar{\mu})){\bm{x}}_{i}+\sigma_{i}\bar{\bm{\mu}_{i}}.
Note that when G∗G^{*} is a quadratic function, (107) is an
optimal solution of (104).
Examples of parameter regularization GG.
We now give examples of G∗​(W)G^{*}(W) and ∇G∗​(W)\nabla G^{*}(W) for two commonly used
regularization: squared ℓ2\ell_{2} and elastic-net regularization.
When G⁡(W)=λ2​‖W‖F2G(W)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|W\|^{2}_{F}, we obtain
G∗​(V)=12​λ​‖V‖F2and∇G∗​(V)=1λ​V.G^{*}(V)=\frac{1}{2\lambda}\|V\|^{2}_{F}\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla G^{*}(V)=\frac{1}{\lambda}V.
(108)
When G⁡(W)=λ2​‖W‖F2+λ​ρ​R​(W)G(W)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|W\|^{2}_{F}+\lambda\rho R(W), for some
other regularizer R⁡(W)R(W), we obtain
∇G∗​(V)\displaystyle\nabla G^{*}(V)
=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑W⟨W,V⟩−λ2​‖W‖F2−λ​ρ​R​(W)\displaystyle=\argmax_{W}~\langle W,V\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|W\|^{2}_{F}-\lambda\rho R(W)
(109)
=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇W12​‖W−Vλ‖F2+ρ​R​(W)\displaystyle=\argmin_{W}~\frac{1}{2}\left\|W-\frac{V}{\lambda}\right\|^{2}_{F}+\rho R(W)
(110)
=𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑ρ​R​(V/λ).\displaystyle=\mathsf{prox}_{\rho R}(V/\lambda).
(111)
The conjugate is equal to G∗​(V)=⟨∇G∗​(V),V⟩−G⁡(∇G∗​(V))G^{*}(V)=\langle\nabla G^{*}(V),V\rangle-G(\nabla G^{*}(V)).
For instance, if we choose R⁡(W)=‖W‖1R(W)=\|W\|_{1}, then
G⁡(W)G(W) is the elastic-net regularization and 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑ρ​R\mathsf{prox}_{\rho R} is
the well-known soft-thresholding operator
𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑ρ​R​(V)=sign​(V)​[|V|−ρ]+,\mathsf{prox}_{\rho R}(V)=\text{sign}(V)[|V|-\rho]_{+},
(112)
where all operations above are performed element-wise.
Summary.
To summarize, on each iteration, we pick i∈[n]i\in[n] and
perform the update
𝝁i←𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑1σi​Ω​(𝒗i/σi),\bm{\mu}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{prox}_{\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}}\Omega}(\bm{v}_{i}/\sigma_{i}),
(113)
where we defined
𝒗i≔∇G∗​(V⁡(μ))​𝒙i+σi​𝝁i\bm{v}_{i}\coloneqq\nabla G^{*}(V(\mu)){\bm{x}}_{i}+\sigma_{i}\bm{\mu}_{i}
and
σi≔‖𝒙i‖2λ\sigma_{i}\coloneqq\frac{\|{\bm{x}}_{i}\|^{2}}{\lambda}.
This update does not require choosing any learning rate.
Interestingly, if 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑τ​Ω\mathsf{prox}_{\tau\Omega} is sparse, then so are the
dual variables {𝝁i}i=1n\{\bm{\mu}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}. Sparsity in the dual variables is
particularly useful
when kernelizing models, as it makes computing predictions more efficient.
When ii is picked uniformly at random, this block coordinate ascent algorithm
is known to converge to an optimal solution W⋆W^{\star}, at a linear rate if
LΩL_{\Omega} is smooth (i.e., if Ω\Omega is strongly convex)
(Shalev-Shwartz and Zhang, 2016). When 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega) is a compact set, another option
to solve (101) that does not involve proximity operators is the block
Frank-Wolfe algorithm (Lacoste-Julien et al., 2012).
8.3 Regularized prediction functions
The regularized prediction function
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) does not generally enjoy a closed-form expression and
one must resort to generic algorithms to compute it. In this section, we first
discuss two such algorithms: projected gradient and conditional gradient
methods. Then, we present a new more efficient algorithm when 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)⊆△d\dom(\Omega)\subseteq\triangle^{d} and Ω\Omega is uniformly separable.
Generic algorithms.
In their greater generality, regularized
prediction functions involve the following optimization problem
min𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)⁡f𝜽​(𝝁)≔Ω⁡(𝝁)−⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩.\min_{\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega)}f_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\mu})\coloneqq\Omega(\bm{\mu})-{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}.
(114)
Assuming Ω\Omega is convex and smooth (differentiable and with
Lipschitz-continuous gradient), we can solve this problem to arbitrary precision
using projected gradient methods. Unfortunately, the projection onto
𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega), which is needed by these algorithms, is often as challenging to
solve as (114) itself.
This is especially the case when 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega) is
𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\conv({\mathcal{Y}}), the convex hull of a combinatorial set of structured objects.
Assuming further that 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega) is a compact set,
an appealing alternative
which sidesteps these issues is provided by
conditional gradient (a.k.a. Frank-Wolfe) algorithms
(Frank and Wolfe, 1956; Dunn and Harshbarger, 1978; Jaggi, 2013). Their main advantage stems from the
fact that they access 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega)
only through the linear maximization oracle
𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐲∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)⟨𝜽,𝐲⟩\argmax_{{\bm{y}}\in\dom(\Omega)}{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}.
CG algorithms maintain the current solution as a sparse convex combination of
vertices of 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\dom(\Omega). At each iteration, the linear maximization oracle
is used to pick a new vertex to add to the combination. Despite its simplicity,
the procedure converges to an optimal solution, albeit at a sub-linear rate
(Jaggi, 2013). Linear convergence rates can be obtained using away-step,
pairwise and full-corrective variants (Lacoste-Julien and Jaggi, 2015).
When Ω\Omega is a quadratic function (as in the case of SparseMAP), an
approximate correction step can achieve finite convergence efficiently. The
resulting algorithm is known as the active set method (Nocedal and Wright, 1999, chapters
16.4 & 16.5), and is a generalization of Wolfe’s min-norm point
algorithm (Wolfe, 1976). See also Vinyes and Obozinski (2017) for a more detailed discussion
on these algorithms and Niculae et al. (2018) for an instantiation, in the specific
case of SparseMAP.
Although not explored in this paper, similar algorithms have been developed to
minimize a function over conic hulls (Locatello et al., 2017). Such algorithms allow
to compute a regularized prediction function that outputs a conic combination of
elementary structures, instead of a convex one. It can be seen as the structured
counterpart of the regularized prediction function over positive measures
(§6).
Reduction to root finding.
When Ω\Omega is a strictly convex regularization function over △d\triangle^{d}
and is uniformly separable, i.e., Ω⁡(𝒑)=∑i=1dg⁡(pi)\Omega({\bm{p}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}g(p_{i}) for some
strictly convex function gg, we now show that 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}) can be computed
in linear time.
Proposition 14.
Reduction to root finding
Let g:[0,1]→ℝ+g\colon[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+} be a strictly convex and differentiable
function. Then,
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐩∈△d⟨𝐩,𝜽⟩−∑j=1dg⁡(pj)=𝐩⁡(τ⋆)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\argmax_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}{\langle{\bm{p}},\bm{\theta}\rangle}-\sum_{j=1}^{d}g(p_{j})={\bm{p}}(\tau^{\star})
where
𝒑⁡(τ)≔(g′)−1​(max⁡{𝜽−τ,g′​(0)}){\bm{p}}(\tau)\coloneqq(g^{\prime})^{-1}(\max\{\bm{\theta}-\tau,g^{\prime}(0)\})\quad
(115)
and where
τ⋆\tau^{\star} is a root of
ϕ⁡(τ)≔⟨𝐩⁡(τ),𝟏⟩−1\phi(\tau)\coloneqq{\langle{\bm{p}}(\tau),\bm{1}\rangle}-1.
Moreover, τ⋆\tau^{\star} belongs to the tight search interval
[τmin,τmax][\tau_{\min},\tau_{\max}], where
τmin≔max⁡(𝜽)−g′​(1)andτmax≔max⁡(𝜽)−g′​(1/d).\tau_{\min}\coloneqq\max(\bm{\theta})-g^{\prime}(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{\max}\coloneqq\max(\bm{\theta})-g^{\prime}\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}\right).
(116)
The equation of 𝒑⁡(τ){\bm{p}}(\tau) can be seen as a generalization of
(12).
An approximate τ\tau such that |ϕ⁡(τ)|≤ϵ|\phi(\tau)|\leq\epsilon can be found in
O⁡(1/log⁡ϵ)O(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\log\epsilon}}) time by, e.g., bisection.
The related problem of Bregman projection
onto the probability simplex was recently studied by Krichene et al. (2015) but our
derivation is different and more direct (cf. §B.8).
For example, when Ω\Omega is the negative α\alpha-Tsallis entropy
−𝖧αt-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{\alpha}, which we saw can be written in separable form in
(52), we obtain
g(t)=tα−tα⁡(α−1),g′(t)=tα−1−1α−1and(g′)−1(s)=(1+(α−1)s)1α−1,g(t)=\frac{t^{\alpha}-t}{\alpha(\alpha-1)},\quad g^{\prime}(t)=\frac{t^{\alpha-1}-1}{\alpha-1}\quad\text{and}\quad(g^{\prime})^{-1}(s)=\big(1+(\alpha-1)s\big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha-1}},
yielding
𝒑⁡(τ)=(1+(α−1)​max⁡(𝜽−τ,−1/α−1))1α−1.{\bm{p}}(\tau)=\big(1+(\alpha-1)\max(\bm{\theta}-\tau,\nicefrac{{-1}}{{\alpha-1}})\big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha-1}}.
From the root τ⋆\tau^{\star} of
ϕ⁡(τ)=⟨𝒑⁡(τ),𝟏⟩−1\phi(\tau)={\langle{\bm{p}}(\tau),\bm{1}\rangle}-1, we obtain
𝒚^−𝖧αt​(𝜽)=𝒑⁡(τ⋆)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{\alpha}}(\bm{\theta})={\bm{p}}(\tau^{\star}).
8.4 Proximity operators
Computing the proximity operator 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑τ​Ω​(𝜼)\mathsf{prox}_{\tau\Omega}(\bm{\eta}), defined in
(2), usually involves a more challenging optimization problem than
𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}). For instance, when Ω\Omega is Shannon’s negative entropy
over △d\triangle^{d},
𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega} enjoys a closed-form solution (the softmax) but not 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑Ω\mathsf{prox}_{\Omega}.
However, we can always compute 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑τ​Ω​(𝜼)\mathsf{prox}_{\tau\Omega}(\bm{\eta}) by first-order
gradient methods given access
to 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}. Indeed, using the Moreau decomposition, we have
𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑τ​Ω​(𝜼)=𝜼−𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑Ω∗τ​(𝜼/τ).\mathsf{prox}_{\tau\Omega}(\bm{\eta})=\bm{\eta}-\mathsf{prox}_{\frac{\Omega^{*}}{\tau}}(\bm{\eta}/\tau).
(117)
Since 𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω∗)=ℝd\dom(\Omega^{*})={\mathbb{R}}^{d}, computing
𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑Ω∗τ​(𝜼/τ)\mathsf{prox}_{\frac{\Omega^{*}}{\tau}}(\bm{\eta}/\tau) only involves an unconstrained
optimization problem, 𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝜽∈ℝd12​‖𝜽−𝜼/τ‖2+1τ​Ω∗​(𝜽)\argmin_{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\eta}/\tau\|^{2}+\frac{1}{\tau}\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}).
Since ∇Ω∗=𝒚^Ω\nabla\Omega^{*}=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}, that optimization problem can easily be
solved by any first-order gradient method
given access to 𝒚^Ω\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}.
For specific choices of Ω\Omega, 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑τ​Ω​(𝜼)\mathsf{prox}_{\tau\Omega}(\bm{\eta})
can be computed directly more efficiently, as we now discuss.
Closed-form expressions.
We now give examples of commonly-used loss functions for which
𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑τ​Ω\mathsf{prox}_{\tau\Omega} enjoys a closed-form expression.
For the squared loss,
we choose Ω⁡(𝝁)=12​‖𝝁‖2\Omega(\bm{\mu})=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mu}\|^{2}. Hence:
𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑τ​Ω​(𝜼)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝝁∈ℝd12​‖𝝁−𝜼‖2+τ2​‖𝝁‖2=𝜼τ+1.\mathsf{prox}_{\tau\Omega}(\bm{\eta})=\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\eta}\|^{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}\|\bm{\mu}\|^{2}=\frac{\bm{\eta}}{\tau+1}.
(118)
For the perceptron loss (Rosenblatt, 1958; Collins, 2002),
we choose Ω=I△d\Omega=I_{\triangle^{d}}. Hence:
𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑τ​Ω​(𝜼)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝐩∈△d12​‖𝐩−𝜼‖2.\mathsf{prox}_{\tau\Omega}(\bm{\eta})=\argmin_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{p}}-\bm{\eta}\|^{2}.
(119)
For the sparsemax loss (Martins and Astudillo, 2016), we choose
Ω=12∥⋅∥2+I△d\Omega=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|^{2}+I_{\triangle^{d}}. Hence:
𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑τ​Ω​(𝜼)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝐩∈△d12​‖𝐩−𝜼‖2+τ2​‖𝐩‖2=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝐩∈△d12​‖𝐩−𝜼τ+1‖2.\mathsf{prox}_{\tau\Omega}(\bm{\eta})=\argmin_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{p}}-\bm{\eta}\|^{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}\|{\bm{p}}\|^{2}=\argmin_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\bm{p}}-\frac{\bm{\eta}}{\tau+1}\right\|^{2}.
(120)
For the cost-sensitive multiclass hinge loss,
we choose
Ω=I△d−⟨⋅,𝒄𝒚⟩\Omega=I_{\triangle^{d}}-\langle\cdot,\bm{c}_{{\bm{y}}}\rangle. Hence:
𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑τ​Ω​(𝜼)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝐩∈△d12​‖𝐩−𝜼‖2−τ⁡⟨𝐩,𝐜𝐲⟩=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝐩∈△d12​‖𝐩−(𝜼+τ​𝐜𝐲)‖2.\mathsf{prox}_{\tau\Omega}(\bm{\eta})=\argmin_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{p}}-\bm{\eta}\|^{2}-\tau\langle{\bm{p}},\bm{c}_{{\bm{y}}}\rangle=\argmin_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{p}}-(\bm{\eta}+\tau\bm{c}_{{\bm{y}}})\|^{2}.
(121)
Choosing 𝒄𝒚=𝟏−𝒚\bm{c}_{{\bm{y}}}=\bm{1}-{\bm{y}}, where 𝒚∈{𝒆i}i=1d{\bm{y}}\in\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d} is the ground-truth label, gives the proximity operator
for the usual multiclass hinge loss (Crammer and Singer, 2001).
For the Shannon entropy and 1.51.5-Tsallis entropy, we show that 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗑−𝖧\mathsf{prox}_{-\mathsf{H}}
reduces to root finding in Appendix A.1.
9 Experiments
In this section, we demonstrate one of the key features of Fenchel-Young losses:
their ability to induce sparse probability distributions. We focus on
two tasks: label proportion estimation (§9.1) and
dependency parsing (§9.2).
9.1 Label proportion estimation experiments
As we saw in §4, α\alpha-Tsallis entropies generate a family
of losses, with the logistic (α→1\alpha\to 1) and sparsemax losses (α=2\alpha=2)
as important special cases.
In addition, they are
twice differentiable for α∈[1,2)\alpha\in[1,2), produce sparse probability distributions for α>1\alpha>1 and are
computationally efficient for any α≥1\alpha\geq 1, thanks to Proposition
14. In this section, we demonstrate their effectiveness on
the task of label proportion estimation and compare different solvers for
computing the regularized prediction function 𝒚^−𝖧αt\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{\alpha}}.
Experimental setup.
Given an input vector 𝒙∈𝒳⊆ℝp{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{p}, where pp is the number of
features, our goal is to estimate a vector of label proportions 𝒚∈△d{\bm{y}}\in\triangle^{d}, where dd is the number of classes. If 𝒚{\bm{y}} is sparse, we expect
the superiority of Tsallis losses over the conventional logistic loss on this
task. At training time, given a set of nn pairs (𝒙i,𝒚i)({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{y}}_{i}), we estimate a
matrix W∈ℝd×pW\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times p} by minimizing the convex objective
R⁡(W)≔∑i=1nLΩ​(W​𝒙i,𝒚i)+λ2​‖W‖F2.R(W)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{\Omega}(W{\bm{x}}_{i};{\bm{y}}_{i})+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|W\|_{F}^{2}.
(122)
We optimize the loss using L-BFGS (Liu and Nocedal, 1989).
From Proposition 4 and using the chain rule, we obtain the
gradient expression
∇R​(W)=(Y^Ω−Y)⊤​X+λ​W\nabla R(W)=(\widehat{Y}_{\Omega}-Y)^{\top}X+\lambda W,
where Y^Ω\widehat{Y}_{\Omega}, YY and XX are matrices whose rows
gather 𝒚^Ω​(W​𝒙i)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(W{\bm{x}}_{i}), 𝒚i{\bm{y}}_{i} and 𝒙i{\bm{x}}_{i}, for i=1,…,ni=1,\dots,n.
At test time, we predict label proportions by
𝒑=𝒚^−𝖧αt​(W​𝒙){\bm{p}}=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{\alpha}}(W{\bm{x}}).
Real data experiments.
We ran experiments on 77 standard multi-label benchmark datasets — see
Table 3 for dataset characteristics11
1
The datasets can
be downloaded from http://mulan.sourceforge.net/datasets-mlc.html and
https://www.csie.ntu.edu.tw/~cjlin/libsvmtools/datasets/..
For all datasets, we removed
samples with no label, normalized samples to have zero mean unit variance, and
normalized labels to lie in the probability simplex. We chose λ∈{10−4,10−3,…,104}\lambda\in\{10^{-4},10^{-3},\dots,10^{4}\} and α∈{1,1.1,…,2}\alpha\in\{1,1.1,\dots,2\} against the
validation set. We report the test set mean Jensen-Shannon divergence,
JS(𝒑,𝒚)≔1/2KL(𝒑∥𝒑+𝒚2)+1/2KL(𝒚∥𝒑+𝒚2)\text{JS}({\bm{p}},{\bm{y}})\coloneqq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\operatorname{KL}({\bm{p}}\|\frac{{\bm{p}}+{\bm{y}}}{2})+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\operatorname{KL}({\bm{y}}\|\frac{{\bm{p}}+{\bm{y}}}{2}), and the mean squared error
1/2​‖𝒑−𝒚‖2\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\,\|{\bm{p}}-{\bm{y}}\|^{2} in Table 4.
As can be seen, the loss with tuned α\alpha achieves the best averaged rank
overall. Tuning α\alpha allows to choose the best loss in the family in a
data-driven fashion.

Synthetic data experiments.
We follow Martins and Astudillo (2016) and generate a document
𝒙∈ℝp{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p} from a mixture of multinomials and label proportions 𝒚∈△d{\bm{y}}\in\triangle^{d} from a multinomial. The number of words in 𝒙{\bm{x}} and labels in 𝒚{\bm{y}}
is sampled from a Poisson distribution — see Martins and Astudillo (2016) for a precise
description of the generative process. We use 1200 samples as training set, 200
samples as validation set and 1000 samples as test set.
We tune λ∈{10−6,10−5,…,100}\lambda\in\{10^{-6},10^{-5},\dots,10^{0}\}
and α∈{1.0,1.1,…,2.0}\alpha\in\{1.0,1.1,\dots,2.0\} against the validation set.
We report the Jensen-Shannon divergence in Figure
10. Results using the mean squared error (MSE)
were entirely similar.
When the number of classes is 10, Tsallis and sparsemax losses
perform almost exactly the same, both outperforming softmax.
When the number of classes is 50, Tsallis losses outperform both sparsemax and
softmax.

9.2 Dependency parsing experiments
We next compare Fenchel-Young losses for structured
output prediction, as formulated in §7,
for non-projective dependency parsing.
The application consists of predicting the directed tree of grammatical
dependencies between words in a sentence (Jurafsky and Martin, 2018, Chapter 14).
We tackle it by learning structured models over the arborescence
polytope, using the structured SVM, CRF, and SparseMAP losses (Niculae et al., 2018),
which we saw to all be instances of Fenchel-Young loss.
Training data,
as in the CoNLL 2017 shared task (Zeman et al., 2017), comes from
the Universal Dependency project (Nivre et al., 2016).
To isolate the effect of the loss, we use the provided gold
tokenization and part-of-speech tags.
We follow closely the bidirectional LSTM arc-factored parser of
Kiperwasser and Goldberg (2016), using the same model configuration;
the only exception is not using externally pretrained embeddings.
Parameters are trained using Adam (Kingma and Ba, 2015), tuning the
learning rate on the grid {.5,1,2,4,8}×10−3\{.5,1,2,4,8\}\times 10^{-3},
expanded by a factor of 2 if the best model is at either end.
We experiment with 5 languages, diverse both in terms of family and in
terms of the amount of training data (ranging from 1,400 sentences
for Vietnamese to 12,525 for English).
Test set results (Table 5) indicate that SparseMAP outperforms
the CRF loss and is competitive with the structured SVM loss,
outperforming it substantially on Chinese.
The Fenchel-Young perspective sheds further light onto the empirical
results, as the SparseMAP loss and the structured SVM (unlike the CRF) both enjoy
the margin property with margin 1, while both SparseMAP and the CRF loss
(unlike structured SVM) are differentiable.
Table 5: Unlabeled attachment accuracy scores for
dependency parsing, using a bi-LSTM model (Kiperwasser and Goldberg, 2016).
For context, we include the scores of the
CoNLL 2017 UDPipe baseline (Straka and Straková, 2017).

10 Related work
10.1 Loss functions
Proper losses.
Proper losses, a.k.a. proper scoring rules, are a well-studied object
in statistics
(Grünwald and Dawid (2004); Gneiting and Raftery (2007); references therein)
and more recently in machine learning (Reid and Williamson, 2010; Williamson et al., 2016).
A loss ℓ⁡(𝒑,𝒚)\ell({\bm{p}};{\bm{y}}), between a ground truth
𝒚∈△d{\bm{y}}\in\triangle^{d} and a probability forecast 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}, where dd is the number of classes,
is said to be proper if it is minimized when
estimating the true 𝒚{\bm{y}}. Formally,
a proper loss ℓ\ell satisfies
ℓ⁡(𝒚,𝒚)≤ℓ⁡(𝒑,𝒚)∀𝒑,𝒚∈△d.\ell({\bm{y}};{\bm{y}})\leq\ell({\bm{p}};{\bm{y}})\quad\forall{\bm{p}},{\bm{y}}\in\triangle^{d}.
(123)
It is strictly proper if the inequality is strict when 𝒑≠𝒚{\bm{p}}\neq{\bm{y}},
implying that it is uniquely minimized by predicting the correct probability.
Strictly proper losses induce Fisher consistent estimators of
probabilities (Williamson et al., 2016).
A key result, which dates back to Savage (1971) (see also
Gneiting and Raftery (2007)), is that given a regularization function Ω:△d→ℝ\Omega\colon\triangle^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}, the function ℓΩ:△d×△d→ℝ+\ell_{\Omega}\colon\triangle^{d}\times\triangle^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+} defined by
ℓΩ​(𝒑,𝒚)≔⟨∇Ω​(𝒑),𝒚−𝒑⟩−Ω⁡(𝒑)\ell_{\Omega}({\bm{p}};{\bm{y}})\coloneqq\langle\nabla\Omega({\bm{p}}),{\bm{y}}-{\bm{p}}\rangle-\Omega({\bm{p}})
(124)
is proper.
It is easy to see that
ℓΩ(𝒑;𝒚)=BΩ(𝒚∥𝒑)−Ω(𝒚)\ell_{\Omega}({\bm{p}};{\bm{y}})=B_{\Omega}({\bm{y}}\|{\bm{p}})-\Omega({\bm{y}}),
which recovers
the well-known
Bregman divergence representation of proper losses.
For example, using the Gini index 𝖧⁡(𝒑)=1−‖𝒑‖2\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})=1-\|{\bm{p}}\|^{2}
generates the Brier score (Brier, 1950)
ℓ−𝖧(𝒑;𝒆k)=∑i=1d([[k=i]]−pi)2,\ell_{-\mathsf{H}}({\bm{p}};\bm{e}_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}([[k=i]]-p_{i})^{2},
(125)
showing that the sparsemax loss and the Brier score share the same generating
function.
More generally, while a proper loss ℓΩ\ell_{\Omega} is related to a primal-space Bregman
divergence, a Fenchel-Young loss LΩL_{\Omega} can be seen as a
mixed-space Bregman divergence (§3).
This difference has a number of
important consequences. First,
ℓΩ\ell_{\Omega} is not necessarily convex in 𝒑{\bm{p}}
(Williamson et al. (2016, Proposition 17) show that it is in fact quasi-convex).
In contrast,
LΩL_{\Omega} is always convex in 𝜽\bm{\theta}. Second, the first argument is
constrained to △d\triangle^{d} for ℓΩ\ell_{\Omega}, while
unconstrained for LΩL_{\Omega}.
In practice, proper losses are often
composed with an invertible link function 𝝍−1:ℝd→△d\bm{\psi}^{-1}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to\triangle^{d}. This form of a loss, ℓΩ​(𝝍−1​(𝜽),𝒚)\ell_{\Omega}(\bm{\psi}^{-1}(\bm{\theta});{\bm{y}}), is sometimes
called composite (Buja et al., 2005; Reid and Williamson, 2010; Williamson et al., 2016).
However, the composition of ℓΩ​(⋅,𝒚)\ell_{\Omega}(\cdot;{\bm{y}}) and 𝝍−1​(𝜽)\bm{\psi}^{-1}(\bm{\theta}) is not
necessarily convex in 𝜽\bm{\theta}. The canonical link function
(Buja et al., 2005) of ℓΩ\ell_{\Omega} is a link function that ensures the convexity of
ℓΩ​(𝝍−1​(𝜽),𝒚)\ell_{\Omega}(\bm{\psi}^{-1}(\bm{\theta});{\bm{y}}) in 𝜽\bm{\theta}. It also plays a key role in
generalized linear models (Nelder and Baker, 1972; McCullagh and Nelder, 1989).
Following Proposition 6,
when Ω\Omega is Legendre type, we obtain
LΩ(𝜽;𝒚)=BΩ(𝒚∥𝒚^Ω(𝜽))=ℓΩ(𝒚^Ω(𝜽);𝒚)+Ω(𝒚).L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=B_{\Omega}({\bm{y}}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}))=\ell_{\Omega}(\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta});{\bm{y}})+\Omega({\bm{y}}).
(126)
Thus, in this case, Fenchel-Young losses and proper
composite losses coincide up to the constant term Ω⁡(𝒚)\Omega({\bm{y}}) (which vanishes
if 𝒚=𝒆k{\bm{y}}=\bm{e}_{k} and Ω\Omega satisfies assumption A.1), with 𝝍−1=𝒚^Ω\bm{\psi}^{-1}=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega} the canonical inverse link function. Fenchel-Young losses, however,
require neither invertible link nor Legendre type assumptions, allowing to
express losses (e.g., hinge or sparsemax) that are not expressible in composite
form. Moreover, as seen in §5, a Legendre-type Ω\Omega
precisely precludes sparse probability distributions and losses enjoying a
margin.
However, the decoupling between loss and link as promoted by proper composite
losses also has some merits (Reid and Williamson, 2010). For instance, using
a non-canonical link is useful to express the exponential loss of Adaboost
(Nowak-Vila et al., 2019).
Other related losses.
Nock and Nielsen (2009) proposed a binary classification loss construction.
Technically, their loss is based on the Legendre transformation (a subset of
Fenchel conjugate functions), precluding non-invertible mappings.
Masnadi-Shirazi (2011) studied the Bayes consistency of related binary
classification loss functions.
Duchi et al. (2018, Proposition 3) derived the multi-class loss
(45), a special case of Fenchel-Young loss over the
probability simplex, and showed (Proposition 4) that any strictly concave
generalized entropy generates a classification-calibrated loss.
Amid and Warmuth (2017) proposed a different family of losses based on the Tsallis
divergence, to interpolate between convex and non-convex losses, for
robustness to label noise.
Finally, several works have explored connections between
another divergence, the ff-divergence (Csiszár, 1975), and
surrogate loss functions (Nguyen et al., 2009; Reid and Williamson, 2011; Garcia-Garcia and Williamson, 2012; Duchi et al., 2018).
Consistency.
In this paper, we have not addressed the question of the consistency of
Fenchel-Young losses when used as a surrogate for a (possibly non-convex) loss.
Although consistency has been widely studied in the multiclass setting
(Zhang, 2004; Bartlett et al., 2006; Tewari and Bartlett, 2007; Mroueh et al., 2012) and in other specific
settings (Duchi et al., 2010; Ravikumar et al., 2011), it is only recently that it was
studied for general losses in a fully general structured prediction setting.
Since the publication of this paper, sufficient conditions for consistency have
been established for composite losses (Nowak-Vila et al., 2019) and for
projection-based losses (Blondel, 2019), both a subset of Fenchel-Young
losses. As shown in these works, the strong convexity of Ω\Omega plays a
crucial role.
10.2 Fenchel duality in machine learning
In this paper, we make extensive use of Fenchel duality to derive loss
functions. Fenchel duality has also played a key role in several machine learning
studies before. It was
used to provide a unifying perspective on convex empirical risk minimization and
representer theorems (Rifkin and Lippert, 2007). It was also used for deriving regret
bounds (Shalev-Shwartz and Singer, 2007; Shalev-Shwartz and Kakade, 2009), risk bounds (Kakade et al., 2009), and
unified analyses of boosting (Shen and Li, 2010; Shalev-Shwartz and Singer, 2010; Telgarsky, 2012).
However, none of these works propose loss functions, as we do. More closely
related to our work are smoothing techniques (Nesterov, 2005; Beck and Teboulle, 2012),
which have been used extensively to create smoothed versions of existing losses
(Song et al., 2014; Shalev-Shwartz and Zhang, 2016). However, these techniques were applied on a
per-loss basis and were not connected to an induced probability distribution. In
contrast, we propose a generic loss construction, with clear links between
smoothing / regularization and the probability distribution produced by
regularized prediction functions.
10.3 Approximate inference with conditional gradient algorithms
In this paper, we suggest conditional gradient (CG) algorithms as a powerful
tool for computing regularized prediction functions and Fenchel-Young losses
over potentially complex output domains. CG algorithms have been used before to
compute an approximate solution to intractable marginal inference problems
(Belanger et al., 2013; Krishnan et al., 2015) or to sample from an intractable
distribution so as to approximate its mean (Bach et al., 2012; Lacoste-Julien et al., 2015).
When combined with reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS), these ideas are
closely related maximum mean discrepancy (Gretton et al., 2012).
11 Conclusion
We showed that the notion of output regularization and Fenchel duality provide
simple core principles, unifying many existing loss functions, and allowing to
create useful new ones easily, on a large spectrum of tasks. We characterized a
tight connection between sparse distributions and losses with a separation
margin, and showed that these losses are precisely the ones that cannot be
written in proper composite loss form. We established the computational tools
to efficiently learn with Fenchel-Young losses, whether in unstructured or
structured settings. We expect that this groundwork will enable the creation of
many more novel losses in the future, by exploring other convex polytopes and
regularizers.

for numerous fruitful discussions and Tim Vieira for
introducing him to generalized exponential families.

and by the Fundação para a Ciência e Tecnologia through contracts UID/EEA/50008/2013 and CMUPERI/TIC/0046/2014 (GoLocal).

In this section, we first show how the proximity operator of uniformly separable

The proximity operator is defined as

A.2 Loss “Fenchel-Youngization”
Not all loss functions proposed in the literature can be written in
Fenchel-Young loss form. In this section, we present a natural method to
approximate (in a sense we will clarify) any loss ℓ:ℝd×{𝒆i}i=1d→ℝ+\ell\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+} with a Fenchel-Young loss. This has two main
advantages. First, the resulting loss is convex even when ℓ\ell is not.
Second, the associated regularized prediction function can be used for
probabilistic prediction even if ℓ\ell is not probabilistic.
From loss to entropy.
Equations (47) and (48),
which relate entropy and conditional Bayes risk,
suggest a reverse construction from entropy to loss function. This
direction has been explored in previous works
(Grünwald and Dawid, 2004; Duchi et al., 2018). The entropy
𝖧ℓ\mathsf{H}_{\ell} generated from ℓ\ell is defined as follows:
𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)≔inf𝜽∈ℝd𝔼𝒑​[ℓ⁡(𝜽,Y)]=inf𝜽∈ℝd∑i=1dpi​ℓ​(𝜽,𝒆i).\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})\coloneqq\inf_{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{p}}}[\ell(\bm{\theta};Y)]=\inf_{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}\ell(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{i}).
(138)
This is the infimum of a linear and thus concave function of 𝒑{\bm{p}}.
Hence, by
Danskin’s theorem (Danskin, 1966; Bertsekas, 1999, Proposition B.25),
Hℓ​(𝒑)H_{\ell}({\bm{p}}) is concave (note that this is true even if ℓ\ell is not convex).
As an example, Duchi et al. (2018, Example 1) show that the entropy associated
with the zero-one loss is 𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)=1−maxj⁡pj\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})=1-\max_{j}p_{j}.
As we discussed in §4.3,
this entropy is known as the Berger-Parker dominance
index (Berger and Parker, 1970), and is a special case of norm entropy
(54).
The following new proposition shows how to compute the entropy
of pairwise hinge losses.
Proposition 15.
Entropy generated by pairwise hinge losses
Let ℓ⁡(𝛉,𝐞k)≔∑j≠kϕ⁡(θj−θk)\ell(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})\coloneqq\sum_{j\neq k}\phi(\theta_{j}-\theta_{k})
and τ⁡(𝐩)≔minj⁡1−pj\tau({\bm{p}})\coloneqq\min_{j}1-p_{j}.
Then, for all 𝐩∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}:
1.
If ϕ⁡(t)\phi(t) is the hinge function [1+t]+[1+t]_{+}
then 𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)=τ​(𝒑)​d\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})=\tau({\bm{p}})d.
2.
If ϕ⁡(t)\phi(t) is the smoothed hinge function
(191), then
𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)=−τ​(𝒑)22​∑j=1d11−pj+τ⁡(𝒑)​d\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})=\frac{-\tau({\bm{p}})^{2}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{1-p_{j}}+\tau({\bm{p}})d.
3.
If ϕ⁡(t)\phi(t) is the squared hinge function 12​[1+t]+2\frac{1}{2}[1+t]^{2}_{+},
then 𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)=12​d2∑j=1d1/(1−pj)\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})=\frac{\frac{1}{2}d^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{d}1/(1-p_{j})}.
See Appendix B.9 for a proof.
The first one recovers (Duchi et al., 2018, Example 5) with a simpler proof. The
last two are new.
These entropies are illustrated in Figure 11.
For the last two choices, 𝖧ℓ\mathsf{H}_{\ell} is strictly concave over △d\triangle^{d} and the
associated prediction function 𝒚^−𝖧ℓ​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}}(\bm{\theta}) is typically
sparse, although we are not aware of a closed-form expression.
Figure 11: Contour comparison of Gini index 𝖧2t\mathsf{H}^{\textsc{t}}_{2} and pairwise hinge entropies
𝖧ℓ\mathsf{H}_{\ell}.
And back to Fenchel-Young loss.
A natural idea is then to construct a Fenchel-Young from 𝖧ℓ\mathsf{H}_{\ell}.
The resulting loss is convex by construction.
Let us assume for simplicity that ℓ\ell achieves its minimum at 00,
implying 𝖧ℓ​(𝒆k)=0\mathsf{H}_{\ell}(\bm{e}_{k})=0.
Combining (45) and (138), we obtain
L−𝖧ℓ​(𝜽,𝒆k)\displaystyle L_{-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})
=(−𝖧ℓ)∗​(𝜽)−θk\displaystyle=(-\mathsf{H}_{\ell})^{*}(\bm{\theta})-\theta_{k}
(139)
=max𝒑∈△d⁡⟨𝜽,𝒑⟩+𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)−θk\displaystyle=\max_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{p}}\rangle}+\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})-\theta_{k}
(140)
=max𝒑∈△d⁡min⁡∑i=1d𝜽′∈ℝd⁡pi​(ℓ⁡(𝜽′,𝒆i)+θi)−θk.\displaystyle=\max_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}\min_{\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}(\ell(\bm{\theta}^{\prime};\bm{e}_{i})+\theta_{i})-\theta_{k}.
(141)
By weak duality, we have for every (possibly non-convex) ℓ\ell
L−𝖧ℓ​(𝜽,𝒆k)\displaystyle L_{-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})
≤min𝜽′∈ℝd⁡max⁡∑i=1d𝒑∈△d⁡pi​(ℓ⁡(𝜽′,𝒆i)+θi)−θk\displaystyle\leq\min_{\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\max_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}(\ell(\bm{\theta}^{\prime};\bm{e}_{i})+\theta_{i})-\theta_{k}
(142)
=min𝜽′∈ℝd⁡maxi∈[d]⁡ℓ⁡(𝜽′,𝒆i)+θi−θk.\displaystyle=\min_{\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\max_{i\in[d]}\ell(\bm{\theta}^{\prime};\bm{e}_{i})+\theta_{i}-\theta_{k}.
(143)
We can see (𝜽;𝒆k)↦maxi∈[d]⁡ℓ⁡(𝜽′,𝒆i)+θi−θk(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})\mapsto\max_{i\in[d]}\ell(\bm{\theta}^{\prime};\bm{e}_{i})+\theta_{i}-\theta_{k} as a family of cost-augmented hinge losses (Tsochantaridis et al., 2005)
parametrized by 𝜽′\bm{\theta}^{\prime}. Hence, L−𝖧ℓ​(𝜽,𝒆k)L_{-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k}) is upper-bounded by the
tightest loss in this family.
If ℓ\ell is convex, strong duality holds and we obtain equality
L−𝖧ℓ​(𝜽,𝒆k)=min𝜽′∈ℝd⁡maxi∈[d]⁡ℓ⁡(𝜽′,𝒆i)+θi−θk.\displaystyle L_{-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})=\min_{\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\max_{i\in[d]}\ell(\bm{\theta}^{\prime};\bm{e}_{i})+\theta_{i}-\theta_{k}.
(144)
Margin.
We can easily upper-bound the margin of L−𝖧ℓL_{-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}}.
Indeed, assuming ℓ⁡(𝜽,𝒆i)\ell(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{i}) is upper-bounded by mm for all 𝜽\bm{\theta} and i∈[d]i\in[d], we get
0≤L−𝖧ℓ​(𝜽,𝒆k)≤maxi∈[d]⁡ℓ⁡(𝜽,𝒆i)+θi−θk≤maxi∈[d]⁡m+θi−θk.0\leq L_{-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})\leq\max_{i\in[d]}\ell(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{i})+\theta_{i}-\theta_{k}\leq\max_{i\in[d]}m+\theta_{i}-\theta_{k}.
(145)
Hence, from Definition 8, we have margin⁡(L−𝖧ℓ)≤m\mathrm{margin}(L_{-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}})\leq m. Note that if
margin⁡(L−𝖧ℓ)≤margin⁡(ℓ)\mathrm{margin}(L_{-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}})\leq\mathrm{margin}(\ell),
then L−𝖧ℓ​(𝜽,𝒆k)=0⇒ℓ⁡(𝜽,𝒆k)=0L_{-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})=0\Rightarrow\ell(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})=0.
Appendix B Proofs
B.1 Proof of Proposition 2
Effect of a permutation.
Let Ω⁡(𝝁)\Omega(\bm{\mu}) be symmetric. We first prove that Ω∗\Omega^{*} is
symmetric as well. Indeed, we have
Ω∗​(𝑷​𝜽)=sup𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)(𝑷​𝜽)⊤​𝝁−Ω⁡(𝝁)=sup𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)𝜽⊤​𝑷⊤​𝝁−Ω⁡(𝑷⊤​𝝁)=Ω∗​(𝜽).\Omega^{*}(\bm{P}\bm{\theta})=\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega)}(\bm{P}\bm{\theta})^{\top}\bm{\mu}-\Omega(\bm{\mu})=\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega)}\bm{\theta}^{\top}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{\mu}-\Omega(\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{\mu})=\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}).
(146)
The last equality was obtained by
a change of variable 𝝁′=𝑷⊤​𝝁\bm{\mu}^{\prime}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{\mu}, from which 𝝁\bm{\mu} is
recovered as 𝝁=𝑷​𝝁′\bm{\mu}=\bm{P}\bm{\mu}^{\prime}, which proves ∇Ω∗(𝑷𝝁)=𝑷∇Ω∗(𝝁)\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{P}\bm{\mu})=\bm{P}\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{\mu}).
Order preservation.
Since Ω∗\Omega^{*} is convex, the gradient operator ∇Ω∗\nabla\Omega^{*} is monotone, i.e.,
(𝜽′−𝜽)⊤​(𝝁′−𝝁)≥0(\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta})^{\top}(\bm{\mu}^{\prime}-\bm{\mu})\geq 0
(147)
for any
𝜽,𝜽′∈ℝd\bm{\theta},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}, 𝝁=∇Ω∗​(𝜽)\bm{\mu}=\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}) and 𝝁′=∇Ω∗​(𝜽′)\bm{\mu}^{\prime}=\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}^{\prime}). Let 𝜽′\bm{\theta}^{\prime} be obtained from 𝜽\bm{\theta} by swapping two coordinates,
i.e., θj′=θi\theta_{j}^{\prime}=\theta_{i}, θi′=θj\theta_{i}^{\prime}=\theta_{j}, and θk′=θk\theta_{k}^{\prime}=\theta_{k} for any k∉{i,j}k\notin\{i,j\}. Then, since Ω\Omega is symmetric, we obtain:
2​(θj−θi)​(μj−μi)≥0,\displaystyle 2(\theta_{j}-\theta_{i})(\mu_{j}-\mu_{i})\geq 0,
(148)
which implies θi>θj⇒μi≥μj\theta_{i}>\theta_{j}\Rightarrow\mu_{i}\geq\mu_{j} and μi>μj⇒θi≥θj\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}\Rightarrow\theta_{i}\geq\theta_{j}. To fully prove the claim, we need to show that the last
inequality is strict: to do this, we simply invoke
∇Ω∗(𝑷𝝁)=𝑷∇Ω∗(𝝁)\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{P}\bm{\mu})=\bm{P}\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{\mu})
with a matrix 𝑷\bm{P} that permutes ii and jj, from which we must have θi=θj⇒μi=μj\theta_{i}=\theta_{j}\Rightarrow\mu_{i}=\mu_{j}.
Approximation error.
Let 𝒚⋆≔𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐲∈𝒴⟨𝜽,𝐲⟩{\bm{y}}^{\star}\coloneqq\argmax_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle} and 𝝁⋆≔𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩−Ω⁡(𝝁)\bm{\mu}^{\star}\coloneqq\argmax_{\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega)}{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}-\Omega(\bm{\mu}). If ff is a differentiable
γ\gamma-strongly convex function with unique minimizer 𝝁⋆\bm{\mu}^{\star}, then
γ2​‖𝒚−𝝁⋆‖22≤f⁡(𝒚)−f⁡(𝝁⋆)∀𝒚∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(f).\frac{\gamma}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-\bm{\mu}^{\star}\|^{2}_{2}\leq f({\bm{y}})-f(\bm{\mu}^{\star})\quad\forall{\bm{y}}\in\dom(f).
(149)
We assume that 𝒴⊆𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω){\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq\dom(\Omega) so it suffices to upper-bound
f⁡(𝒚⋆)−f⁡(𝝁⋆)f({\bm{y}}^{\star})-f(\bm{\mu}^{\star}), where f⁡(𝝁)≔Ω⁡(𝝁)−⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩f(\bm{\mu})\coloneqq\Omega(\bm{\mu})-{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}.
Since L≤Ω⁡(𝝁)≤UL\leq\Omega(\bm{\mu})\leq U for all 𝝁∈𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ω)\bm{\mu}\in\dom(\Omega), we have
⟨𝜽,𝝁⋆⟩≥−f⁡(𝝁⋆)+L{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}^{\star}\rangle}\geq-f(\bm{\mu}^{\star})+L and
−f⁡(𝒚⋆)+U≥⟨𝜽,𝒚⋆⟩-f({\bm{y}}^{\star})+U\geq{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}^{\star}\rangle}. Together with
⟨𝜽,𝒚⋆⟩≥⟨𝜽,𝝁⋆⟩{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}^{\star}\rangle}\geq{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}^{\star}\rangle}, this implies f⁡(𝒚⋆)−f⁡(𝝁⋆)≤U−Lf({\bm{y}}^{\star})-f(\bm{\mu}^{\star})\leq U-L. Therefore, 12​‖𝒚⋆−𝝁⋆‖2≤U−Lγ\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{y}}^{\star}-\bm{\mu}^{\star}\|^{2}\leq\frac{U-L}{\gamma}.
Temperature scaling & constant invariance.
These immediately follow from properties of the 𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑\argmax operator.
B.2 Proof of Proposition 6
We set Ω≔Ψ+I𝒞\Omega\coloneqq\Psi+I_{\mathcal{C}}.
Bregman projections.
If Ψ\Psi is Legendre type, then ∇Ψ​(∇Ψ∗​(𝜽))=𝜽\nabla\Psi(\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}))=\bm{\theta} for
all 𝜽∈𝗂𝗇𝗍(𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ∗))\bm{\theta}\in\interior(\dom(\Psi^{*})). Using this and our assumption that
𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ∗)=ℝd\dom(\Psi^{*})={\mathbb{R}}^{d}, we get for all 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:
BΨ(𝒑∥∇Ψ∗(𝜽))=Ψ(𝒑)−⟨𝜽,𝒑⟩+⟨𝜽,∇Ψ∗(𝜽)⟩−Ψ(∇Ψ∗(𝜽)).B_{\Psi}({\bm{p}}\|\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}))=\Psi({\bm{p}})-{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{p}}\rangle}+{\langle\bm{\theta},\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta})\rangle}-\Psi(\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta})).
(150)
The last two terms are independent of 𝒑{\bm{p}} and therefore
𝒚^Ω(𝜽)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐩∈𝒞⟨𝜽,𝐩⟩−Ψ(𝐩)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝐩∈𝒞BΨ(𝐩∥∇Ψ∗(𝜽)),\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\argmax_{{\bm{p}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{p}}\rangle}-\Psi({\bm{p}})=\argmin_{{\bm{p}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}B_{\Psi}({\bm{p}}\|\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta})),
(151)
where 𝒞⊆𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ){\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\dom(\Psi).
The r.h.s. is the
Bregman projection of ∇Ψ∗​(𝜽)\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}) onto 𝒞{\mathcal{C}}.
Difference of Bregman divergences.
Let 𝒑=𝒚^Ω​(𝜽){\bm{p}}=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}).
Using (150), we obtain
BΨ(𝒚∥∇Ψ∗(𝜽))−BΨ(𝒑∥∇Ψ∗(𝜽))\displaystyle B_{\Psi}({\bm{y}}\|\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}))-B_{\Psi}({\bm{p}}\|\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}))
=Ψ⁡(𝒚)−⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩+⟨𝜽,𝒑⟩−Ψ⁡(𝒑)\displaystyle=\Psi({\bm{y}})-{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}+{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{p}}\rangle}-\Psi({\bm{p}})
(152)
=Ω⁡(𝒚)−⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩+Ω∗​(𝜽)\displaystyle=\Omega({\bm{y}})-{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}+\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})
(153)
=LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚),\displaystyle=L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}),
(154)
where we assumed 𝒚∈𝒞{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{C}} and 𝒞⊆𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ){\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\dom(\Psi),
implying Ψ⁡(𝒚)=Ω⁡(𝒚)\Psi({\bm{y}})=\Omega({\bm{y}}).
If 𝒞=𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ){\mathcal{C}}=\dom(\Psi) (i.e., Ω=Ψ\Omega=\Psi), then 𝒑=∇Ψ∗​(𝜽){\bm{p}}=\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}) and BΨ(𝒑∥∇Ψ∗(𝜽))=0B_{\Psi}({\bm{p}}\|\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}))=0. We thus get
the composite form of Fenchel-Young losses
BΩ(𝒚∥∇Ω∗(𝜽))=BΩ(𝒚∥𝒚^Ω(𝜽))=LΩ(𝜽;𝒚).B_{\Omega}({\bm{y}}\|\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}))=B_{\Omega}({\bm{y}}\|\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}))=L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}).
(155)
Bound.
Let 𝒑=𝒚^Ω​(𝜽){\bm{p}}=\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta}). Since 𝒑{\bm{p}} is the Bregman projection of ∇Ψ∗​(𝜽)\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}) onto 𝒞{\mathcal{C}},
we can use the well-known Pythagorean theorem for
Bregman divergences (see, e.g., Banerjee et al. (2005, Appendix A)) to
obtain for all 𝒚∈𝒞⊆𝖽𝗈𝗆(Ψ){\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\dom(\Psi):
BΨ(𝒚∥𝒑)+BΨ(𝒑∥∇Ψ∗(𝜽))≤BΨ(𝒚∥∇Ψ∗(𝜽)).B_{\Psi}({\bm{y}}\|{\bm{p}})+B_{\Psi}({\bm{p}}\|\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta}))\leq B_{\Psi}({\bm{y}}\|\nabla\Psi^{*}(\bm{\theta})).
(156)
Using (154), we obtain for all 𝒚∈𝒞{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{C}}:
0≤BΨ(𝒚∥𝒑)≤LΩ(𝜽;𝒚).0\leq B_{\Psi}({\bm{y}}\|{\bm{p}})\leq L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}}).
(157)
Since Ω\Omega is a l.s.c. proper convex
function, from Proposition 4, we immediately get
𝒑=𝒚⇔LΩ(𝜽;𝒚)=0⇔BΨ(𝒚∥𝒑)=0.{\bm{p}}={\bm{y}}\Leftrightarrow L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=0\Leftrightarrow B_{\Psi}({\bm{y}}\|{\bm{p}})=0.
(158)
B.3 Proof of Proposition 7
The two facts stated in Proposition 7 (𝖧\mathsf{H} is
always non-negative and maximized by the uniform distribution) follow directly
from Jensen’s inequality. Indeed, for all 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}:
•
𝖧⁡(𝒑)≥∑j=1dpj​𝖧​(𝒆j)=0\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})\geq\sum_{j=1}^{d}p_{j}\mathsf{H}(\bm{e}_{j})=0;
•
𝖧⁡(𝟏/d)=𝖧⁡(∑𝑷∈𝒫1d!​𝑷​𝒑)≥∑𝑷∈𝒫1d!​𝖧​(𝑷​𝒑)=𝖧⁡(𝒑)\mathsf{H}(\mathbf{1}/d)=\mathsf{H}\left(\sum_{\bm{P}\in\mathcal{P}}\frac{1}{d!}\bm{P}{\bm{p}}\right)\geq\sum_{\bm{P}\in\mathcal{P}}\frac{1}{d!}\mathsf{H}(\bm{P}{\bm{p}})=\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}}),
where 𝒫\mathcal{P} is the set of d×dd\times d permutation matrices.
Strict concavity ensures that 𝒑=𝟏/d{\bm{p}}=\bm{1}/d is the unique maximizer.
B.4 Proof of Proposition 9
We start by proving the following lemma.
Lemma 16.
Let 𝖧\mathsf{H} satisfy assumptions A.1–A.3.
Then:
1.
We have 𝜽∈∂(−𝖧)​(𝒆k)\bm{\theta}\in\partial(-\mathsf{H})(\bm{e}_{k}) iff θk=(−𝖧)∗​(𝜽)\theta_{k}=(-\mathsf{H})^{*}(\bm{\theta}). That is:
∂(−𝖧)(𝒆k)={𝜽∈ℝd:θk≥⟨𝜽,𝒑⟩+𝖧(𝒑),∀𝒑∈△d}.\partial(-\mathsf{H})(\bm{e}_{k})=\{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\colon\theta_{k}\geq{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{p}}\rangle}+\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}}),\,\,\forall{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}\}.
(159)
2.
If 𝜽∈∂(−𝖧)​(𝒆k)\bm{\theta}\in\partial(-\mathsf{H})(\bm{e}_{k}),
then, we also have 𝜽′∈∂(−𝖧)​(𝒆k)\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in\partial(-\mathsf{H})(\bm{e}_{k})
for any 𝜽′\bm{\theta}^{\prime} such that θk′=θk\theta_{k}^{\prime}=\theta_{k} and
θi′≤θi\theta_{i}^{\prime}\leq\theta_{i}, for all i≠ki\neq k.
Proof of the lemma:
Let Ω=−𝖧\Omega=-\mathsf{H}.
From Proposition 2 (order preservation), we can consider ∂Ω⁡(𝒆1)\partial\Omega(\bm{e}_{1}) without loss of generality, in which case any 𝜽∈∂Ω⁡(𝒆1)\bm{\theta}\in\partial\Omega(\bm{e}_{1}) satisfies θ1=maxj⁡θj\theta_{1}=\max_{j}\theta_{j}.
We have 𝜽∈∂Ω⁡(𝒆1)\bm{\theta}\in\partial\Omega(\bm{e}_{1}) iff
Ω⁡(𝒆1)=⟨𝜽,𝒆1⟩−Ω∗​(𝜽)=θ1−Ω∗​(𝜽)\Omega(\bm{e}_{1})={\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{e}_{1}\rangle}-\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})=\theta_{1}-\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}).
Since Ω⁡(𝒆1)=0\Omega(\bm{e}_{1})=0,
we must have θ1=Ω∗​(𝜽)≥sup𝒑∈△d⟨𝜽,𝒑⟩−Ω⁡(𝒑)\theta_{1}=\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})\geq\sup_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{p}}\rangle}-\Omega({\bm{p}}), which proves part 1.
To see 2, note that we have θk′=θk≥⟨𝜽,𝒑⟩−Ω⁡(𝒑)≥⟨𝜽′,𝒑⟩−Ω⁡(𝒑)\theta_{k}^{\prime}=\theta_{k}\geq{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{p}}\rangle}-\Omega({\bm{p}})\geq{\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime},{\bm{p}}\rangle}-\Omega({\bm{p}}), for all 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}, from which the result follows. ■\blacksquare
We now proceed to the proof of Proposition 9.
Let Ω=−𝖧\Omega=-\mathsf{H}, and suppose that LΩL_{\Omega} has the separation margin property. Then, 𝜽=m​𝒆1\bm{\theta}=m\bm{e}_{1} satisfies the margin condition θ1≥m+maxj≠1⁡θj\theta_{1}\geq m+\max_{j\neq 1}\theta_{j}, hence LΩ​(m​𝒆1,𝒆1)=0L_{\Omega}(m\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{1})=0.
From the first part of Proposition 4, this implies m​𝒆1∈∂Ω⁡(𝒆1)m\bm{e}_{1}\in\partial\Omega(\bm{e}_{1}).
Conversely,
let us assume that m​𝒆1∈∂Ω⁡(𝒆1)m\bm{e}_{1}\in\partial\Omega(\bm{e}_{1}).
From the second part of Lemma 16,
this implies that
𝜽∈∂Ω⁡(𝒆1)\bm{\theta}\in\partial\Omega(\bm{e}_{1}) for
any 𝜽\bm{\theta} such that θ1=m\theta_{1}=m and θi≤0\theta_{i}\leq 0 for all i≥2i\geq 2; and more generally we have 𝜽+c​𝟏∈∂Ω⁡(𝒆1)\bm{\theta}+c\mathbf{1}\in\partial\Omega(\bm{e}_{1}).
That is, any 𝜽\bm{\theta} with θ1≥m+maxi≠1⁡θi\theta_{1}\geq m+\max_{i\neq 1}\theta_{i} satisfies
𝜽∈∂Ω⁡(𝒆1)\bm{\theta}\in\partial\Omega(\bm{e}_{1}). From Proposition 4,
this is equivalent to LΩ​(𝜽,𝒆1)=0L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{1})=0.
Let us now determine the margin of LΩL_{\Omega}, i.e., the
smallest mm such that
m​𝒆1∈∂Ω⁡(𝒆1)m\bm{e}_{1}\in\partial\Omega(\bm{e}_{1}).
From Lemma 16,
this is equivalent to
m≥m​p1−Ω⁡(𝒑)m\geq mp_{1}-\Omega({\bm{p}}) for any 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}, i.e.,
−Ω⁡(𝒑)1−p1≤m\frac{-\Omega({\bm{p}})}{1-p_{1}}\leq m.
Note that by Proposition 2 the “most competitive” 𝒑{\bm{p}}’s are sorted as 𝒆1\bm{e}_{1}, so we may write p1=‖𝒑‖∞p_{1}=\|{\bm{p}}\|_{\infty} without loss of generality. The margin of LΩL_{\Omega} is the smallest possible such margin, given by (63).
B.5 Proof of Proposition 10
Let us start by showing that conditions 1 and 2 are equivalent.
To show that 2 ⇒\Rightarrow 1, take an arbitrary 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}. From Fenchel-Young duality and the Danskin’s theorem, we have that ∇(−𝖧)∗​(𝜽)=𝒑⇒𝜽∈∂(−𝖧)​(𝒑)\nabla(-\mathsf{H})^{*}(\bm{\theta})={\bm{p}}\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,\bm{\theta}\in\partial(-\mathsf{H})({\bm{p}}), which implies the subdifferential set is non-empty everywhere in the simplex. Let us now prove that 1 ⇒\Rightarrow 2. Let Ω=−𝖧\Omega=-{\mathsf{H}}, and assume that Ω\Omega has non-empty subdifferential everywhere in △d\triangle^{d}.
We need to show that for any 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}, there is some 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} such that
𝒑∈𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝗂𝗇𝐩′∈△dΩ​(𝐩′)−⟨𝜽,𝐩′⟩{\bm{p}}\in\argmin_{{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in\triangle^{d}}\Omega({\bm{p}}^{\prime})-{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\rangle}.
The Lagrangian associated with this minimization problem is:
ℒ⁡(𝒑,𝝁,λ)=Ω⁡(𝒑)−⟨𝜽+𝝁,𝒑⟩+λ⁡(𝟏⊤​𝒑−1).\mathcal{L}({\bm{p}},\bm{\mu},\lambda)=\Omega({\bm{p}})-{\langle\bm{\theta}+\bm{\mu},{\bm{p}}\rangle}+\lambda(\mathbf{1}^{\top}{\bm{p}}-1).
(160)
The KKT conditions are:
{0∈∂pℒ⁡(𝒑,𝝁,λ)=∂Ω⁡(𝒑)−𝜽−𝝁+λ​𝟏⟨𝒑,𝝁⟩=0𝒑∈△d,𝝁≥0.\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}0\in\partial_{p}\mathcal{L}({\bm{p}},\bm{\mu},\lambda)=\partial\Omega({\bm{p}})-\bm{\theta}-\bm{\mu}+\lambda\mathbf{1}\\
{\langle{\bm{p}},\bm{\mu}\rangle}=0\\
{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d},\,\,\bm{\mu}\geq 0.\end{array}\right.
For a given 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}, we seek 𝜽\bm{\theta} such that
(𝒑,𝝁,λ)({\bm{p}},\bm{\mu},\lambda) are a solution to the KKT conditions
for some 𝝁≥0\bm{\mu}\geq 0 and λ∈ℝ\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}.
We will show that such 𝜽\bm{\theta} exists by simply choosing
𝝁=𝟎\bm{\mu}=\mathbf{0} and λ=0\lambda=0.
Those choices are dual feasible and guarantee that the slackness complementary condition is satisfied.
In this case, we have from the first condition that
𝜽∈∂Ω⁡(𝒑)\bm{\theta}\in\partial\Omega({\bm{p}}).
From the assumption that Ω\Omega has non-empty subdifferential in all the simplex, we have that for any 𝒑∈△d{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d} we can find a
𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} such that (𝒑,𝜽)({\bm{p}},\bm{\theta}) are a dual pair, i.e., 𝒑=∇Ω∗​(𝜽){\bm{p}}=\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta}), which proves that ∇Ω∗​(ℝd)=△d\nabla\Omega^{*}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})=\triangle^{d}.
Next, we show that condition 1⇒31\Rightarrow 3. Since ∂(−𝖧)​(𝒑)≠∅\partial(-\mathsf{H})({\bm{p}})\neq\varnothing everywhere in the simplex, we can take an arbitrary 𝜽∈∂(−𝖧)​(𝒆k)\bm{\theta}\in\partial(-\mathsf{H})(\bm{e}_{k}). From Lemma 16, item 2, we have that 𝜽′∈∂(−𝖧)​(𝒆k)\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in\partial(-\mathsf{H})(\bm{e}_{k}) for θk′=θk\theta^{\prime}_{k}=\theta_{k} and θj′=minℓ⁡θℓ\theta^{\prime}_{j}=\min_{\ell}\theta_{\ell}; since (−𝖧)∗(-\mathsf{H})^{*} is shift invariant, we can without loss of generality have θ′=m​𝒆k\theta^{\prime}=m\bm{e}_{k} for some m>0m>0, which implies from Proposition 9 that LΩL_{\Omega} has a margin.
Let us show that, if −𝖧-\mathsf{H} is separable, then 3⇒13\Rightarrow 1, which establishes equivalence between all conditions 1, 2, and 3.
From Proposition 9, the existing of a separation margin implies that there is some mm such that m​𝒆k∈∂(−𝖧)​(𝒆k)m\bm{e}_{k}\in\partial(-\mathsf{H})(\bm{e}_{k}).
Let 𝖧⁡(𝒑)=∑i=1dh⁡(pi)\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}h(p_{i}), with h:[0,1]→ℝ+h:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+} concave. Due to assumption A.1, hh must satisfy h⁡(0)=h⁡(1)=0h(0)=h(1)=0.
Without loss of generality, suppose 𝒑=[𝒑~;𝟎k]{\bm{p}}=[\tilde{{\bm{p}}};\mathbf{0}_{k}], where 𝒑~∈𝗋𝖾𝗅𝗂𝗇𝗍(△d−k)\tilde{{\bm{p}}}\in\relint(\triangle^{d-k}) and 𝟎k\mathbf{0}_{k} is a vector with kk zeros.
We will see that there is a vector 𝒈∈ℝd\bm{g}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} such that
𝒈∈∂(−𝖧)​(𝒑)\bm{g}\in\partial(-\mathsf{H})({\bm{p}}), i.e., satisfying
−𝖧⁡(𝒑′)≥−𝖧⁡(𝒑)+⟨𝒈,𝒑′−𝒑⟩,∀𝒑′∈△d.-\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}}^{\prime})\geq-\mathsf{H}({\bm{p}})+\langle\bm{g},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}-{\bm{p}}\rangle,\quad\forall{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in\triangle^{d}.
(165)
Since 𝒑~∈𝗋𝖾𝗅𝗂𝗇𝗍(△d−k)\tilde{{\bm{p}}}\in\relint(\triangle^{d-k}), we have p~i∈]0,1[\tilde{p}_{i}\in]0,1[
for i∈{1,…,d−k}i\in\{1,\ldots,d-k\}, hence ∂(−h)​(p~i)\partial(-h)(\tilde{p}_{i}) must be
nonempty, since −h-h is convex and ]0,1[]0,1[ is an open set. We show that the following 𝒈=(g1,…,gd)∈ℝd\bm{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{d})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} is a subgradient of −𝖧-\mathsf{H} at 𝒑{\bm{p}}:
gi={∂(−h)​(p~i),i=1,…,d−km,i=d−k+1,…,d.g_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial(-h)(\tilde{p}_{i}),&\text{$i=1,\ldots,d-k$}\\
m,&\text{$i=d-k+1,\ldots,d$}.\end{array}\right.
By definition of subgradient, we have
−ψ⁡(pi′)≥−ψ⁡(p~i)+∂(−h)​(p~i)​(pi′−p~i),for i=1,…,d−k.-\psi(p_{i}^{\prime})\geq-\psi(\tilde{p}_{i})+\partial(-h)(\tilde{p}_{i})(p_{i}^{\prime}-\tilde{p}_{i}),\quad\text{for $i=1,\ldots,d-k$}.
(166)
Furthermore, since mm upper bounds the separation margin of 𝖧\mathsf{H}, we have from Proposition 9 that
m≥𝖧⁡([1−pi′,pi′,0,…,0])1−max⁡{1−pi′,pi′}=h⁡(1−pi′)+h⁡(pi′)min⁡{pi′,1−pi′}≥h⁡(pi′)pi′m\geq\frac{\mathsf{H}([1-p_{i}^{\prime},p_{i}^{\prime},0,\ldots,0])}{1-\max\{1-p_{i}^{\prime},p_{i}^{\prime}\}}=\frac{h(1-p_{i}^{\prime})+h(p_{i}^{\prime})}{\min\{p_{i}^{\prime},1-p_{i}^{\prime}\}}\geq\frac{h(p_{i}^{\prime})}{p_{i}^{\prime}} for any pi′∈]0,1]p_{i}^{\prime}\in]0,1]. Hence, we have
−ψ⁡(pi′)≥−ψ⁡(0)−m⁡(pi′−0),for i=d−k+1,…,d.-\psi(p_{i}^{\prime})\geq-\psi(0)-m(p_{i}^{\prime}-0),\quad\text{for $i=d-k+1,\ldots,d$}.
(167)
Summing all inequalities in Eqs. (166)–(167), we obtain the expression in Eq. (165), which finishes the proof.
B.6 Proof of Proposition 11
Define Ω=−𝖧\Omega=-\mathsf{H}.
Let us start by writing the margin expression (63) as a unidimensional optimization problem. This is done by noticing that the max-generalized entropy problem constrained to max⁡(𝒑)=1−t\max({\bm{p}})=1-t gives 𝒑=[1−t,td−1,…,td−1]{\bm{p}}=\left[1-t,\frac{t}{d-1},\ldots,\frac{t}{d-1}\right], for t∈[0,1−1d]t\in\left[0,1-\frac{1}{d}\right] by a similar argument as the one used in Proposition 7. margin⁡(LΩ)=supt∈[0,1−1d]−Ω⁡([1−t,td−1,…,td−1])t.\mathrm{margin}(L_{\Omega})=\sup_{t\in\left[0,1-\frac{1}{d}\right]}\frac{-\Omega\left(\left[1-t,\frac{t}{d-1},\ldots,\frac{t}{d-1}\right]\right)}{t}.
