B.7 Proof of Proposition 13
We start by proving the following lemma, which generalizes Lemma 16.
Lemma 17.
Let Ω\Omega be convex.
Then:
1.
We have
∂Ω(𝒚)={𝜽∈ℝd:⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩−Ω(𝒚)≥⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩−Ω(𝝁),∀𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)}\partial\Omega({\bm{y}})=\{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\colon{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}-\Omega({\bm{y}})\geq{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}-\Omega(\bm{\mu}),\,\,\forall\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}})\}.
2.
If 𝜽∈∂Ω⁡(𝒚)\bm{\theta}\in\partial\Omega({\bm{y}}),
then, we also have 𝜽′∈∂Ω⁡(𝒚)\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in\partial\Omega({\bm{y}})
for any 𝜽′\bm{\theta}^{\prime} such that ⟨𝜽′−𝜽,𝒚′⟩≤⟨𝜽′−𝜽,𝒚⟩{\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}\leq{\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}, for all 𝒚′∈𝒴{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}.
Proof of the lemma:
The first part comes directly from the definition of Fenchel conjugate.
To see the second part, note that, if 𝜽∈∂Ω⁡(𝒚)\bm{\theta}\in\partial\Omega({\bm{y}}), then
⟨𝜽′,𝒚⟩−Ω⁡(𝒚)=⟨𝜽′−𝜽,𝒚⟩+⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩−Ω⁡(𝒚)≥⟨𝜽′−𝜽,𝒚⟩+⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩−Ω⁡(𝝁){\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime},{\bm{y}}\rangle}-\Omega({\bm{y}})={\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}+{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}-\Omega({\bm{y}})\geq{\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}+{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}-\Omega(\bm{\mu}) for every 𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}}). Let 𝝁=∑𝒚′∈𝒴p⁡(𝒚′)​𝒚′\bm{\mu}=\sum_{{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}p({\bm{y}}^{\prime}){\bm{y}}^{\prime} for some distribution 𝒑∈△|𝒴|{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}.
Then, we have ⟨𝜽′−𝜽,𝒚⟩+⟨𝜽,𝝁⟩−Ω⁡(𝝁)=⟨𝜽′−𝜽,𝒚⟩+∑𝒚′∈𝒴p⁡(𝒚′)​⟨𝜽,𝒚′⟩−Ω⁡(𝝁)≥⟨𝜽′−𝜽,𝒚⟩+∑𝒚′∈𝒴p⁡(𝒚′)​(⟨𝜽′,𝒚′⟩−⟨𝜽′−𝜽,𝒚⟩)−Ω⁡(𝝁)=⟨𝜽′,𝝁⟩−Ω⁡(𝝁){\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}+{\langle\bm{\theta},\bm{\mu}\rangle}-\Omega(\bm{\mu})={\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}+\sum_{{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}p({\bm{y}}^{\prime}){\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}-\Omega(\bm{\mu})\geq{\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}+\sum_{{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}p({\bm{y}}^{\prime})({\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime},{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}-{\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle})-\Omega(\bm{\mu})={\langle\bm{\theta}^{\prime},\bm{\mu}\rangle}-\Omega(\bm{\mu}).
We now proceed to the proof of Proposition 13.
Suppose that LΩL_{\Omega} has the separation margin property, and let 𝜽=m​𝒚\bm{\theta}=m{\bm{y}}. Then, for any 𝒚′∈𝒴{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}, we have
⟨𝜽,𝒚′⟩+m2​‖𝒚−𝒚′‖2=m⁡⟨𝒚,𝒚′⟩+m2​‖𝒚−𝒚′‖2=m2|𝒚|+m2​‖y′‖2=m​r2=⟨m​𝒚,𝒚⟩=⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}+\frac{m}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\|^{2}=m{\langle{\bm{y}},{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}+\frac{m}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\|^{2}=\frac{m}{2}\|{\bm{y}}\|+\frac{m}{2}\|y^{\prime}\|^{2}=mr^{2}={\langle m{\bm{y}},{\bm{y}}\rangle}={\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}, which implies that LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=0L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}})=0. From the first part of Proposition 4, this implies m​𝒚∈∂Ω⁡(𝒚)m{\bm{y}}\in\partial\Omega({\bm{y}}).
Conversely,
let us assume that m​𝒚∈∂Ω⁡(𝒚)m{\bm{y}}\in\partial\Omega({\bm{y}}).
From Lemma 17,
this implies that
𝜽∈∂Ω⁡(𝒚)\bm{\theta}\in\partial\Omega({\bm{y}}) for
any 𝜽\bm{\theta} such that ⟨𝜽−m​𝒚,𝒚′⟩≤⟨𝜽−m​𝒚,𝒚⟩{\langle\bm{\theta}-m{\bm{y}},{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}\leq{\langle\bm{\theta}-m{\bm{y}},{\bm{y}}\rangle} for all 𝒚′∈𝒴{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}.
Therefore, we have ⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩−m​r2≥⟨𝜽,𝒚′⟩−m⁡⟨𝒚,𝒚′⟩{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}-mr^{2}\geq{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}-m{\langle{\bm{y}},{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}, that is, ⟨𝜽,𝒚⟩=⟨𝜽,𝒚′⟩+m2​‖𝒚−𝒚′‖2{\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}\rangle}={\langle\bm{\theta},{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\rangle}+\frac{m}{2}\|{\bm{y}}-{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\|^{2}. That is, any such 𝜽\bm{\theta} satisfies
𝜽∈∂Ω⁡(𝒚)\bm{\theta}\in\partial\Omega({\bm{y}}). From Proposition 4,
this is equivalent to LΩ​(𝜽,𝒚)=0L_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta};{\bm{y}})=0.
Let us now determine the margin of LΩL_{\Omega}, i.e., the
smallest mm such that
m​𝒚∈∂Ω⁡(𝒚)m{\bm{y}}\in\partial\Omega({\bm{y}}).
From Lemma 17,
this is equivalent to
m​r2−Ω⁡(𝒚)≥⟨m​𝒚,𝝁⟩−Ω⁡(𝝁)mr^{2}-\Omega({\bm{y}})\geq{\langle m{\bm{y}},\bm{\mu}\rangle}-\Omega(\bm{\mu}) for any 𝝁∈𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗏(𝒴)\bm{\mu}\in\conv({\mathcal{Y}}), i.e.,
−Ω⁡(𝝁)+Ω⁡(𝒚)r2−⟨𝒚,𝝁⟩≤m\frac{-\Omega(\bm{\mu})+\Omega({\bm{y}})}{r^{2}-{\langle{\bm{y}},\bm{\mu}\rangle}}\leq m, which leads to the expression (94).
B.8 Proof of Proposition 14
Let Ω⁡(𝒑)=∑j=1dg⁡(pj)+I△d​(𝒑)\Omega({\bm{p}})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}g(p_{j})+I_{\triangle^{d}}({\bm{p}}), where
g:[0,1]→ℝ+g\colon[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+} is a
non-negative, strictly convex, differentiable function.
Therefore, g′g^{\prime} is strictly monotonic on [0,1][0,1], thus invertible.
We show how computing ∇(Ω)∗\nabla(\Omega)^{*} reduces to finding the root of
a monotonic scalar function, for which efficient algorithms
are available.
From strict convexity and the definition of the convex conjugate,
∇(Ω)∗​(𝜽)=𝖺𝗋𝗀𝗆𝖺𝗑𝐩∈△d⟨𝐩,𝜽⟩−∑jg⁡(pj).\nabla(\Omega)^{*}(\bm{\theta})=\argmax_{{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d}}{\langle{\bm{p}},\bm{\theta}\rangle}-\sum_{j}g(p_{j}).
(173)
The constrained optimization problem above has Lagrangian
ℒ⁡(𝒑,𝝂,τ)≔∑j=1dg⁡(pj)−⟨𝜽+𝝂,𝒑⟩+τ⁡(𝟏⊤​𝒑−1).\mathcal{L}({\bm{p}},\bm{\nu},\tau)\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{d}g(p_{j})-{\langle\bm{\theta}+\bm{\nu},{\bm{p}}\rangle}+\tau(\mathbf{1}^{\top}{\bm{p}}-1).
(174)
A solution
(𝒑⋆,𝝂⋆,τ⋆)({\bm{p}}^{\star},\bm{\nu}^{\star},\tau^{\star})
must satisfy the KKT conditions
g′​(pj)−θj−νj+τ\displaystyle g^{\prime}(p_{j})-\theta_{j}-\nu_{j}+\tau
=0∀j∈[d]\displaystyle=0\qquad\forall j\in[d]
(175)
⟨𝒑,𝝂⟩\displaystyle{\langle{\bm{p}},\bm{\nu}\rangle}
=0\displaystyle=0
(176)
𝒑∈△d,𝝂\displaystyle{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d},\,\,\bm{\nu}
≥0.\displaystyle\geq 0.
(177)
Let us define
τmin≔max⁡(𝜽)−g′​(1)andτmax≔max⁡(𝜽)−g′​(1d).\tau_{\min}\coloneqq\max(\bm{\theta})-g^{\prime}(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{\max}\coloneqq\max(\bm{\theta})-g^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{d}\right).
Since gg is strictly convex, g′g^{\prime} is increasing and so
τmin<τmax\tau_{\min}<\tau_{\max}. For any τ∈[τmin,τmax]\tau\in[\tau_{\min},\tau_{\max}],
we construct 𝝂\bm{\nu} as
νj≔{0,θj−τ≥g′​(0)g′​(0)−θj+τ,θj−τ<g′​(0)\nu_{j}\coloneqq\begin{cases}0,&\theta_{j}-\tau\geq g^{\prime}(0)\\
g^{\prime}(0)-\theta_{j}+\tau,&\theta_{j}-\tau<g^{\prime}(0)\\
\end{cases}
By construction, νj≥0\nu_{j}\geq 0, satisfying dual feasibility.
Injecting ν\nu into (175)
and combining the two cases, we obtain
g′​(pj)=max⁡{θj−τ,g′​(0)}.g^{\prime}(p_{j})=\max\{\theta_{j}-\tau,~g^{\prime}(0)\}.
(178)
We show that i) the stationarity conditions have a unique solution given
τ\tau, and ii) [τmin,τmax][\tau_{\min},\tau_{\max}] forms a
sign-changing bracketing interval, and thus contains τ⋆\tau^{\star}, which can
then be found by one-dimensional search. The solution verifies all KKT conditions,
thus is globally optimal.
Solving the stationarity conditions.
Since gg is strictly convex, its derivative g′g^{\prime} is continuous and strictly
increasing, and is thus a one-to-one mapping between [0,1][0,1] and
[g′​(0),g′​(1)][g^{\prime}(0),g^{\prime}(1)]. Denote by (g′)−1:[g′​(0),g′​(1)]→[0,1](g^{\prime})^{-1}\colon[g^{\prime}(0),g^{\prime}(1)]\rightarrow[0,1]
its inverse. If θj−τ≥g′​(0)\theta_{j}-\tau\geq g^{\prime}(0),
we have
g′​(0)≤g′​(pj)=θj−τ\displaystyle g^{\prime}(0)\leq g^{\prime}(p_{j})=\theta_{j}-\tau
≤max⁡(𝜽)−τmin\displaystyle\leq\max(\bm{\theta})-\tau_{\min}
(179)
=max⁡(𝜽)−max⁡(𝜽)+g′​(1)\displaystyle=\max(\bm{\theta})-\max(\bm{\theta})+g^{\prime}(1)
=g′​(1).\displaystyle=g^{\prime}(1).
Otherwise,
g′​(pj)=g′​(0)g^{\prime}(p_{j})=g^{\prime}(0). This verifies that the r.h.s. of (178)
is always within the domain of (g′)−1(g^{\prime})^{-1}. We can thus apply the inverse to both
sides to solve for pjp_{j}, obtaining
pj​(τ)=(g′)−1​(max⁡{θj−τ,g′​(0)}).p_{j}(\tau)=(g^{\prime})^{-1}(\max\{\theta_{j}-\tau,~g^{\prime}(0)\}).
(180)
Strict convexity implies the optimal 𝒑⋆{\bm{p}}^{\star} is unique; it can be seen
that τ⋆\tau^{\star} is also unique. Indeed, assume optimal τ1⋆,τ2⋆\tau^{\star}_{1},\tau^{\star}_{2}. Then, 𝒑⁡(τ1⋆)=𝒑⁡(τ2⋆){\bm{p}}(\tau^{\star}_{1})={\bm{p}}(\tau^{\star}_{2}), so
max⁡(𝜽−τ1⋆,g′​(0))=max⁡(𝜽−τ2⋆,g′​(0))\max(\bm{\theta}-\tau^{\star}_{1},g^{\prime}(0))=\max(\bm{\theta}-\tau^{\star}_{2},g^{\prime}(0)).
This implies either τ1⋆=τ2⋆\tau^{\star}_{1}=\tau^{\star}_{2}, or 𝜽−τ{1,2}⋆≤g′​(0)\bm{\theta}-\tau^{\star}_{\{1,2\}}\leq g^{\prime}(0), in which case 𝒑=𝟎∉△d{\bm{p}}=\bm{0}\notin\triangle^{d},
which is a contradiction.
Validating the bracketing interval.
Consider the primal infeasibility function
ϕ⁡(τ)≔⟨𝒑⁡(τ),𝟏⟩−1\phi(\tau)\coloneqq{\langle{\bm{p}}(\tau),\bm{1}\rangle}-1;
𝒑⁡(τ){\bm{p}}(\tau) is primal feasible iff ϕ⁡(τ)=0.\phi(\tau)=0.
We show that ϕ\phi is decreasing on [τmin,τmax][\tau_{\min},\tau_{\max}], and that
it has opposite signs at the two extremities.
From the intermediate value theorem, the unique
root τ⋆\tau^{\star} must satisfy τ⋆∈[τmin,τmax]\tau^{\star}\in[\tau_{\min},\tau_{\max}].
Since g′g^{\prime} is increasing, so is (g′)−1(g^{\prime})^{-1}.
Therefore, for all jj, pj​(τ)p_{j}(\tau) is decreasing, and so is the
sum ϕ⁡(τ)=∑jpj​(τ)−1\phi(\tau)=\sum_{j}p_{j}(\tau)-1. It remains to check the signs
at the boundaries.
∑ipi​(τmax)\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}(\tau_{\max})
=∑i(g′)−1​(max⁡{θi−max⁡(𝜽)+g′​(1/d),g′​(0)})\displaystyle=\sum_{i}(g^{\prime})^{-1}(\max\{\theta_{i}-\max(\bm{\theta})+g^{\prime}\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}\right),~g^{\prime}(0)\})
(181)
≤d​(g′)−1​(max⁡{g′​(1/d),g′​(0)})\displaystyle\leq d~(g^{\prime})^{-1}(\max\{g^{\prime}\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}\right),~g^{\prime}(0)\})
=d​(g′)−1​(g′​(1/d))=1,\displaystyle=d~(g^{\prime})^{-1}\left(g^{\prime}\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}\right)\right)=1,
where we upper-bounded each term of the sum by the largest one. At the other end,
∑ipi​(τmin)\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}(\tau_{\min})
=∑i(g′)−1​(max⁡{θi−max⁡(𝜽)+g′​(1),g′​(0)})\displaystyle=\sum_{i}(g^{\prime})^{-1}(\max\{\theta_{i}-\max(\bm{\theta})+g^{\prime}(1),~g^{\prime}(0)\})
(182)
≥(g′)−1​(max⁡{g′​(1),g′​(0)})\displaystyle\geq(g^{\prime})^{-1}(\max\{g^{\prime}(1),~g^{\prime}(0)\})
=(g′)−1​(g′​(1))=1,\displaystyle=(g^{\prime})^{-1}(g^{\prime}(1))=1,
using that a sum of non-negative terms is no less than its largest term.
Therefore, ϕ⁡(τmin)≥0\phi(\tau_{\min})\geq 0 and ϕ⁡(τmax)≤0\phi(\tau_{\max})\leq 0. This
implies that there must exist τ⋆\tau^{\star} in [τmin,τmax][\tau_{\min},\tau_{\max}]
satisfying ϕ⁡(τ⋆)=0\phi(\tau^{\star})=0.
The corresponding triplet (𝒑⁡(τ⋆),𝝂⁡(τ⋆),τ⋆)\left({\bm{p}}(\tau^{\star}),\bm{\nu}(\tau^{\star}),\tau^{\star}\right) thus satisfies all of the KKT conditions, confirming that
it is the global solution.
Algorithm 1 is an example of a bisection algorithm for finding
an approximate solution; more advanced root finding methods can also be used. We
note that the resulting algorithm resembles the method provided
in Krichene et al. (2015), with a non-trivial difference being the order of the
thresholding and (−g)−1(-g)^{-1} in Eq. (180).
Algorithm 1 Bisection for 𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)=∇Ω∗​(𝜽)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})=\nabla\Omega^{*}(\bm{\theta})
Input: 𝜽∈ℝd\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},
Ω⁡(𝒑)=I△d+∑ig⁡(pi)\Omega({\bm{p}})=I_{\triangle^{d}}+\sum_{i}g(p_{i})
𝒑⁡(τ)≔(g′)−1​(max⁡{𝜽−τ,g′​(0)}){\bm{p}}(\tau)\coloneqq(g^{\prime})^{-1}(\max\{\bm{\theta}-\tau,g^{\prime}(0)\})
ϕ⁡(τ)≔⟨𝒑⁡(τ),𝟏⟩−1\phi(\tau)\coloneqq{\langle{\bm{p}}(\tau),\bm{1}\rangle}-1
τmin←max⁡(𝜽)−g′​(1)\tau_{\min}\leftarrow\max(\bm{\theta})-g^{\prime}(1);
τmax←max⁡(𝜽)−g′​(1/d)\tau_{\max}\leftarrow\max(\bm{\theta})-g^{\prime}\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}\right)
τ←(τmin+τmax)/2\tau\leftarrow(\tau_{\min}+\tau_{\max})/2
while |ϕ⁡(τ)|>ϵ|\phi(\tau)|>\epsilon
if ϕ⁡(τ)<0\phi(\tau)<0 τmax←τ\tau_{\max}\leftarrow\tau
else τmin←τ\tau_{\min}\leftarrow\tau
τ←(τmin+τmax)/2\tau\leftarrow(\tau_{\min}+\tau_{\max})/2
Output: ∇𝒚^Ω​(𝜽)≈𝒑⁡(τ)\nabla\widehat{{\bm{y}}}_{\Omega}(\bm{\theta})\approx{\bm{p}}(\tau)
B.9 Proof of Proposition 15
We first need the following lemma.
Lemma 18.
Let ℓ⁡(𝛉,𝐞k)\ell(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k}) be defined as
ℓ⁡(𝜽,𝒆k)≔{∑jck,j​ϕ​(θj) if ​𝜽⊤​𝟏=0∞ o.w. ,\ell(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\sum_{j}c_{k,j}\phi(\theta_{j})&\mbox{ if }\bm{\theta}^{\top}\bm{1}=0\\
\infty&\mbox{ o.w. }\end{cases},
(183)
where ϕ:ℝ→ℝ+\phi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+} is convex.
Then, 𝖧ℓ\mathsf{H}_{\ell} defined in (138) equals
−𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)=min⁡∑jτ∈ℝ⁡(𝒑⊤​𝒄j)​ϕ∗​(−τ𝒑⊤​𝒄j).-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})=\min_{\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}}\sum_{j}({\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j})\phi^{*}\left(\frac{-\tau}{{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j}}\right).
(184)
Proof.
We want to solve
𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)=min⁡∑j𝜽⊤​𝟏=0⁡pj​ℓ​(𝜽,𝒆j)=min⁡∑j𝜽⊤​𝟏=0⁡pj​∑icj,i​ϕ​(θi)=min⁡∑i𝜽⊤​𝟏=0⁡(𝒑⊤​𝒄i)​ϕ​(θi),\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})=\min_{\bm{\theta}^{\top}\bm{1}=0}\sum_{j}p_{j}\ell(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{j})=\min_{\bm{\theta}^{\top}\bm{1}=0}\sum_{j}p_{j}\sum_{i}c_{j,i}\phi(\theta_{i})=\min_{\bm{\theta}^{\top}\bm{1}=0}\sum_{i}({\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{i})\phi(\theta_{i}),
(185)
where 𝒄i\bm{c}_{i} is a vector gathering cj,ic_{j,i} for all jj.
Introducing a Lagrange multiplier we get
𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)=min𝜽∈ℝ|𝒴|⁡max⁡∑iτ∈ℝ⁡(𝒑⊤​𝒄i)​ϕ​(θi)+τ​∑iθi.\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})=\min_{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}}\max_{\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}}\sum_{i}({\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{i})\phi(\theta_{i})+\tau\sum_{i}\theta_{i}.
(186)
Strong duality holds and we can swap the order of the min and max.
After routine calculations, we obtain
−𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)=min⁡∑iτ∈ℝ⁡(𝒑⊤​𝒄i)​ϕ∗​(−τ𝒑⊤​𝒄i).-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})=\min_{\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}}\sum_{i}({\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{i})\phi^{*}\left(\frac{-\tau}{{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{i}}\right).
(187)
∎
We now prove Proposition 15. First, we rewrite
ℓ⁡(𝜽,𝒆k)=∑j≠kϕ⁡(θj−θk)\ell(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})=\sum_{j\neq k}\phi(\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}) as ℓ⁡(𝜽,𝒆k)=∑jck,j​ϕ​(θj−θk)\ell(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})=\sum_{j}c_{k,j}\phi(\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}), where we choose ck,j=0c_{k,j}=0 if k=jk=j, 11
otherwise, leading to 𝒑⊤​𝒄j=1−pj{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j}=1-p_{j}. Because ϕ⁡(θj−θk)\phi(\theta_{j}-\theta_{k})
is shift invariant w.r.t. 𝜽\bm{\theta}, without loss of generality, we can further rewrite
the loss as ℓ⁡(𝜽,𝒆k)=∑jck,j​ϕ​(θj)\ell(\bm{\theta};\bm{e}_{k})=\sum_{j}c_{k,j}\phi(\theta_{j}) with 𝖽𝗈𝗆(ℓ)={𝜽∈ℝ|𝒴|:𝜽⊤​𝟏=0}\dom(\ell)=\{\bm{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{Y}}|}\colon\bm{\theta}^{\top}\bm{1}=0\}. Hence, Lemma
184 applies. We now derive closed form for
specific choices of ϕ\phi.
Hinge loss.
When ϕ⁡(t)=[1+t]+\phi(t)=[1+t]_{+}, the conjugate is
ϕ∗​(u)={−u if ​u∈[0,1]∞ o.w. .\phi^{*}(u)=\begin{cases}-u&\mbox{ if }u\in[0,1]\\
\infty&\mbox{ o.w. }\end{cases}.
(188)
The constraint set for τ\tau is therefore 𝒞≔⋂j∈[d][−𝒑⊤​𝒄j,0]=[−minj∈[d]⁡𝒑⊤​𝒄j,0]{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq\bigcap_{j\in[d]}\left[-{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j},0\right]=\left[-\displaystyle{\min_{j\in[d]}}~{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j},0\right].
Hence
−𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)=minτ∈𝒞⁡d​τ=−d​minj∈[d]​𝒑⊤​𝒄j.-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})=\min_{\tau\in{\mathcal{C}}}d\tau=-d\min_{j\in[d]}{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j}.
(189)
This recovers Duchi et al. (2018, Example 5, §A.6) with a simpler proof. We
next turn to the following new results.
Smoothed hinge loss.
We add quadratic regularization to the conjugate (Shalev-Shwartz and Zhang, 2016):
ϕ∗​(u)={−u+12​u2 if ​u∈[0,1]∞ o.w. .\phi^{*}(u)=\begin{cases}-u+\frac{1}{2}u^{2}&\mbox{ if }u\in[0,1]\\
\infty&\mbox{ o.w. }\end{cases}.
(190)
Going back to ϕ\phi, we obtain:
ϕ⁡(t)={0 if ​t≤−1t+12 if ​t≥012​(1+t)2 o.w. .\phi(t)=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }t\leq-1\\
t+\frac{1}{2}&\mbox{ if }t\geq 0\\
\frac{1}{2}(1+t)^{2}&\mbox{ o.w. }\end{cases}.
(191)
The constraint set for τ\tau is the same as before,
𝒞=[−minj∈[d]⁡𝒑⊤​𝒄j,0]{\mathcal{C}}=\left[-\displaystyle{\min_{j\in[d]}}~{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j},0\right].
Plugging ϕ∗\phi^{*} into (187), we obtain
−𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)=minτ∈𝒞⁡τ22​∑j=1d1𝒑⊤​𝒄j+d​τ.-\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})=\min_{\tau\in{\mathcal{C}}}\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j}}+d~\tau.
(192)
Since the problem is unidimensional, let us solve for τ\tau unconstrained
first:
τ=−d/(∑j=1d1/(𝒑⊤𝒄j)).\tau=-d\Big/(\sum_{j=1}^{d}1/({\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j})).
(193)
We notice that τ≤−minj⁡𝒑⊤​𝒄j\tau\leq-\min_{j}{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j} for 𝒄j≥𝟎\bm{c}_{j}\geq\bm{0} since
∑jmini⁡𝒑⊤​𝒄i𝒑⊤​𝒄j∈[0,d]\sum_{j}\frac{\min_{i}{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{i}}{{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j}}\in[0,d].
This expression of τ\tau is not feasible.
Hence the optimal
solution is at the boundary and τ⋆=−minj⁡𝒑⊤​𝒄j\tau^{\star}=-\min_{j}{\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j}.
Plugging that expression back into (192) gives the
claimed expression of 𝖧ℓ\mathsf{H}_{\ell}.
Squared hinge loss.
When ϕ⁡(t)=12​[1+t]+2\phi(t)=\frac{1}{2}[1+t]^{2}_{+}, the conjugate is
ϕ∗​(u)={−u+12​u2 if ​u≥0∞ o.w. .\phi^{*}(u)=\begin{cases}-u+\frac{1}{2}u^{2}&\mbox{ if }u\geq 0\\
\infty&\mbox{ o.w. }\end{cases}.
(194)
The constraint is now τ≤0\tau\leq 0. Hence, the optimal solution of the
problem w.r.t. τ\tau in (187) is now
(193) for all 𝒑∈△d,𝒄j≥𝟎{\bm{p}}\in\triangle^{d},\bm{c}_{j}\geq\bm{0}.
Simplifying, we get
𝖧ℓ​(𝒑)=12​d2∑j=1d1/(𝒑⊤​𝒄j).\mathsf{H}_{\ell}({\bm{p}})=\frac{\frac{1}{2}d^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{d}1/({\bm{p}}^{\top}\bm{c}_{j})}.
(195)

Instructions for reporting errors
We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
methods listed below:
Click the "Report Issue" (
) button, located in the page header.
Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.
Our team has already identified the following issues. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
all.
Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion, and welcome developer contributions.
We gratefully acknowledge support from our major funders,
member institutions, ,
and all contributors.
